L55: ## I. Executive Summary
L56: ## 1. Overview
L57: ## 1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L58: On August 29, 2025, Genzyme Corporation (Applicant) submitted a new drug application (NDA) for rilzabrutinib for the proposed indication of the treatment of adult patients with persistent or chronic immune thrombocytopenia (ITP) who have had an insufficient response (b)(4) to a previous treatment.
L59: Rilzabrutinib was granted Orphan Drug Designation (ODD) on October 15, 2018, for the treatment of ITP, and Fast Track designation for rilzabrutinib for the treatment of ITP on October 15, 2020.
L60: The Applicant submitted results from one Phase 3 Study (LUNA 3) to establish the superiority of rilzabrutinib compared to placebo in adults with chronic or persistent primary ITP. LUNA 3 included a 24-week randomized, double blind treatment period. An initial assessment was performed after the first 12 weeks of blinded treatment (Week 13). Participants who had a platelet response during the first 12 weeks continued blinded treatment for a total of 24 weeks. The primary efficacy endpoint was durable platelet count, in the absence of rescue medication, assessed between Weeks 12 and 24. Participants who discontinued blinded treatment at Week 13 were considered nonresponders. Analysis of efficacy data for adults enrolled to LUNA 3 found a higher percentage of participants in the rilzabrutinib arm achieved a durable platelet response (23% [95% confidence interval (CI), 16.12% to 30.49%]) compared to no participants in the placebo arm. Rilzabrutinib also demonstrated superiority for the first four key secondary endpoints, which included assessments of number of weeks with platelet response, time to first response, and proportion of participants not requiring rescue therapy during the 24-week blinded treatment.
L61: These findings of efficacy are supported by platelet response data from Study PRN1008-010 (LUNA 2), a two-part dose finding with cohort expansion study of adults with primary or secondary ITP who had relapsed or had an insufficient response to prior therapies. The platelet response rate in the dose finding portion of the study was 40%. The durable platelet response rate in the cohort expansion part was 35% (95% CI: 17, 56).
L62: Assessment of risk was based primarily on data from the 202 participants (133 in the rilzabrutinib arm and 69 in the placebo arm) who received blinded treatment for up to 24 weeks on LUNA 3.
L63: NDA 219685 was reviewed by the multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends approval of rilzabrutinib for the treatment of adult patients with persistent or chronic immune thrombocytopenia who have had an insufficient response to a previous treatment, and I, the signatory authority for this application, concur with those recommendations. The overall benefit-risk is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework below. For detailed information
L64: supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed reviews included in this integrated review.
L65: ## 1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L66: Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
L67: Chronic ITP is a rare, serious, and potentially life-threatening disease, and it is reasonable in this situation to establish effectiveness based on one adequate and well-controlled study plus confirmatory evidence. The one adequate and well controlled trial was Study PRN1008-018 (LUNA 3), a multicenter, randomized, placebo-controlled study conducted in 202 adults with chronic ITP. The results for the primary endpoint of durable platelet count provided compelling evidence of superiority of rilzabrutinib over placebo. These findings were supported by the first four key secondary endpoints which evaluated number of weeks with platelet responses, time to first platelet response, and use of rescue medication. For these secondary endpoints, rilzabrutinib also demonstrated superiority over placebo.
L68: The Applicant also conducted Study PRN1008-010 (LUNA 2), an open-label dose-finding with cohort expansion study in patients with ITP who have relapsed or have an insufficient response to prior therapies, which provides confirmatory evidence of effectiveness for rilzabrutinib based on the observed pharmacodynamic effects. Spontaneous sustained increases in platelet count in patients with chronic ITP would not be expected. Therefore, it is reasonable to consider the durable responses observed in this single-arm study as evidence of activity related to rilzabrutinib and confirmatory evidence supporting the findings from LUNA 3.
L69: Together the data from LUNA 3 with confirmatory evidence from Study LUNA 2 establish substantial evidence of effectiveness of rilzabrutinib in adults with chronic ITP.
L70: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L71: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L72: **Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework**
L73: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Immune thrombocytopenia (ITP), also known as immune or idiopathic thrombocytopenic purpura, is an autoimmune disorder characterized by immunologic destruction of platelets and decreased platelet production.
The resulting thrombocytopenia predisposes to bleeding, bruising or petechiae.
Bleeding may be fatal or life threatening
Acute ITP often resolved spontaneously within 3 months.
Persistent ITP is defined as a condition lasting between 3 and 12 months after diagnosis, while chronic ITP is defined as lasting longer than 12 months.
The estimated incidence of ITP among adults in the United States is 3.3/100,000 per year.
ITP is an uncommon autoimmune disorder that can cause bleeding, which may be fatal or life threatening.
Current treatment options	The goal of treatment in ITP is to increase and sustain platelet counts to a level that limits bleeding episodes and allows the patient to continue or resume normal activities of daily living. The platelet count need not be a normal platelet count to achieve these goals. The following are currently available ITP treatments:Decrease the rate of platelet destruction:Corticosteroids
Immunoglobulins
Anti-D immunoglobulin
Splenectomy
Fostamatinib (Tavalisse) (oral administration) FDA approved

Reduce B-cell antibody productionCorticosteroids
Rituximab (anti-CD20) (intravenous administration)

Increase platelet productionRomiplostim (Nplate) (intravenous administration) FDA approved
Eltrombopag (Promacta) (oral administration) FDA approved


There is no cure for ITP. Although there are other treatment options for patients with ITP, there remain patients for whom currently available therapies are not effective or do not provide sustained benefit.
L74: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	Evidence of benefit is derived primarily from LUNA 3, a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in 202 adults with ITP who had had an unsustained response to treatment with intravenous immunoglobulins (IVIG), anti-D therapy, or corticosteroids. Participants were enrolled and randomized 2:1 to receive rilzabrutinib 400 mg orally twice daily or placebo equivalent. Randomization was stratified by splenectomy status and severity of thrombocytopenia. The primary assessment was based on a 24-week blinded period. An initial assessment was performed during after the first 12-weeks of blinded treatment. Participants who had a platelet response during the first 12 weeks continued blinded treatment for a total of 24 weeks.
The participants were generally heavily pretreated with 71% of all participants having received ≥3 prior unique ITP treatments and 46% having received at least 5 prior unique ITP treatments.
After 24 weeks of treatment, rilzabrutinib was superior to placebo on:Proportion of participants achieving a durable platelet response1, with 23.3% in the rilzabrutinib arm ([95% CI, 16.12% to 30.49%]) compared to no responses in the placebo arm, resulting in a risk difference of 23.1% [95%CI, 15.95, 30.31], p-value<0.0001.
Number of weeks with platelet response2, with an LS Mean3 of 7.18 (SE: 0.747) weeks in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to 0.72 (SE: 0.350) weeks in the placebo arm, yielding an LS Mean difference versus placebo of 6.46 (95% CI: 4.923, 7.990), p<0.0001.
Number of weeks with platelet counts ≥30,000/µL and at least doubled from baseline over the 24-week blinded treatment period in the absence of rescue therapy with an LS Mean of 6.95 (SE: 0.749) weeks in the rilzabrutinib arm compared with 0.64 (SE: 0.337) weeks in the placebo arm, yielding an LS mean difference versus placebo of 6.31 (95% CI: 4.787, 7.831), p<0.0001.
Time to first platelet count of ≥50,000/µL OR between ≥30,000/µL and <50,000/µL and doubled from baseline with median of 36 days (95% CI: 22, 44) in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to NA in the placebo arm (HR 3.1 [95% CI: 1.945, 4.934], p<0.0001).
Proportion of participants requiring rescue therapy during the 24-week blinded treatment period, with 33.1% in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to 58.0% in the placebo arm requiring rescue therapy, with a median time to first use of rescue not reached in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to 56 days in the placebo arm.

The benefit of rilzabrutinib for the treatment of adult patients with ITP with insufficient response to previous treatment was demonstrated by an increased and sustained platelet counts in Study PRN1008-018.
The submitted evidence shows durable platelet response in approximately 23% of participants who received rilzabrutinib compared to 0 participants in the placebo arm.
The majority of enrolled population was heavily pretreated having received at least 3 prior types of ITP treatments.
An increase and sustained platelet response to a level that limits bleeding episodes and allows the patient to pursue normal activities of daily living is a clinical benefit increase.
Fewer participants who received rilzabrutinib required rescue therapy during the first 24 weeks of treatment compared to participants who received placebo.
L75: ## NDA 219685
## WAYRILZ (rilzabrutinib)
L76: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	The safety profile of rilzabrutinib in ITP was established primarily based on experience in 202 adults (133 who received rilzabrutinib at the approved dosage and 69 who received placebo) during the randomized, double-blind period of LUNA 3.
The mean duration of exposure for this primary safety population was 17.4 weeks for rilzabrutinib versus 13.5 weeks for placebo. Of participants treated with rilzabrutinib, 37% remained on treatment at 24 weeks compared to only 13% of those who received placebo.
Key safety findings were:There was one death in the pooled integrated safety population occurring during the double-blind (DB) period of LUNA 3 in a participant receiving rilzabrutinib. The fatal TEAE was pulmonary aspergillosis and was assessed by the review team as related to rilzabrutinib.
During the DB period, the frequency of SAEs was similar between arms. 9% of participants in the rilzabrutinib arm had at least one SAE. After review of narratives, only 4 participants (3%) were assessed as having had a serious adverse reaction (SAR). These SARs were all related to infection.
Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation occurred in 6% of participants in the rilzabrutinib arm and no participant in the placebo arm. These included pneumonia (fatal), neutropenia, peripheral embolism, erythema nodosum, and multijoint pain, each occurring in one participant. Multiple TEAEs occurred in three additional patients leading to discontinuation of rilzabrutinib. They were diarrhea, nausea, abdominal discomfort; esophageal varices hemorrhage hepatic enzyme increased; and heartburn, headache, and leg pain.
Important adverse reactions of special interest and clinical interest occurring in participants while receiving rilzabrutinib on clinical trials included: infections, and neutropenia.
The most common adverse reaction, occurring in at least 5% of participants in the rilzabrutinib arm and at least 3% more than in the placebo arm are: diarrhea (32%), nausea (20%), headache (18%), COVID-19 (14%), arthralgia (9%), dizziness (8%), abdominal pain (8%), nasopharyngitis (7%), vomiting (7%), abdominal pain upper (5%), dyspepsia (5%), and cough (5%).

Evaluation of an integrated safety population comprising 276 participants with ITP who received at least one dose of rilzabrutinib at 400 mg orally twice daily in clinical trials LUNA 3 and PRN1008-010 Parts A and B did not identify a new or more serious safety signal than identified in DB period of LUNA 3.
The safety data submitted were sufficient to characterize the toxicity profile of rilzabrutinib.
Studies showed the safety profile was acceptable in the studied population.
The observed safety profile was generally consistent with other approved BTKIs and possibly less toxic.
Warnings and precautions in the USPI will include increased risk of infections as well as BTKi class labeling for hepatotoxicity(b)(4). Although these events were not observed in the pooled population of ITP patients treated with rilzabrutinib on clinical trials, the size of the population and extent of exposure is small.
Adverse reactions seen in the clinical trials were generally manageable with monitoring, dose interruption and appropriate clinical management.
L77: 1Durable platelet response was defined as platelet counts \ge 50,000/\mu\text{L} for \ge two-thirds of at least 8 nonmissing weekly scheduled platelet measurements during the last 12 weeks of the 24-week blinded treatment period in the absence of rescue therapy, provided that at least 2 nonmissing weekly scheduled platelet measurements were at or above 50,000/\mu\text{L} during the last 6 weeks of the 24-week blinded treatment period.

Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; BTKi; Bruton's tyrosine kinase inhibitor; CI, confidence interval; COVID-19, coronavirus disease of 2019; HR, hazard ratio; LS, least squares; PT, preferred term; SE, standard error; SOC; system organ class; TEAE, treatment-emergent adverse event; USPI, United States Prescribing Information
L81: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L82: Chronic immune thrombocytopenia is an uncommon acquired autoimmune disorder characterized by low platelet count that can result in a significant risk of bleeding. Goals of treatment are to prevent bleeding and maintain platelet count above a safe level to reduce the risk of bleeding. There are approved therapies for the treatment of ITP, but there remain patients for whom currently available therapies are not effective or do not provide sustained benefit.
L83: Overall, the benefit-risk analysis of rilzabrutinib for the treatment of chronic or persistent ITP is favorable. The LUNA 3 study was an adequate and well-controlled trial that demonstrated rilzabrutinib as an effective treatment for chronic and persistent ITP as demonstrated by durable platelet count and reduction in use of rescue medications. The risks associated with rilzabrutinib based on LUNA 3 were manageable and can be adequately described in labeling.
L84: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L85: ## 3. Introduction
L86: Rilzabrutinib (proposed proprietary name, WAYRILZ), is a new molecular entity and small-molecule, selective, covalent, reversible kinase inhibitor targeting Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK). Rilzabrutinib mediates its therapeutic effect in ITP through immune modulation including inhibition of B cell activation, and interruption of antibody-coated cell phagocytosis by fragment crystallizable gamma receptor (FcγR) in spleen and liver.
L87: The proposed indication is for treatment of adults with persistent or chronic ITP who have had an insufficient response to a previous treatment. The proposed dosing regimen for rilzabrutinib is 400 mg orally twice daily.
L88: ITP is an autoimmune disorder characterized by immunologic destruction of platelets resulting in thrombocytopenia with a predisposition to bleeding, bruising, and petechiae. The estimated incidence of ITP among adults in the United States is 3.3/100,000 per year. The decision to start treatment is determined by severity of bleeding, bleeding risk, activity level, and patient preference. Treatments of chronic ITP including agents intended to decrease the rate of platelet destruction e.g., corticosteroids, intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG), fostamatinib, and splenectomy, reduce the production of B-cell antibodies, including antiplatelet antibodies e.g., corticosteroids, rituximab, or increase platelet production e.g., thrombopoietin-receptor agonists (TPO-RAs).
L89: The single study submitted to support approval of rilzabrutinib in adults with chronic or persistent ITP, LUNA 3, was a multicenter, randomized, placebo-controlled trial designed to demonstrate superiority of rilzabrutinib over placebo on durable platelet response.
L90: This review serves as the interdisciplinary assessment for the original NDA for rilzabrutinib in chronic and persistent ITP and includes assessments from nonclinical related to safety and toxicity in nonclinical studies, clinical pharmacology related to safety and dosing based on clinical studies, statistics related to efficacy, and clinical related to efficacy and safety.
L91: ## 3.1. Review Issue List
L92: ## 3.1.1. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L93: ## 3.1.1.1. High Dropout Prior to Completion of the 24-Week Double-Blind Treatment Period
L94: A minority of participants in both the rilzabrutinib arms completed the 24-week double-blind period, 46.6% (62 of 133) and 14.5% (10 of 69) in the rilzabrutinib and placebo arms respectively. The high level of dropout raises concerns regarding the interpretability of the efficacy results.
L95: ## 3.1.2. Key Safety Review Issues
L96: None.
L97: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L98: Table 3 provides an overview of the clinical studies submitted to support the benefit-risk assessment for rilzabrutinib for patients with persistent or chronic ITP. The pivotal study, LUNA 3, was the primary source of evidence to support the finding of efficacy, with confirmatory evidence from uncontrolled, open-label, Study LUNA 2 Parts A and B.
L99: The assessment of benefit was based on a comparison of efficacy data from the double-blind treatment period of LUNA 3 where participants with persistent and chronic ITP were randomized 2:1 to receive rilzabrutinib or placebo. The assessment of risk was based primarily on the data derived from LUNA 3 during the double-blind treatment period. This was supported by analysis of an integrated pool comprised of participants with persistent or chronic ITP who received at least one dose of rilzabrutinib at the intended dose on LUNA 3 DB, open-label (OL), and long-term extension (LTE) periods and the main and long-term treatment periods of LUNA 2 Parts A and B.

L123: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L124: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L125: ## Primary Pharmacology
L126: Autoantibodies targeting platelets play a central role in the pathogenesis of ITP, simultaneously enhancing platelet clearance and suppressing thrombopoiesis. Bruton's tyrosine kinase is an enzyme and signaling molecule expressed in B cells, myeloid lineage cells (monocytes,
L127: macrophages, mast cells, and neutrophils), osteoclasts, platelets, and some cells of neural origin (neurons and astrocytes). Bruton's tyrosine kinase is a key component of the B cell receptor (BCR) that plays a role in B cell development, and it is involved in signaling pathways in myeloid cells, such as Toll-like receptor and Fc receptor signaling primarily through Fc\gammaR and Fc\epsilonR. Upon antigen binding, BTK activation occurs downstream through a cascade involving lymphocyte-specific protein tyrosine kinase (lck)/yamaguchi sarcoma virus oncogene (yes)-related protein tyrosine kinase (Lyn kinase) and spleen tyrosine kinase, leading to phosphatidylinositol-3,4,5-triphosphate kinase/protein kinase B (PI3K/AKT) strain transforming kinase pathway activation and phosphatidylinositol-3 kinase generation, which facilitates BTK recruitment and activation (Figure 1). By inhibiting BTK, rilzabrutinib blocks these downstream signaling pathways, thereby reducing antiplatelet antibody production and platelet destruction in ITP.
L128: Rilzabrutinib is an orally administered BTK inhibitor that decreases signaling downstream of the B-cell receptor in B-cells, leading to decreased antiplatelet antibody production and B-cell development. Rilzabrutinib also interrupts antibody-coated cell Fc receptor-mediated phagocytosis pathways in cells of myeloid lineage (Figure 1). Given both mechanisms contribute to platelet destruction in immune thrombocytopenia, a plausible scientific rationale for the development of rilzabrutinib for the treatment of immune thrombocytopenia is supported.
L129: Figure 1. Mechanism of Action Relevant to Reduction in Platelet Loss of Rilzabrutinib in Immune Thrombocytopenia
L130: Source: (Kuter et al. 2023)
L131: Abbreviations: AKT, Ak strain transforming kinase; serine-threonine kinase; BTK, Bruton's tyrosine kinase; Fc\gammaR, Fc gamma receptor; LYN, Lck/yes-related protein tyrosine kinase; PI3K, phosphatidylinositol-3 kinase; PIP3, phosphatidylinositol-3,4,5-triphosphate kinase; SYK, spleen tyrosine kinase
L132: The Applicant conducted in vitro studies evaluating the effect of rilzabrutinib on the potency and selectivity for BTK, binding characterization and its kinetic profile, the inhibition of BTK function, and proof of concept in vivo studies using a mouse model of ITP and other animal models of BTK-related autoimmune disorders.
L133: ## 5.1.1. In Vitro BTK Binding
L134: Rilzabrutinib exhibits high-affinity binding to BTK with a half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC_{50}) of 1.3nM in competitive inhibition with adenosine triphosphate (ATP). The binding affinity of rilzabrutinib is inversely correlated with ATP concentration (IC_{50s} were 1.3, 3.0, and 9.8nM at ATP concentrations of 16, 160 and 800\muM, respectively). The binding kinetics of rilzabrutinib are characterized by rapid association and very slow (reversible-covalent) dissociation rates, resulting in an extended residence time with a dissociation half-life of approximately 7 days. This kinetic behavior contributes to sustained target engagement, with &gt;85% occupancy observed at 24 hours postinitial binding. Rilzabrutinib forms a covalent bond with BTK, as demonstrated through controlled trypsin digestion studies. Following incubation of rilzabrutinib with excess recombinant BTK and subsequent protein denaturation via trypsin digestion, 137% \pm37% (mean; n=2) of rilzabrutinib was recovered, indicating reversibility of the covalent interaction upon protein degradation.
L135: ## 5.1.2. In Vitro Pharmacology
L136: Rilzabrutinib exhibited inhibitory effects on B cell activation in human whole blood from healthy volunteers with an IC_{50} of 123nM, while BCR-induced B cell proliferation was suppressed at an IC_{50} of 5nM. Additionally, rilzabrutinib inhibited IgG-mediated monocyte activation with an IC_{50} of 55.7nM. These findings collectively underscore rilzabrutinib's efficacy in modulating key cellular processes, including B cell activation, proliferation, and Fc\gamma R-mediated monocyte activation. The potency differences are considered to reflect differences in the experimental conditions and the IC_{50s} are not directly comparable.
L137: The specificity of rilzabrutinib for BTK-expressing cells was demonstrated by its minimal cytotoxic effects on HCT-116 cells, a BTK-negative human colorectal carcinoma cell line, with an IC_{50} of 16,000nM. This observation suggests cells lacking BTK expression are protected.
L138: Furthermore, in vitro platelet aggregation assays were conducted to evaluate the impact of rilzabrutinib on platelet function. These studies were performed using blood samples from both healthy volunteers and patients with ITP at rilzabrutinib concentrations meant to mimic anticipated plasma concentrations (i.e., 0.3\muM or 1\muM). Notably, rilzabrutinib did not significantly affect platelet aggregation in blood from either population.
L139: ## 5.1.3. In Vivo Proof-of-Concept Animal Model Data
L140: A proof-of-concept study was conducted using male BALB/c mice dosed with rilzabrutinib or control vehicle once daily for three consecutive days before induction of thrombocytopenia using anti- cluster of differentiation (CD)41 antibodies after the last dose. CD41 (integrin \alpha IIb) is a platelet-specific surface glycoprotein commonly targeted by autoantibodies in patients with ITP. Upon injection of anti-CD41 antibodies, platelets are marked for destruction by macrophages through Fc receptor-mediated phagocytosis, mimicking the autoimmune platelet destruction seen in ITP. Analysis of platelet counts six hours post anti-CD41 injection revealed that rilzabrutinib exhibited dose-dependent protective effects against platelet loss compared to vehicle-treated control (Figure 2). Results provide evidence for rilzabrutinib's capacity to mitigate antibody-induced thrombocytopenia via modulation of macrophage activity, decreasing the efficiency with
L141: which they recognized and destroyed antibody-coated platelets. A durable platelet response was observed after oral administration of rilzabrutinib in adults and adolescents with persistent or chronic immune thrombocytopenia highlighting the translational value of the in vitro and in vivo evidence.
L142: Figure 2. Effect of Rilzabrutinib on Platelet Counts in the Mouse ITP Model
L143: Source: Study report DVR0288
L144: Platelet counts in mice (n=5/group) at 6 hours following induction of ITP via anti-CD41 injection
L145: Abbreviations: ITP, immune thrombocytopenia; IVIG, intravenous immunoglobulin injection for infusion
L146: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology / Pharmacokinetics
L147: Rilzabrutinib's clinical pharmacology characteristics, including absorption, distribution, metabolism, and elimination, are summarized in the following table (Table 5).
L148: **Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L149: Characteristic	Drug Information
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Pharmacologic Activity
Kinase inhibitor
Mechanism of action	Rilzabrutinib is a small-molecule, covalent, reversible kinase inhibitor targeting Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK). Rilzabrutinib mediates its therapeutic effect in ITP through immune modulation including 1) inhibition of B cell activation, and 2) interruption of antibody-coated cell phagocytosis by Fcy receptor (FcyR) in spleen and liver. In vitro, rilzabrutinib reduced autoantibody signaling mediated through the FcyR pathway, blocked B cell signaling, and decreased autoantibody generation through effects on B cell activation.
Active moieties	Rilzabrutinib
QT prolongation	A thorough QT (TQT) study demonstrated that rilzabrutinib did not cause QTc interval prolongation (i.e., >10 msec) after a single dose of 400 mg with ritonavir (strong CYP3A inhibitor). A single dose of 400 mg administered with ritonavir adequately covers the worst-case clinical exposures. However, a concentration-dependent QTcF shortening was observed. Following a single dose of rilzabrutinib 400 mg, the mean maximum QTcF decrease of -7 msec (90% confidence interval: -9 msec to -5 msec) was observed at 2 hours postdose. The mean maximum QTcF decrease at exposures 4-fold the highest recommended dose i.e., 400 mg BID, was -10 msec (90% confidence interval: -12 msec to -8 msec) at 2 hours postdose.

Dose proportionality	Rilzabrutinib exposure (Cmax and AUC) increased in a nearly dose proportional manner (50 – 600 mg) manner, however, increasing the dose from 600 to 1200 mg did not change the exposure of rilzabrutinib.
Accumulation	Limited accumulation (1.5- to 1.8-fold) at steady state was observed after multiple dosing of 300 mg and 450 mg BID and 600 mg once daily (QD) in healthy participants. In ITP population, accumulation of up to 1.3-fold was estimated following dosing at 400 mg BID using PopPK analysis.
Time to achieve steady state	Because there is no significant accumulation upon repeat dosing, along with the short elimination half-life, the steady state exposures of rilzabrutinib can be expected to be achieved within 3 days.

Tmax	The median time to peak plasma concentrations of rilzabrutinib is approximately 1.5 hours in the fasted state (ranging from 0.5 to 2 hours).
Food effect (fed/fasted)	The effect of a high-fat meal on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics was tested with 400 mg to-be-marketed tablets in Study PRN1008-025.

Distribution
Volume of distribution	The mean volume of distribution is 149 L.
Plasma protein binding	Rilzabrutinib is approximately 97.5% bound to human plasma proteins.
Drug as substrate of transporters	Rilzabrutinib is a substrate of P-gp.
Elimination
Mass balance results	Following administration of 300 mg [14C]-rilzabrutinib, less than 5% of the dose is recovered in urine and 86% is recovered in feces. Unchanged rilzabrutinib represented about 9% of the administered dose in feces and <1% in urine (Study PRN1008-015).
Clearance	The clearance of rilzabrutinib is not time-dependent. Following multiple doses of 400 mg BID rilzabrutinib in patients with ITP, mean CL/F ranged from 246 to 911 L/h. Based on the PopPK analysis in patients with ITP, the mean CL/F was 516 L/h.
Half-life	Mean t1/2 of rilzabrutinib ranged from 1.5 to 4 hours after single dose administration of 50 or 1200 mg, and 3.8 and 4.5 hours after multiple dose administration of 300 or 600 mg.
Metabolic pathway(s)	Rilzabrutinib is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4, with minor contribution from CYP2D6.
Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	Approximately 92.9% of the dose administered orally (300 mg liquid formulation) was recovered, in which approximately 5% and 86% of the dose were recovered in the urine and the feces, respectively.
L153: ## NDA 219685
## WAYRILZ (rilzabrutinib)
L154: Characteristic	Drug Information
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	Based on PopPK analyses, body weight is a statistically significant covariate. However, the changes in rilzabrutinib exposures due to body weight are not clinically significant.
Age	Based on PopPK analyses, age is not a statistically significant covariate on rilzabrutinib exposure.
Renal impairment	A dedicated renal impairment study was not conducted. Renal elimination is not expected to be the major elimination pathway of rilzabrutinib. Less than 5% was recovered in urine in the mass balance study with unchanged rilzabrutinib only in trace amounts. PopPK analysis showed no significant difference in systemic exposure of rilzabrutinib between patients with mild or moderate renal impairment and normal renal function. The effect of severe renal impairment including effect of dialysis is unknown.
Hepatic impairment	Mild hepatic impairment did not appear to significantly affect the total systemic exposure of rilzabrutinib. There was approximately 4.5-fold higher exposure of total rilzabrutinib in participants with moderate hepatic impairment compared to subjects with normal hepatic function.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	In vitro studies show rilzabrutinib is an inhibitor and inducer of CYP3A4.
In vivo evaluation:
Coadministration of rilzabrutinib with midazolam, a CYP3A4 substrate, increased the exposure of midazolam by 2.4-fold (AUC) upon administration with a single dose of 600 mg rilzabrutinib.
PBPK simulation:
Modeling of coadministration of rilzabrutinib with midazolam also significantly increased the exposure of midazolam upon administration with rilzabrutinib at 400 mg BID for 14 days.
Please see Section 14.5.3 for details.
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	In vitro studies show rilzabrutinib is a potential inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP and OATP1B3.
In vivo evaluation:
No clinical DDI studies were conducted to evaluate the inhibitory effect of rilzabrutinib towards P-gp, BCRP or OATP1B3.
PBPK simulation:
PBPK analyses of transporter related drug interactions are considered inadequate. For risk assessment only, the simulation results cannot rule out the potentially significant interactions of rilzabrutinib on the pharmacokinetics of substrates of these transporters.
Please see Section 14.5.3 for details.

L157: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L158: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L159: ## 6.1.1. Applicant's Proposed Dosing Regimen
L160: The proposed dosing regimen of rilzabrutinib is 400 mg taken orally twice daily.
L161: This dose and dosing regimen is the same as used in LUNA 3: Selection of Dosing Regimen for Phase 3 Studies.
L162: The phase 1 study, PRN1008-001, suggested rilzabrutinib was well-tolerated up to 600 mg once daily (QD) and 450 mg twice daily (BID). Rilzabrutinib was evaluated in the Phase 1/2 Study, LUNA 2, which was conducted in two parts in individuals with ITP. Part A consisted of intrapatient dose escalation using a 3+3 design, where treatment was initiated with 1 of 4 doses: 200 mg QD, 400 mg QD, 300 mg BID, or 400 mg BID. Intrapatient dose escalation was allowed every 28 days if the participant did not experience a platelet response, as defined in the primary endpoint, or a dose-limiting toxicity (DLT) at that dose level. Once a participant escalated to the 400 mg BID dose, they remained at that dose. Part B was an open-label study that investigated the selected dose of 400 mg BID in a new cohort. In both parts A and B, the active treatment period was 24 weeks.
L163: Based on BTK occupancies in the mouse model of antiplatelet driven immune thrombocytopenia, the top dose of 40 mg/kg/day resulted in a maximum BTK occupancy of 91%, matching the expected peak occupancies with the maximum 400 mg QD and 400 mg BID doses. The 400 mg BID dose was expected to produce higher trough, or predose BTK occupancies relative to QD dosing, while having similar peak values. Dosing above 400 mg BID was thought unlikely to result in greater target inhibition. In Part A of LUNA 2, among patients who initiated and maintained rilzabrutinib doses of 400 mg BID throughout the main treatment period, 40.0% achieved the primary platelet response (Table 7). Primary platelet response was defined as \ge 2 consecutive platelet counts, separated by at least 5 days of \ge 50,000/\mu\text{L} and increased \ge 20,000/\mu\text{L} from baseline without requiring rescue medication. Table 8 summarizes primary platelet response by dose level (dose attributed to/before the response) and overall, for the main treatment period. From the dose finding part of Study LUNA 2, the 400 mg rilzabrutinib BID dosing regimen was also associated with a faster and more pronounced increase in platelet count compared to lower doses. Based on the data from this dose-finding portion of the study, the 400 mg BID dose was selected for further investigation in Part B of LUNA 2.

L174: Based on results from LUNA 2, the Applicant selected 400 mg BID rilzabrutinib for further evaluation in the pivotal Phase 3 study PRN1008-018. As noted earlier, a dosing regimen higher than 400 mg BID were not expected to result in any meaningful increase in BTK receptor occupancy. Moreover, systemic exposure of rilzabrutinib reaches a plateau beyond 600 mg.
L175: ## 6.1.1.1. Exposure-Response for Efficacy and Safety
L176: Exposure-response (E-R) analyses of efficacy were conducted using data from study LUNA 3 based on the repeated-measures endpoints (longitudinal platelet counts) and rilzabrutinib exposure metrics (C_{\text{max,ss}}, C_{\text{min,ss}}, and area under the concentration-time curve (AUC_{\text{ss}})). In
L177: addition, exploratory analyses were performed using studies LUNA 2 and LUNA 3 to determine the exposure response relationship for three adverse events (AEs) (nausea, diarrhea, and anemia). The results of the E-R analyses (Figure 3) show a trend for greater increase in platelet counts from baseline with higher rilzabrutinib exposure (AUC_{ss}). However, response rate as defined by the primary endpoint of LUNA 3, i.e., durable platelet response, did not appear to depend on exposure over the range at 400 mg BID (Table 10). In addition, participants with higher rilzabrutinib exposures (AUC_{ss} and C_{min,ss}) had relatively higher rates of diarrhea and nausea events, Figure 4 and Figure 5). See detail in Section 14.5.2.

L192: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Studies Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L193: ## 6.2.1. LUNA 3 Study
L194: ## 6.2.1.1. Design, LUNA 3
L195: The study design of LUNA 3 is shown in Figure 6.
L196: ## NDA 219685
## WAYRILZ (rilzabrutinib)
L197: LUNA 3 was a global, multicenter randomized, parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial in approximately 194 adults4 with ITP who had had an unsustained response to either intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG), anti-D, or CS. Participants were randomized 2:1 to receive rilzabrutinib 400 mg taken orally twice daily or matching placebo. Randomization was stratified by splenectomy status (yes/no) and severity of thrombocytopenia (platelet count &lt;15,000/\mu\text{L} or \ge 15,000/\mu\text{L}). Concomitant ITP medications (an oral CS and/or a TPO-RA) were permitted.
L198: The primary assessment was performed on a 24-week blinded treatment period, which was followed by a 28-week open-label period during which all participants received rilzabrutinib. An initial assessment for platelet response was performed after 12 weeks of blinded treatment (Week 13). Participants who had a platelet response5 during the first 12 weeks continued blinded treatment for a total of 24 weeks. Participants who did not have a platelet response6 could discontinue from the study or enter the 28-week OL period.
L199: The primary objective of the study was to demonstrate the efficacy of rilzabrutinib versus placebo in participants with refractory/relapsed ITP.
L200: The primary efficacy endpoint was durable platelet response defined as the proportion of participants able to achieve platelet counts at or above 50,000/\mu\text{L} for \ge two-thirds of at least 8 nonmissing weekly scheduled platelet measurements during the last 12 weeks of the 24-week blinded treatment period in the absence of rescue therapy, provided that at least 2 nonmissing weekly scheduled platelet measurements were at or above 50,000/\mu\text{L} during the last 6 weeks of the 24-week blinded treatment period.
L201: 4 Although the study enrolled both adult and adolescent participants, only data from adult (defined as age at least 18) participants is the subject of this review and all information in this section should be assumed as related to adults.
L202: 5 Platelet response for assessment at Week 13 is defined as achieving a platelet count of \ge 50,000/\mu\text{L} OR a platelet count \ge 30,000/\mu\text{L} and &lt; 50,000/\mu\text{L} and at least doubled from baseline at any time during the first 12 weeks AND the absence of rescue medication in the 4 weeks prior to platelet count that meets the Week 13 response criterion.
L203: 6 Ibid.
L204: Figure 6. Study Design, LUNA 3
L205: Source: Study LUNA 3 Clinical Study Report, Figure 1
L206: ## 6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, LUNA 3
L207: Important eligibility criteria are summarized below, and the full criteria are available in Section 15.1.1
L208: ## Inclusion Criteria
L209: - Male and female participants with primary ITP with a duration of &gt;3 months
- Response (achievement of platelet count \ge 50,000/\mu\text{L}) to intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIg), anti-D, or corticosteroids (CSs) that was not sustained and documented intolerance, insufficient response, or any contraindication to any appropriate courses of standard of care ITP therapy
- An average of 2 platelet counts at least 5 days apart of &lt;30,000/\mu\text{L} during the screening period, and no single platelet count &gt;35,000/\mu\text{L}, within 14 days prior to the first dose of study drug
L210: ## Exclusion Criteria
L211: - Participants with secondary ITP
- Change in CS and/or TPO-RA dose within 14 days prior to Study Day 1 (more than 10% variation from current doses)
- Treatment with rituximab or splenectomy within the 3 months prior to Study Day 1
Participants treated with rituximab were required to have normal B-cell counts prior to enrollment.
L212: ## 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, LUNA 3
L213: In the final statistical analysis plan (SAP) version 3, dated March 18, 2024, the primary efficacy analysis compared rilzabrutinib to placebo using the Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel test adjusted by randomization stratification factors based on the ITT population which included all participants who are randomized. Participants were analyzed according to the intervention allocated by randomization. The Mantel-Haenszel estimate of common risk difference in response rates and its associated 95% CI based on Mantel-Haenszel stratum weights and the Sato variance estimator were reported. The observed response rate for each treatment group was presented along with its associated 95% asymptotic CI.
L214: The key secondary endpoints included in the statistical testing hierarchy and analysis methods are summarized below.
L215: - Number of weeks with platelet count \ge 50,000/\mu\text{L} or between \ge 30,000/\mu\text{L} and &lt;50,000/\mu\text{L} and at least doubled from baseline over the 24-week blinded treatment period in the absence of rescue therapy. The mixed-effect model with repeated measures (MMRM) approach was used to analyze the longitudinal binary platelet response data across visits in the ITT population.
- Number of weeks with platelet counts \ge 30,000/\mu\text{L} and at least doubled from baseline over the 24-week blinded treatment period in the absence of rescue therapy. The MMRM approach was used to analyze the longitudinal binary platelet response data across visits in the ITT population.
- Time to first platelet count of \ge 50,000/\mu\text{L} OR between \ge 30,000/\mu\text{L} and &lt;50,000/\mu\text{L} and doubled from baseline. The log-rank test adjusted by randomization stratification factors was used. Hazard ratio and its 95% CI were modeled using Cox regression model with treatment and randomization stratification factors as covariates.
- Proportion of participants requiring rescue therapy during the 24-week blinded treatment period. The log-rank test adjusted by randomization stratification factors was used. Hazard ratio and its 95% CI were modeled using Cox regression model with treatment and randomization stratification factors as covariates.
- Change from baseline on Item 10 of the Immune Thrombocytopenia-Patient Assessment Questionnaire (ITP-PAQ) (i.e., physical fatigue) in adult participants (\ge 18 years) at Week 13. The analysis was based on the analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model with the treatment group, randomization stratification factors, and geographic region as fixed effects and baseline Item 10 score as a covariate.
L216: For additional SAP details see 16.1.1.

L218: ## 6.2.1.4.1. Patient Disposition
L219: Table 11 summarizes participant disposition for adult participants. In LUNA 3, a total of 393 participants were screened and 202 were randomized: 133 who received rilzabrutinib 400 mg twice daily and 69 who received placebo. Most participants completed 12 weeks of the double-blind period in both the rilzabrutinib group (121 [91.0%] participants) and the placebo group (66 [95.7%] participants). The percentage of participants who completed the 24-week double-blind
L220: period was higher in the rilzabrutinib group compared with the placebo group (62 [46.6%] participants versus 10 [14.5%] participants, respectively). The most frequent reason for discontinuation of treatment in the double-blind period was lack of response, which was lower in the rilzabrutinib group compared with the placebo group (55 [41.4%] participants and 55 [79.7%] participants, respectively).

L258: ## 6.2.1.4.4. Subgroup Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
L259: Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in the treatment effect of rilzabrutinib compared to placebo based on durable platelet response across various demographic and clinical characteristic groups. Overall, the treatment effect was consistent across baseline subgroups and demonstrated positive trend in favor of rilzabrutinib (Figure 28). Since these subgroup analyses are limited by the small numbers of subjects, the results should be interpreted with caution.

L286: ## **Evaluation of the PRO**
L287: The fifth key secondary endpoint was change from baseline on Item 10 of ITP-PAQ (i.e., physical fatigue) in adult participants (\ge 18 years) at Week 13.
L288: The Division of Clinical Outcomes Assessment (DCOA), after review of all relevant information provided by the Applicant, concluded that there was insufficient evidence to support that ITP-PAQ Item 10 is fit-for-purpose for the context of this development program. For complete details, see COA review memo dated March 27, 2025 (DARRTS Reference ID: 5558745).
L289: Key concerns with the instrument include the following:
L290: - The ITP-PAQ uses only one item (Item 10) to assess physical fatigue over the past 4 weeks. A single item cannot sufficiently measure fatigue, which is multidimensional (physical fatigue, mental fatigue). In addition, Item 10 measures both disease and treatment-related
L291: symptoms, making it a double-barreled question (measures more than one concept, e.g., it asks, “In the past 4 weeks, how often did ITP or its treatment cause you to feel physically fatigued?”) which leads to unwanted variability in the data. Combining these concepts into one question makes it unclear what is being measured.

2. The Patient Global Impression of Severity (PGIS) and Patient Global Impression of Change (PGIC) anchor scales to assess the threshold for meaningful within-patient score change in the ITP-PAQ Fatigue domain score limit the interpretability of the clinically meaningfulness of the statistical results, as they do not align with the ITP-PAQ Item 10. Specifically, the concept measured in the PGIS and PGIC items is generic (disease-related symptoms; disease-related fatigue) and is not fully aligned with the concept of the ITP-PAQ item 10 (physical fatigue). Likewise, the recall period of the PGIS items (“past week”) does not align with the assessment time period of the prespecified ITP-PAQ Item 10 endpoint (“past 4 weeks”). Furthermore, while ITP-PAQ Item 10 measures symptom frequency, the PGIS items measure symptom severity.
L292: (b) (4)
L293: ## 6.2.2. Confirmatory Evidence of Effectiveness
L294: Chronic ITP is a rare, serious, and potentially life-threatening disease. It is reasonable in this situation to establish effectiveness based on one adequate and well-controlled study plus confirmatory evidence. LUNA 3 is an adequate and well-controlled study that provides strong evidence of rilzabrutinib's effectiveness. LUNA 2, an open-label dose-finding with cohort expansion study in participants with ITP who have relapsed or have had an insufficient response to prior therapies, provides confirmatory evidence of effectiveness for rilzabrutinib based on the observed PD effects.
L295: ## 6.2.2.1. Confirmatory Evidence, LUNA 2
L296: ## Study Design
L297: Refer to Section 15.2
L298: ## Enrolled Population
L299: Part A enrolled 60 participants with 95% having a diagnosis of primary ITP and 5% with secondary ITP. The mean duration of ITP was 11.3 years (standard deviation (SD): 13.41) with a median of 6.3 years (range: 0.4, 52.5). The median baseline platelet count was 14.75 \times 10^9/L (range: 0.4, 33.3) with 50% of participants having a platelet count &lt;15,000/\mu L. The median number of prior unique ITP therapies (including splenectomy) was 3 (range: 1, 14).
L300: Part B enrolled 26 participants with a diagnosis of primary ITP. The mean duration of ITP was 13.1 years (SD: 12.3) with a median of 10.3 years (range: 0.7, 48.2). The median baseline platelet count was 13.2 \times 10^9/L (range: 1.7, 23.7) with 54% of participants having a platelet count &lt;15,000/\mu L. The median number of prior unique ITP therapies (including splenectomy) was 6 (range: 3, 17).
L301: ## **Analysis of the Primary Efficacy Endpoint**
L302: Efficacy endpoints in LUNA 2 Parts A and B were assessed during the 24-week treatment period using descriptive statistics. See Section 6.1.
L303: In Part A, of the 60 participants evaluated for efficacy, cumulatively across dosages, 24 participants (40.0%) had a platelet response as defined above (Table 8).
L304: In Part B, of the 26 participants evaluated for efficacy in the ITT population, 9 participants (34.6%) had a durable platelet response, as defined above (Table 9).
L305: ## **Conclusions**
L306: Spontaneous increases in platelet count, particularly sustained increases as defined for the primary endpoint in Part B, in heavily pretreated participants with chronic ITP would not be expected. Therefore, it is reasonable to consider the responses observed in this single arm study as evidence of activity related to rilzabrutinib and confirmatory evidence supporting the findings from LUNA 3.
L307: ## **6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues**
L308: ## **6.3.1. High Dropout Prior to Completion of the 24-Week Double-Blind Treatment Period**
L309: ## **Issue**
L310: A minority of participants in both the rilzabrutinib arms completed the 24-week double-blind period, 46.6% (62 of 133) and 14.5% (10 of 69) in the rilzabrutinib and placebo arms respectively. The high level of dropout raises concerns regarding the interpretability of the efficacy results.
L311: ## **Background**
L312: The design of LUNA 3 included an assessment at Week 13, and participants who did not respond during the first 12 weeks of blinded treatment were unblinded and allowed to switch to other treatments, i.e., crossing over to rilzabrutinib arm for nonresponders in the placebo arm or discontinuing study to receive other treatments off study. This design element is consistent with the clinical approach to treating participants with low platelets where therapies are assessed for a limited period and switched to something else, given the risk for serious bleeding. This approach is also similar to other clinical studies in ITP.
L313: ## **Assessment**
L314: The Applicant and review team conducted multiple sensitivity analyses under varying assumptions to assess the impact of missing data and dropouts to ensure the robustness of the primary efficacy findings of the evaluation of rilzabrutinib's effectiveness.
L315: ## Durable Platelet Response During the 12-Week Double-Blind Period
L316: To explore the consistency of treatment effect over the first 12-week duration compared to the second 12-weeks, the statistical review team examined durable platelet response through Week 12 and found that 25 (18.8%) participants, (95% CI: 12.16%, 25.44%) in the rilzabrutinib group achieved durable response, compared to one (1.4%) participant, (95% CI: 0.00, 4.27%) in the placebo group. The Mantel-Haenszel risk difference was 17.0% [95% CI: 9.92, 24.12], with a p-value of 0.005. This result is consistent to the primary analysis results for the primary endpoint, which assessed durable platelet count between Week 12 and 24.
L317: ## Sensitivity Analyses: Durable Platelet Response During the 12-Week and 24-Week Double-Blind Periods
L318: The Applicant conducted multiple sensitivity analyses per FDA statistical review team requests to handle all types of missing data regardless of the reasons for missing data or dropouts rather than only treated lack of response or related AE as nonresponder. Table 116 and Table 117 present tipping point analyses for the 12-week and 24-week periods, respectively. In these analyses, participants with missing response data were imputed as responders at varying probabilities—denoted as p_0 for the placebo group and p_1 for the rilzabrutinib group. The values of p_0 and p_1 were incrementally increased from 0% to 100% in 10% steps to evaluate the impact of different imputation assumptions on the treatment effect.
L319: Table 116 and Table 117 showed that the statistical significance of the treatment effect was lost when the assumed response rate among missing participants is relatively high in the placebo group (p_0 close to 0.2) and relatively low in the rilzabrutinib group (p_1 close to 0). For example, when p_0=0.2, the p-value is no longer significant if p_1=0 or 0.1. In this study, the placebo group had a durable response rate below 5%, indicating that assuming a higher p_0 is not consistent with the observed data. Overall, the tipping point results demonstrated that the primary analyses results for rilzabrutinib on durable platelet response during the 12-week and 24-week double-blind period remain robust (see Section 16.1.2).
L320: ## Over Time Performance
L321: Over time analyses of platelet count change from baseline further support the primary analysis. Figure 7 shows the median platelet count by visit during the 24-week double-blind treatment period. Mann-Whitney tests were performed to assess the distribution of platelet counts at Week 2 through Week 25 between the rilzabrutinib arm and the placebo arm (Table 20). Figure 7 and Table 20 show that the platelet counts in the rilzabrutinib arm were higher than those in the placebo arm across Week 2 to Week 25, with all nominal p-values \le0.05.

Abbreviation: ITT, intent-to-treat
L327: ## Conclusion
L328: To address the high amount of missing data in both treatment arms, multiple sensitivity analyses were conducted to assess the impact of missing data and dropouts on the primary endpoint. Tipping point analyses were also performed by varying imputation of the assumed performance in both treatment groups. Results showed that statistical significance was generally maintained unless implausibly high response rates were assumed in the placebo group. In addition, analyses of platelet count over time from baseline to Week 25 showed consistently higher platelet counts in the rilzabrutinib group compared to placebo, with nominal p-values \le 0.05 at all visits. The review team determined that missing data did not meaningfully change the conclusion of effectiveness of rilzabrutinib for the treatment of ITP in adults.
L329: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L330: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L331: A comprehensive toxicological evaluation in healthy animals revealed that rilzabrutinib primarily affected the liver in dogs, the gastrointestinal tract in dogs and rats, and brain in rats. Refer to Section 13.1.5 for detailed study descriptions.
L332: ## Hepatotoxicity
L333: Liver findings in the chronic 39-week study in dogs included reversible increases in alanine aminotransferase (ALT) and aspartate aminotransferase (AST), with additional increases in ALP observed in females. The changes in liver enzymes were coincident with microscopic hepatic findings including Kupffer cell hypertrophy and pigment accumulation. Kupffer cell hypertrophy was noted at \ge 30 mg/kg/day females and 50 mg/kg/day males and increased Kupffer cell pigmentation at 50 mg/kg/day in both males and females with exposures ranges approximating the clinical exposures, respectively, the clinical exposures at the maximum recommended dose (MRHD) based on AUC. While Kupffer cell hypertrophy with increased pigmentation can indicate phagocytic uptake of foreign materials or cellular debris that may occur during pathogenic processes and ALT/AST and/or ALP increases were observed, it is important to note that no significant histopathological impacts on hepatocytes were observed. No significant hepatic findings were observed in rats in studies of durations up to 26 weeks.
L334: The issue of hepatotoxicity in humans for various BTK inhibitors is well known and described in literature and product labeling. However, available data indicate that animal studies are poor predictors of the type of idiosyncratic drug-induced liver injury (DILI) observed in patients treated with BTK inhibitors. Idiosyncratic DILI typically has a complex, immune-related etiology, in contrast to direct toxicity to hepatocytes. Given that rilzabrutinib may be associated with infrequent idiosyncratic DILI events in humans based on observations with this class of drugs, this risk is better characterized by clinical trial data.
L335: ## Gastrointestinal Toxicity
L336: Findings in the chronic 26-week rat study were observed in the gastrointestinal (GI) tract, which occurred primarily at doses \ge 150 mg/kg/day that resulted in exposures \ge 5 times the clinical exposures at the MRHD, based on AUC. Findings consisted of hemorrhage in duodenum, esophagus, and stomach in the presence of mixed cell and neutrophil inflammation. Findings in dogs consisted of mild increases in intraepithelial lymphocytes with mucosal atrophy at 50 mg/kg/day in males with exposures approximating the clinical exposures at the MRHD, based on AUC, indicative of gastritis. GI side effects are not uncommon with this class of drugs.
L337: ## Brain Findings
L338: While acute oral doses (single or 3 daily exposures) of rilzabrutinib to Wistar Han rats up to 500 mg/kg (21 times the clinical exposure, based on AUC) did not affect the gross behavioral, physiological, or neurological state of the animals, repeated oral administration of rilzabrutinib at 500 mg/kg/day caused minimal to mild neutrophilic inflammation in the meninges and neuropil
L339: and increased ionized calcium binding adaptor molecule 1 (IBA-1) positivity (indicating microglial activation) in brain. With repeated dosing for \ge 13 weeks (including the 26-week chronic study), minimal to mild neutrophil infiltration in the meninges and perivascular spaces, as well as alterations in IBA-1 expression in specific brain regions were observed at 5 times the clinical exposures, based on AUC, indicative of microgliosis. Concurrent increases in glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP) expression, indicative of astrocyte activity, were observed in similar areas at all doses tested. Rilzabrutinib-induced changes in IBA-1 and GFAP expression showed partial resolution at the end of recovery periods.
L340: While rilzabrutinib induced inflammatory changes (neutrophil infiltration, microglial activation) in rat brains during chronic toxicology studies, importantly, these effects occurred without evidence of neuronal damage or degeneration. The occurrence of microgliosis in the absence a direct effect on neurons is consistent with the reported BTK expression pattern in the brain (higher microglial expression, lower neuronal expression), although rilzabrutinib brain penetration was shown to be limited. This implies these brain effects are secondary to some other process (e.g., interaction with the gut-brain axis). No similar findings were observed in dogs.
L341: While the clinical risk of microgliosis remains uncertain, the findings occurred only at clinical exposure margins in excess (\ge 5 times) of the clinical exposures, with no evidence of findings at the clinical exposures.
L342: ## Reproductive Toxicology
L343: Refer to Section 8.4.
L344: ## Genetic Toxicology and Carcinogenicity
L345: There are no concerns for malignancy based on negative results of genetic toxicology and carcinogenicity studies with rilzabrutinib, see Sections 13.1.7 and 13.1.8.
L346: ## Nonclinical Recommendation
L347: Overall, there are no nonclinical safety issues that would preclude approvability for the treatment of persistent or chronic ITP and no postmarketing requirements (PMRs) are recommended.
L348: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L349: There are currently three FDA approved irreversible Bruton's tyrosine kinase inhibitors (BTKIs)7 and one reversible BTKI8. All are approved only in oncology indications. Across the class of BTKIs, the following risks are identified in Section 5, Warnings and Precautions: Infection, Hemorrhage, Cardiac arrhythmia/failure, Hypertension, Cytopenias, (Second) malignancy, Hepatotoxicity. These risks represent both on-target, off-target, and combination effects. Observation of these risks with rilzabrutinib may differ from the approved BTKIs given it is a more selective BTKI, and based on non-clinical studies, exhibits a rapid “on-rate” and a slow
L350: 7 Ibrutinib (Imbruvica), acalabrutinib (Calquence), zanubutnib (Brukinsa).
L351: 8 Pirtobrutinib (Jaypirca).
L352: “off-rate” for BTK, ensuring prolonged target occupancy even with low systemic exposure, favoring lower off target effect.
L353: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns
## Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L354: Rilzabrutinib is not currently approved or marketed in the United States or in any country.
L355: ## 7.3.1. Adverse Events Identified in Postmarket Experiences
L356: Rilzabrutinib is not currently approved or marketed in the United States or in any country.
L357: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L358: The safety assessment for the intended population of patients with chronic or recurrent ITP is based on data derived from a total of 276 unique participants who received at least one dose of the proposed dosage of rilzabrutinib (400 mg orally twice daily) while enrolled in LUNA 3 or LUNA 3. A description of these studies is provided in Table 3. The data cutoff for LUNA 3 was March 14, 2024, and for LUNA 2 Part A, May 4, 2021, and Part B, January 31, 2023.
L359: The primary safety profile for the proposed dosing regimen of rilzabrutinib 400 mg twice daily in adults with persistent or chronic ITP is based on analysis of safety data from the double-blind (DB) 24-week treatment period of LUNA 3. Additional analyses were performed on pooled ITP populations (See Section 17.1 for description)
L360: The application also included safety data from Study PRN 1008-023 (See Table 3), a phase 1 pharmacokinetics and tolerability study in healthy subjects, review of which did not identify any additional potential safety signals.
L361: Clinical study data were independently analyzed by the Agency. Clinical data scientists from the Division of Biomedical Informatics who generated the safety tables and figures. These include safety tables and figures for participants disposition, exposure data, AEs, and serious adverse events (SAEs). Frequency analyses were performed based on preferred term (PT) and based using Office of New Drug Custom Medical Queries (OCMQ) (narrow). Additional exploration and analysis of AEs were performed by the clinical reviewers using JMP, JMP Clinical, and the Office of Computational Science Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA)-based Adverse Event Diagnostics tool.
L362: No major data quality or integrity issues were identified that would preclude performing a safety review for this NDA. There were no major identified issues with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs. The Applicant’s translations of verbatim terms to MedDRA lowest level term for the events reported in LUNA 3 were reviewed and found to be acceptable. Treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs) were protocol-defined as adverse events that developed, worsened, or became serious during the treatment-emergent period (after the first dose of rilzabrutinib had been administered and until the end of study visit [4 weeks after the last dose of study intervention]). Serious adverse events were defined as any untoward medical occurrence that, at
L363: any dose that results in death, is life-threatening, requires hospitalization or prolongation of existing hospitalization, results in persistent incapacity or substantial disruption of the ability to conduct normal life functions, or is a congenital anomaly or birth defect. All AEs in the reviewed studies were graded using common terminology criteria for adverse events (CTCAE) version 5.0 (CTEP 2017).
L364: Review of safety was based upon:
L365: - Clinical Study Report for studies.
- Protocol for studies.
- Datasets for populations noted above.
- Summary of clinical safety.
- 120-day safety update.
- Patient narratives.
- Case report forms.
- Case report forms and narratives were provided and reviewed for deaths, SAEs, adverse events of special interest, and discontinuations associated with AEs.
L366: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L367: The safety database is adequate for the comprehensive safety assessment of rilzabrutinib for the proposed indication, patient population, dosage regimen, and duration. Table 21 summarizes the duration of exposure during the double-blind treatment period in LUNA 3. The mean duration of exposure for rilzabrutinib was 17.4 weeks versus 13.5 weeks for placebo. Nearly 37% of participants treated with rilzabrutinib remained on treatment at 24 weeks compared to only 13% of those who received placebo.

L375: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L376: ## Overall Summary
L377: The safety profile of rilzabrutinib in patients with ITP is based primarily on analysis of data from the DB period of LUNA 3 with additional evaluation of safety assessments and reporting from the open-label periods of LUNA 3 and LUNA 2 Parts A and B.
L378: The overall safety profile of rilzabrutinib at the proposed dose regimen for the treatment of adult patients with persistent and chronic ITP is acceptable. Key findings are:
L379: - There was one death on LUNA 3, which occurred during the DB period in a participant receiving rilzabrutinib. The fatal TEAE was pulmonary aspergillosis and was assessed by the review team as related to rilzabrutinib.
- The frequency of serious adverse events was similar between arms. In the rilzabrutinib arm, 9% of participants had at least one SAE. After review of the narratives, four participants in the rilzabrutinib arm (3%) were assessed as having had at least one SAE (considered at least possibly related to rilzabrutinib). These SAEs were all infection related.
- Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation occurred in 6% of participants in the rilzabrutinib arm and no participants in the placebo arm. These included pneumonia (fatal), neutropenia, peripheral embolism, erythema nodosum, and multijoint pain, each occurring in one participant. Multiple TEAEs occurred in three additional patients leading to discontinuation of rilzabrutinib. They were diarrhea, nausea, abdominal discomfort; esophageal varices hemorrhage hepatic enzyme increased; and heartburn, headache, and leg pain. No TEAE at the PT level occurred in more than one participant.
- Important adverse events of special interest occurring in participants while receiving rilzabrutinib on clinical trials are: infections, neutropenia, and second malignancies.
L380: - The most common adverse events at the system organ class (SOC) level occurring in \ge 20\% of participants in the rilzabrutinib arm and more frequently than in the placebo arm are Gastrointestinal disorders (54% in rilzabrutinib arm compared to 23% in placebo arm), Infections and Infestations (33% in rilzabrutinib arm compared to 20% in placebo arm), Musculoskeletal and Connective Tissue Disorders (20% in rilzabrutinib arm compared to 12% in placebo arm), and Nervous System Disorders (24% in rilzabrutinib arm compared to 12% in placebo arm).
- The most common adverse reaction, occurring in at least 5% of participants in the rilzabrutinib arm and at least 3% more than in the placebo arm are: diarrhea (32%, nausea (20%), headache(18%), COVID-19 (14%), arthralgia (9%), dizziness (8%), abdominal pain (8%), nasopharyngitis (7%), vomiting (7%), abdominal pain upper (5%), dyspepsia (5%), and cough (5%).
- Evaluation of an integrated safety population comprising 276 participants with ITP who received at least one dose of rilzabrutinib at 400 mg orally twice daily in LUNA 3 and LUNA 2 Parts A and B did not identify any new or more serious safety signal, including for rare but serious events that may be detectable only in a larger dataset.
L381: The 120-day safety update was reviewed and found consistent with the original safety analysis.
L382: ## 7.6.1. Safety Results, LUNA 3
L383: ## 7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Double-Blind Treatment Period, LUNA 3
L384: Table 22 presents a high-level summary of TEAEs based on frequency analysis of the DB treatment period for LUNA 3
L385: During the DB treatment period, 84% of participants treated with rilzabrutinib compared to 75% of those treated with placebo experienced at least one TEAE. Most TEAEs in the rilzabrutinib arm were Grade 1 or 2 in severity, with 11% of participants having at least one Grade \ge 3 TEAE. One participant treated with rilzabrutinib died due to *Aspergillus* pneumonia. SAEs were reported in 12 (9%) participants treated with rilzabrutinib as compared to 8 (12%) of placebo treated participants. Permanent discontinuation due to TEAE was reported in eight (6%) of rilzabrutinib treated participants, while no placebo treated participants discontinued treatment due to AEs. Dose interruption due to TEAEs was reported for seven (5.3%) rilzabrutinib treated participants compared to two (2.9%) placebo treated participants.

L395: ## 7.6.1.2. Deaths, LUNA 3
L396: ## Double-Blind Period
L397: One participant had a TEAE leading to death during the 24-week DB period. This death occurred in a participant who received rilzabrutinib and died due to pneumonia. Summary narrative for this death is provided below. The Division assessed the event as at least possibly related to rilzabrutinib based on the mechanism of action of rilzabrutinib and the association with other BTKIs with an increased risk of infection.
L398: **Participant** [REDACTED] (b) (6) was a 75-year-old white female with chronic ITP and long-term corticosteroid use who received rilzabrutinib and developed pneumonia on Study Day 61. Bronchoalveolar lavage culture grew cytomegalovirus, candidiasis, *Aspergillus fumigatus* and *nocardia spp* and *Aspergillus fumigatus*. Rilzabrutinib was permanently discontinued due to the event of pneumonia with last dose of rilzabrutinib administered on Day 72. During time on study the participant continued to concomitantly receive corticosteroids. On Day 76 the participant died due to pneumonia.
L399: The Investigator and Applicant assessed the death as unrelated to rilzabrutinib. The review team did not agree and assessed the event as at least possibly related to rilzabrutinib. Although the participant had a long-standing history of corticosteroid use, which is a risk factor for development of fungal infections including *Aspergillus spp.*, BTKIs, including rilzabrutinib, can impact immune cell function and response and can increase susceptibility to infections and fatal
L400: and non-fatal infections (including bacterial, viral or fungal) have been occurred in patients treated with Bruton Tyrosine Kinase inhibitors.
L401: ## Open-Label and Long-Term Extension Periods
L402: As of the cutoff dates, no adverse event leading to death had occurred in the OL or the LTE periods.
L403: ## 7.6.1.3. Serious Adverse Events, LUNA 3
L404: ## Double-Blind Period
L405: A frequency-based analysis of SAEs during the DB period, by MedDRA PT, is provided in Table 23. The frequency of SAEs was similar between arms (12 [9%] rilzabrutinib versus 8 [12%] in placebo). Analysis using OCMQ (narrow) did not identify any safety signal not already captured in analysis by preferred term (see Table 118). At the PT level, no PT occurs more than once in an arm. At the SOC level, the most commonly occurring SAE in participants who received rilzabrutinib is Infections and Infestations, which occurred more frequently in participants in the rilzabrutinib arm (3%) compared to placebo (0%); however, SAEs in this SOC are still rare, and the observed difference between arms may be due to chance.
L406: A total of 15 SAEs were reported in 12 participants in the rilzabrutinib group during the DB period. The PTs of immune thrombocytopenia, thrombocytopenia, and platelet count decreased each occurred in one participant and are considered related to the underlying disease. The PTs of periorbital hematoma (Participant (b) (6)), vaginal hemorrhage (Participant (b) (6)), and epistaxis (Participant (b) (6)) each occurred in one participant. At the time of each of these events, the participant's platelet count was less than 10 \times 10^9/L. These events are considered related to the underlying disease. The PT of esophageal varices hemorrhage is discussed below with narrative for Participant (b) (6).
L407: The remaining eight SAEs not considered related to ITP occurred in six participants. Narrative for the fatal SAE of pneumonia occurring in Participant (b) (6) is discussed with Deaths. Narratives for the remaining participants having at least one SAE are presented below.
L408: Based on the narratives, the Division assessed the following SAEs as at least possibly related to rilzabrutinib: pneumonia (Participant (b) (6)); urinary tract infection and renal abscess (Participant (b) (6)); wound infection (Participant (b) (6)); coronavirus disease of 2019 (COVID-19) infection (Participant (b) (6)).
