L418: ## Narratives
L419: - **Participant** (b) (6) is a 50-year-old female with chronic ITP and history of iliac artery stenosis, peripheral artery occlusive disease (ongoing for 3.5 years prior to study start), artery bypass, and stent insertion who was receiving concomitant eltrombopag and experienced the SAE of **peripheral embolism**. On Day 32 of the blinded treatment period with rilzabrutinib, the participant was hospitalized for grade 3 peripheral embolism of the left lower leg. Magnetic resonance imaging demonstrated occlusion of the previously known bypass at the left lower leg. Platelet count was 112,000 cells/microliter. The participant underwent thrombolysis. Rilzabrutinib was permanently discontinued due to the event and last administered on Day 31. The event was reported as recovered on Day 36.
- The investigator's assessment of causality was related to the rilzabrutinib and concomitant use of thrombopoietin receptor agonist. The Applicant assessed the event as was not related to rilzabrutinib. Based on rilzabrutinib's mechanism of action, platelet count at
L420: the time of the event, and the participant's history of prior arterial occlusion, bypass and stent placement. The review team agreed with the Applicant's assessment that the event was not related to rilzabrutinib.
L421: - **Participant** (b) (6) is a 58-year-old white male with a history of chronic ITP and fungal skin infection at baseline who was randomized to the rilzabrutinib treatment group and continued concomitant prednisone.

The SAE of **urinary tract infection (Grade 3)** was reported on treatment Day 54 and required hospitalization. Laboratory assessments were significant for elevations in creatinine, C-reactive protein (426 \text{ mg/L}) white blood cell (33.26 \times 10^9/\text{L}), erythrocyte sedimentation rate (77 \text{ mm/hr}), and low platelets of 6 \times 10^9/\text{L}. Urine culture was negative. The participant was treated with IV antibiotics followed by oral to Day 62. Corticosteroid dose was initially increased due to urinary infection but then decreased for the kidney abscess. Rilzabrutinib was interrupted on Day 55 and resumed on Day 79.

- The SAE of **renal abscess (Grade 3)** occurred on Day 84 and required hospitalization. Platelets at time were 8 \times 10^9/\text{L}. Treatment included drainage and culture (*Klebsiella pneumoniae*) of the abscess and IV antibiotics. IVIg was administered on Day 84 for platelet count less than 20 \times 10^9/\text{L} and need for procedure. Rilzabrutinib was interrupted from Day 84 to Day 94.

- The Investigator's and Applicant's assessments of causality for the SAEs of urinary tract infection and renal abscess was not related to rilzabrutinib. Based on the mechanism of action and known risk of infection associated with BTKIs, the review team did not agree and assessed the event as *at least possibly related* to rilzabrutinib.

- **Participant** (b) (6) is a 70-year-old white male with history of chronic ITP, obesity, hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and a cat bite to left wrist (4 months prior to study) with a subsequent skin graft (one month prior to study) who was randomized to treatment with rilzabrutinib. He continued concomitant treatment with prednisolone 10 \text{ mg} daily. On Day 21 the SAE of **wound infection (Grade 2)** occurred. The participant required hospitalization from received oral antibiotics. Day 20 (Week 4) platelet count was 40 \times 10^9/\text{L}. During hospitalization, the laboratory values included elevated C-reactive protein 87.4 \text{ mg/L}, persistently elevated leukocytes 14.2 \times 10^9/\text{L} (baseline value: 17 \text{ to } 48 \times 10^9/\text{L} [normal range: 3.9 \text{ to } 10.50 \times 10^9/\text{L}], normal range: 3.5 \text{ to } 11.0 \times 10^9/\text{L}), and platelet count at 33 \times 10^9/\text{L} (baseline value: 19 \times 10^9/\text{L}, normal range: 150 \text{ to } 370 \times 10^9/\text{L}). No action was taken with rilzabrutinib. The Investigator's and Applicant's assessments of causality was not related to rilzabrutinib. Based on the mechanism of action and known risk of infection associated with BTKIs, the review team did not agree and assessed the event as *at least possibly related* to rilzabrutinib.

- **Participant** (b) (6) is a 74-year-old white male with chronic ITP, diabetes mellitus, hepatic cirrhosis, esophageal varices status post bleeding episode, obesity, and hypertension, randomized to receive rilzabrutinib and continued weekly romiplostim and daily prednisone. Baseline liver function values (AST, ALT, and bilirubin) were normal.

On Day 29 he was admitted to the hospital for the SAEs of **esophageal variceal hemorrhage (Grade 3)** and **hepatic cirrhosis (Grade 3)**. His platelets at the time of hospital admission were 50 \times 10^9 \text{ K}/\mu\text{L}. He underwent lower variceal esophageal bleeding
L422: band ligation, was administered intravenous (IV) terlipressin, oral lactulose, IV ceftriaxone, and blood transfusion. No action was taken with rilzabrutinib. He was discharged from the hospital.

- On Day 49 he was re-admitted to the hospital with **recurrent esophageal variceal hemorrhage (Grade 4)**, hemorrhagic shock, a decrease in hemoglobin to 4 g/dL (baseline value: 12.5 g/dL), and SAE of **hepatic enzyme increased Grade 3** (ALT elevation [11x(upper limit of normal) ULN], AST elevation [11x ULN], and normal bilirubin). His platelet count on Day 42 was 56\times 10^9/L, Day 49 was 274\times 10^9/L. The only intervention was discontinuation of rilzabrutinib on Day 43.The Investigator's and Applicant's assessments of causality was these events were not related to rilzabrutinib.
- For the SAE of hepatic cirrhosis and hepatic enzyme increased, the review team agreed based on the participant's past medical history these events were *not related* to rilzabrutinib.
- For the SAE of esophageal variceal hemorrhage, the review team also considered the events as *unlikely to be related* to study drug given the history of esophageal variceal hemorrhage. However, given the association of other BTKIs with hemorrhage, the review team considered that rilzabrutinib could have contributed to the severity of the bleeding, although this is confounded by the thrombocytopenia at the time of the events.
- **Participant** (b) (6) is a 19-year-old Asian male with chronic ITP who was randomized to receive rilzabrutinib and continued concomitant prednisolone 20 mg daily. On Day 34, the participant had an SAE of **COVID-19 (Grade 3)**. He was admitted to the hospital. Platelet counts were 7\times 10^9/L (local lab, normal range: 140-450\times 10^9/L, baseline value: 3\times 10^9/L [local lab] and 15\times 10^9/L [central lab, normal range: 140-400\times 10^9/L). He received treatment with IV remdesivir daily for 4 days and platelet transfusion on Day 37. No action was taken with rilzabrutinib.
L423: The Investigator's and Applicant's assessments of causality for the event of COVID-19 was not related to rilzabrutinib. Based on the mechanism of action and known risk of infection associated with BTKi, the review team disagreed with the Applicant and assesses the event as *at least possibly related* to rilzabrutinib.
L424: ## **Open-Label and Long-Term Extension Periods**
L425: Serious adverse events occurring during the OL and the LTE periods are presented in Table 119. Notable SAEs not related to ITP nor observed during the DB period are: pulmonary embolism and deep vein thrombosis experienced during the open-label period for one participant ((b) (6)) and paresthesia experienced during the OL period and peripheral motor neuropathy experienced during the LTE period reported in one participant ((b) (6)), and peripheral sensory neuropathy reported in one participant ((b) (6)) during the LTE period.
L426: ## Narratives
L427: - **Participant** (b) (6) is a 23-year-old white female with chronic immune thrombocytopenia, ulcerative colitis, and vitamin D deficiency who was randomized to receive rilzabrutinib. She continued no concomitant medications for ITP.

On Day 243 the participant had a TEAE of sarcoidosis (Grade 1, nonserious) with no action with rilzabrutinib taken. On Day 256 the participant had a TEAE of facial paralysis (Grade 2) Oral methylprednisolone was administered and the participant recovered on Day 268. No action was taken with rilzabrutinib. On Day 274, the participant had a SAE of **paraesthesia (Grade 3)**, concerning for neurosarcoidosis and required hospitalization. The participant's platelet count was 226 (nl:150 to 400)x109/L. Electromyography and lumbar puncture were normal. No action was taken with rilzabrutinib. The participant was reported as recovered from the event on Day 282.

- On Day 365 the participant had the SAE of **peripheral motor neuropathy (Grade 2)** requiring hospitalization. Participant was treated with immunoglobulins (Days 367 to 371). The rilzabrutinib was interrupted due to the event and permanently discontinued on Day 367 due to participant's decision. The participant recovered from the peripheral motor neuropathy on Day 372 (28 days after the last dose of rilzabrutinib).

- The investigator and the Applicant assessed the SAEs as not related to rilzabrutinib. The review team agreed assessing the events as *not related* but more likely related to the diagnosis of sarcoidosis.

- **Participant** (b) (6) is a 61-year-old white Asian male with chronic immune thrombocytopenia, hypertension, and Cushingoid habitus who was randomized to receive placebo and continued eltrombopag and prednisolone. He withdrew from the double-blind period treatment at Week 13 due to Sponsor discretion and started rilzabrutinib during the OL period on Day 86 and continued in the LTE period from Day 310 of the study.

On Day 498 of rilzabrutinib, the participant had a TEAE of peripheral sensory neuropathy (Grade 2, nonserious). On Day 501 of rilzabrutinib, the **peripheral sensory neuropathy was considered serious** since it required hospitalization. Magnetic resonance imaging showed bilateral medial motor, sensory neuropathy, with compression at the wrist, endplate signal abnormality at C5-6. On Day 550 the participant underwent right hand/wrist carpal tunnel surgery. On day 607 peripheral sensory neuropathy (sensory neuropathy of bilateral hands) improved to Grade 1. No action was taken with the rilzabrutinib.

- The Investigator and the Applicant assessed the event as not related to rilzabrutinib. The review team agreed, assessing the neuropathy as more likely related to the diagnosis of carpal tunnel syndrome.

- **Participant** (b) (6) is a 36-year-old female with chronic ITP, overweight, hepatic steatosis, anemia, and hypophosphatemia who was randomized to treatment with rilzabrutinib and continued treatment with prednisone and avatrombopag. On Day 309 the participant experienced the SAEs of **pulmonary embolism (Grade 3)** and the next day **deep vein thrombosis (Grade 3)**. She was hospitalized. Imaging by ultrasound revealed deep vein thrombosis from the common femoral vein to the popliteal vein with occlusion of the femoral vein. Imaging by computed tomography and angiography of the chest revealed thrombi of the
L428: middle and bilateral lower lobar pulmonary arteries and distal branches. Her platelet count on Day 309 was 79\times 10^9/L (normal range: 150 to 400\times 10^9/L, baseline value: 3\times 10^9/L). Treatment included enoxaparin sodium and edoxaban tosilate monohydrate. No action was taken with the rilzabrutinib. The investigator's assessment of causality of deep vein thrombosis and pulmonary embolism was not related to rilzabrutinib and related to thrombopoietin receptor agonist. The Applicant's assessment of causality was not related to rilzabrutinib. The review team assessed the event as unlikely to be related to rilzabrutinib given the platelet count was only 79\times 10^9/L (nl: 150 to 400\times 10^9/L) and the higher risk of thrombosis with TPO-RAs.
L429: ## 7.6.1.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation and Interruptions, LUNA 3
L430: ## Double-Blind Treatment Period
L431: Frequency analysis of TEAEs leading to permanent discontinuation is shown in Table 24. Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation of rilzabrutinib during the DB treatment period occurred in eight (6%) participants, compared to no participant in the placebo arm. These included pneumonia (fatal), neutropenia, peripheral embolism, erythema nodosum, and multijoint pain, each occurring in one participant. Multiple TEAEs occurred in three additional patients leading to discontinuation of rilzabrutinib. They were diarrhea, nausea, abdominal discomfort; esophageal varices hemorrhage hepatic enzyme increased; and heartburn, headache, and leg pain. No TEAE at the PT level occurred in more than one participant. The most common AE leading to discontinuation at the SOC level was gastrointestinal disorders, cumulatively occurring in three (2.3%) participants.

L442: ## **Open-Label and Long-Term Extension Periods**
L443: During the OL and LTE periods, TEAEs leading to permanent discontinuation or treatment interruption were rare and were most frequently related to underlying disease (low platelets or bleeding) or infection.

During the LTE, one (2%) participant discontinued treatment due to pregnancy.
L445: During the OL period, eight (4.4%) participants experienced a TEAE that led to treatment interruption. There were thrombocytosis, COVID-19, petechiae, suspected sepsis, diarrhea, nausea, vomiting, pulmonary *Aspergillosis*, CMV viremia, and thrombocytosis. During the LTE, four (8%) participants experienced a TEAE that led to treatment interruption. These were: stomach pain, nausea, vomiting, peripheral motor neuropathy, and COVID-19.

L458: ## 7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Double-Blind Treatment Period, LUNA 3
L459: The protocol specified the routine monitoring of complete blood count, clinical chemistries, hepatic and renal evaluations. See Section 15.1.1.
L460: Overall, there were no meaningful clinical changes in mean value for hemoglobin, chemistry, and kidney laboratory assessments over time (see Section 17.2.4.1).
L461: Outlier analysis for cytopenia is discussed in Section 7.6.3.4. Outlier analysis for liver analytes is discussed in Section 7.6.3.2.
L462: ## General Chemistry
L463: Outlier analysis for general chemistry laboratory assessments is found in Table 125. Frequency of abnormalities in electrolyte and chemistries did not differ significantly between treatment groups, including those more commonly observed with BTK inhibitors, e.g., hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, hypophosphatemia.
L464: ## Renal Function
L465: Outlier analysis for laboratory assessments of renal function is shown in Table 126. For eGFR9, 7 participants out of 127 for whom there were baseline and post values (5.5%) in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to no participants in the placebo arm had a 25% to &lt;50% decrease.
L466: 9 Based on chronic kidney disease epidemiology collaboration (CKD-EPI) equation
L467: However, outlier analysis of increased creatinine identified only two out of 131 participants (1.5%) with Level 1 (\ge 1.5x and &lt;2xULN), compared to none in the placebo arm. In both participants, the increase in creatinine was transient, with values returning to baseline within reference range by the next assessment, with no change in rilzabrutinib dosing, and remained within reference range for several months of continued treatment with rilzabrutinib. Thus, the observed transient changes are unlikely to be related to rilzabrutinib.

L484: ## 7.6.3. Adverse Events of Special Interest and Adverse Events of Clinical Interest, LUNA 3
L485: The Applicant identified the following AESIs in LUNA 3.
L486: - Any Grade 4 or 5 infection where the patient is hospitalized \ge 24 hours and/or requires emergency care and/or requires intravenous antibiotics
- Increase in ALT &gt;3\times \text{ULN} (Grade 2)
- Pregnancy of a female participant entered in a study and received at least one dose of the IMP as well as pregnancy occurring in a female partner of a male participant entered in a study with IMP
- Symptomatic overdose with IMP
L487: Based on nonclinical studies with rilzabrutinib, clinical experience with other BTKIs, and patient population, the Agency considered the following additional adverse events to be adverse events of clinical interest: cardiac toxicity (particularly arrhythmias), cytopenias, new malignancy, hemorrhage, and gastrointestinal disorders (including diarrhea, nausea, vomiting), and thromboembolic events. These, along with hepatotoxicity and infection, are discussed individually below. The Division did not consider increase in ALT &gt;3\times \text{ULN} as a sufficient criterion to evaluate possible hepatotoxicity and performed a more comprehensive evaluation of hepatotoxicity, subsuming the Applicant's criterion. The Division did not agree with the Applicant's definition of AESI of infection and analyzed and reported here infections by seriousness and grade.
L488: ## 7.6.3.1. Infections
L489: ## Experience With Other BTKIs
L490: Increased risk of infections10 with use of the drug is included in Warnings and Precautions of all four approved BTKIs and include report of fatal infections occurring with three of the BTKIs. The rates of Grade 3 or greater or serious infections range from 21% to 32%. This is an on-target effect due to inhibition of BTK, which is crucial for the function of various immune cells (Wanget al. 2022).
L491: ## Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L492: In the DB period of LUNA 3, the frequency of infection overall is higher in the rilzabrutinib arm (33%) compared to the placebo arm (20%). The frequency of serious adverse events in the Infection and Infestation SOC was 3% in the rilzabrutinib arm, including one fatal event (0.8%), compared to no serious or fatal infections in the placebo arm. There were no Grade 4 infections on either arm. There were four Grade 3 events (3%) in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to no Grade 3 events in the placebo arm.
L493: Across all periods (DB, OLE, LTE) in LUNA 3, 101 (37%) of 198 participants had at least one TEAE in the Infection and Infestation SOC while receiving rilzabrutinib. There were no
L494: 10 For CTCAE grading of specific types of infection, in general, Grade 2 involves need for oral intervention e.g., antibiotic, antifungal, or antiviral; Grade 3 involves need for intravenous antimicrobial or invasive intervention, and Grade 4 involves life threatening consequence or need for urgent intervention.
L495: NDA 219685
WAYRILZ (rilzabrutinib)
L496: additional fatal infections beyond the double-blind period, resulting in an overall frequency across periods of 0.3%. A total of six (3%) participants had a serious infection (i.e., serious adverse event under the SOC of Infections and Infestations) while receiving rilzabrutinib in any period on LUNA 3. There were no Grade 4 events. The frequency of Grade 3 infections across the three periods was 4.5% (9/198).
L497: Frequency analysis of infections by preferred term is presented in Table 124.
L498: ## Conclusion
L499: As with other BTKIs, rilzabrutinib is associated with an increased risk of infection. The overall rate is approximately 1.5 times the rate seen with placebo. While rare, fatal and serious adverse events occurred in participants who received rilzabrutinib and were not observed in participants who received placebo. The frequency of serious infections was similar across all periods in participants receiving rilzabrutinib.
L500: Infections will be included in Warnings and Precautions as follows:
L501: An increased risk of serious infections (including bacterial, viral, or fungal) can occur in patients treated with Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) inhibitors, including WAYRILZ. In the LUNA-3 trial, fatal pneumonia occurred in one participant in the WAYRILZ group. Other serious infections [one each (0.8%)] included COVID-19 infection, wound infection, and one patient experienced urinary tract infection and kidney abscess. Monitor patients for signs and symptoms of infection and treat appropriately.
L502: ## 7.6.3.2. Hepatotoxicity and Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury
L503: ## Experience With Other BTKIs
L504: Risk of hepatotoxicity, including severe, life-threatening, and potentially fatal cases of DILI, has occurred in patients treated with each of the approved BTKIs and is included in Warnings and Precautions of the United States Prescribing Information (USPI) for each of them. Specific number of cases/rates of hepatotoxicity or DILI are not included in Section 5 of the USPIs for these products. The mechanism by which the other BTKIs cause hepatotoxicity is not well understood but may include direct drug toxicity, off-target effects, and immune suppression resulting in reactivation of viral hepatitis, particularly hepatitis B virus.
L505: ## Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L506: ## Laboratory Assessments
L507: Outlier analysis of liver analytes alkaline phosphatase (ALP), ALT, AST, and bilirubin is shown in Table 28. Overall, elevation of any of these analytes is rare, and there are no significant differences between the two arms. No participant on either arm had Level 3 observed for any analyte. In the rilzabrutinib arm, three (2.3%) of participants had elevation &gt;3x and ≤5xULN of ALT and one (0.8%) had elevation &gt;5x and ≤10xULN. This is not significantly different from placebo arm.
L508: In the OL period, one participant (0.6%) had at least one assessment of Grade 2 ALT increase. No participant had elevated AST or ALP. In the LTE, no participant had an abnormal liver assessment (AST, ALT, ALP, Bilirubin) \ge Grade 1.
L509: In LUNA 2, Part A, one participant had an abnormal ALT that went from within reference range at screening to 2.3xULN on Cycle 4, Day 1, which returned to within reference range during Cycle 5 but was mildly elevated during Cycle 6 (max 1.6xULN). The same participant had similar changes with AST.

L530: ## Potential DILI Cases
L531: - **Participant** (b) (6) was identified based on laboratory values as a possible case of DILI. Full narrative for this participant who had a past medical history of hepatic cirrhosis and esophageal variceal hemorrhage and had recurrent esophageal variceal hemorrhage at the time of the liver transaminase elevation is presented in Section 7.6.1.3. The participant had AST, ALT, and total bilirubin values within reference range at baseline. Post baseline, on Day 49, the participant had an ALT increase &gt;10\times\text{ULN}, AST &gt;8\times\text{ULN}, with alkaline phosphatase above ULN and bilirubin within reference range. The participant discontinued rilzabrutinib. On Day 52, ALT remained elevated (&gt;3\times\text{ULN}) along with elevated total bilirubin (&gt;3\times\text{ULN}) and alkaline phosphatase above ULN. Given the past medical history and concurrent events of liver cirrhosis and esophageal variceal hemorrhage, the review team concludes this case does not meet criteria for DILI.
L532: ## Temples Corollary Cases
L533: - **Participant** (b) (6) is a 24-year-old Hispanic/Latino female with history of chronic ITP, epilepsy, increased gamma-glutamyl transferase (ongoing for 2 years), increased ALT, increased AST (both reported normal 30 days prior to study start), intermenstrual bleeding and anemia ongoing for 4.5 months prior to start of study, who was randomized to receive rilzabrutinib and continued concomitant eltrombopag and prednisone. On Day 10 and 11 of the study, intermenstrual bleeding, pelvic inflammatory disease, fever (Grade 3), and vaginal hemorrhage (Grade 3 SAE) were reported, and the participant was hospitalized and treated with ceftriaxone, clindamycin, IV hydrocortisone, oral ethinylestradiol/levonorgestrel, cimetidine, hyoscine butyl bromide, oral dexchlorpheniramine maleate, platelet transfusion, and red blood cell transfusion. Cyclosporine was started on Day 15 for platelet count rescue. On Day 20, the participant had increased ALT (Grade 2) of 113 IU/L (local lab, normal range: 10 to 37 IU/L), increased AST (Grade 1) at 55.5 IU/L (local lab, normal range: 10 to 37 IU/L). ALP and bilirubin values were not reported. On Day 27 the ALT increase improved to Grade 1. The participant experienced intermittent worsening of anemia to Grade 3, followed by gastroenteritis (Grade 2) and ALT increased to Grade 2 on Day 55. On No action was taken with the rilzabrutinib due to the elevated ALT.
L534: During the open-label treatment period, on Day 165 of the study, an AE of increased ALT (Grade 3) was reported. No action was taken with the rilzabrutinib. The elevated ALT was attributed to cyclosporine given as rescue medication, carbamazepine and phenytoin. The increase in ALT was not attributed to rilzabrutinib by the Investigator. The review team agreed that the ALT increase was likely attributable to the medications administered for the anemia, seizure prophylaxis, and intercurrent illness, and rilzabrutinib is unlikely to have been the cause of these events. The team concludes the case does not meet criteria for DILI.
L535: - **Participant** (b) (6) is a 19-year-old male with history of chronic ITP who was randomized to rilzabrutinib arm. On study day 57, he had an isolated elevation of AST to 3.4 ULN, AST to 1.1xULN, with normal (baseline) bilirubin. No action was taken with the rilzabrutinib. These events occurred just after an upper respiratory infection for which he received oseltamivir from Day 47 to Day 57. On day 85, all liver assessments had returned to baseline (within reference range). There were no subsequent abnormal liver assessments reported. The review team concludes given the transience of the abnormal liver laboratory
L536: assessments and resolution without change to rilzabrutinib, the study drug did not cause the liver abnormalities. The case does not meet criteria for DILI.
L537: - **Participant** (b) (6) is a 21-year-old Asian male with history of chronic ITP who was randomized to rilzabrutinib arm and continued oral prednisolone and eltrombopag. He experienced elevations of ALT and AST on Day 36 with an ALT of 121 IU/L (normal range: 10 to 42 IU/L, baseline value: 34 IU/L [normal range: 10 to 53 IU/L]), AST at 44 IU/L (normal range: 13 to 30 IU/L, baseline value: 18 IU/L [normal range: 14 to 43 IU/L]), with normal ALP and bilirubin. Interventions to improve transaminase levels included: discontinuation of eltrombopag, famotidine, IV cysteine/ glycine/ glycyrrhizic acid, oral dL-methionine/ glycine/ glycyrrhizic acid, oral ammonium salt, and glutathione. On Day 40 an AE of hepatic steatosis (Grade 1) was diagnosed by ultrasound and computed tomography of the abdomen and ALT peaked at 150 IU/L (normal range 10 to 42 IU/L) (Grade 2) and 130 IU/L (normal range: 10 to 53 IU/L) and AST 49 IU/L (normal range: 13 to 30 IU/L) and 44 IU/L (normal range: 14 to 43 IU/L). The participant recovered from abnormalities of liver analytes on Day 92. Hepatic steatosis did not resolve. No action was taken with the rilzabrutinib for the abnormal transaminases. The Investigator's assessment of causality for abnormal hepatic function was not related to rilzabrutinib. The review team agrees given the fluctuations of the abnormal transaminases with ultimate return to baseline despite no change to rilzabrutinib dosing. The case does not meet criteria for DILI.
L538: Analysis of adverse event reporting identified 2 participants, both in the rilzabrutinib arm, with TEAEs in the AEHLGT Hepatic and hepatobiliary disorders during the DB period, participant (b) (6) and participant (b) (6). Full narratives for these patients are provided above. No additional TEAEs under the AEHLGT Hepatic and hepatobiliary disorders occurred during the OL or LTE periods.
L539: In LUNA 2 Study, no additional TEAEs under the AEHLGT Hepatic and hepatobiliary disorders were reported.
L540: ## **Conclusion**
L541: Hepatotoxicity and DILI has been reported with use of other BTKIs in clinical trials and is included in Warnings and Precautions for all of them. No cases of DILI were identified in participants who received rilzabrutinib on LUNA 3 or LUNA 2. There does not appear to be a significant signal of hepatotoxicity in participants who received rilzabrutinib on LUNA 3 or LUNA 2. Increases in liver analytes during the DB period of LUNA 3 are rare, with equal frequency between arms. Because hepatotoxicity is identified in all other BTKIs, is potentially an on-target effect, and the safety population for rilzabrutinib is small, hepatotoxicity will be included in Warnings and Precautions class labeling as follows:
L542: Hepatotoxicity, including severe, life-threatening, and potentially fatal cases of DILI, can occur in patients treated with BTK inhibitors. In the clinical trials of WAYRILZ in patients with ITP, elevations of liver transaminases occurred and were generally mild to moderate in severity. Evaluate bilirubin and transaminases at baseline and as clinically indicated during treatment with WAYRILZ. For patients who develop abnormal liver tests after WAYRILZ, monitor more frequently for liver test abnormalities and clinical signs and symptoms of hepatic toxicity. If DILI is suspected, withhold WAYRILZ. Upon confirmation of DILI, discontinue WAYRILZ.
L543: ## 7.6.3.3. Cardiac Arrhythmias
L544: ## Experience With Other BTKIs
L545: Risk of cardiac arrhythmias occurring with the use of the drug is included in Warnings and Precautions for all approved BTKIs. The rate of Grade 3 or 4 atrial arrhythmia (atrial fibrillation or flutter) ranged from 1.5% to 3.7% (all grades reported in some USPIs ranged from 3.2% to 7%). The rate of Grade 3 or 4 ventricular arrhythmias reported in some USPIs ranged from 0.2% to 0.6%. Deaths or sudden deaths was reported in Warnings and Precautions for one BTKI and was 1% with Grade \ge3 cardiac failure of 1.2%. However, cardiac deaths and failures were not included in the USPI for 3 of the 4 approved BTKIs. Cardiac arrhythmias are a primarily an off-target effect (McMullen et al. 2014). They are not a known complication of conditions associated with absence or non-functional BTK (Kanegane et al. 2025).
L546: ## Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L547: ## Thorough QT Study
L548: The Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies reviewed the Applicant's QT evaluation report11. Based on the report, there were no cardiac related serious AEs or AEs that resulted in rilzabrutinib discontinuation. None of the events identified to be of clinical importance per the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) E14 guidelines (May 2024) (i.e., seizure, significant ventricular arrhythmias, or sudden cardiac death) occurred in this study. The thorough QT (TQT) study demonstrated that rilzabrutinib was observed to shorten the QTc interval without changes in PR or QRS. This pattern of electrocardiogram (ECG) changes is consistent with mexiletine, a Vaughan-Williams IB sodium channel blocker. If rilzabrutinib is a sodium channel blocker, then it could be associated with an increased proarrhythmic risk in patients with structural heart disease. Because the Applicant has not conducted experiments to elucidate the mechanism of the observed QTc shortening, it is not possible to rule out that rilzabrutinib is a sodium channel blocker.
L549: Final labeling to Section 12.2 Cardiac Electrophysiology states:
L550: In a Thorough QT study, concentration dependent shortening in the QTc interval was observed. Following a single dose of rilzabrutinib 400 mg, the mean maximum QTcF decrease of -7 msec (90% confidence interval: -9 msec to -5 msec) was observed at 2 hours post-dose. The mean maximum QTcF decrease at exposures 4-fold the highest recommended dose, i.e., 400 mg BID, was -10 msec (90% confidence interval: -12 msec to -8 msec) at 2 hours post-dose.
L551: ## Clinical Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L552: During the DB period of LUNA 3, two participants in the rilzabrutinib arm (1.5%) and two participants in the placebo arm (2.8%) had a TEAE with the AEHLGT Cardiac arrhythmias or cardiac disorders, signs and symptoms NEC. TEAEs in the rilzabrutinib arm were cardiac arrhythmias (PT Arrhythmia (Grade 1) in one participant and PTs extrasystoles [Grade 1] and
L553: 11 Report included the previous integrated review under investigational new drug (IND) 132668 dated 04/05/2018, study report PR1008-014, study protocol PR1008-014, cardiac safety report – last appendix in SAP (NDA 210695), the Applicant's Proposed Label (NDA 219685) and highlights of clinical pharmacology and cardiac safety.
L554: NDA 219685
WAYRILZ (rilzabrutinib)
L555: tachycardia [Grade 2] in the other). In the placebo arm, one participant had PT of bundle branch block right (Grade 1) and another had PT of palpitations (Grade 1).
L556: During LUNA 2 Part A, there was one (2.2%) participant in the 400 mg BID group who had a TEAE of heart rate irregular (Grade 1). During LUNA 2 Part B, there was one (3.8%) participant in the 400 m BID group who had TEAE of palpitations during the main treatment group (Grade 1).
L557: The Applicant reported in the ITP rilzabrutinib pool (N=284), which included LUNA 2 and LUNA 3 (across all treatment periods), there was one case of syncope (Grade 3) and there were no cases of Torsade des pointes, sudden death, ventricular tachycardia, ventricular fibrillation, ventricular flutter, or seizure. Our review of the AE data confirmed this.
L558: Per the Applicant, two (0.7%) participants had abnormal clinically significant ECG results. One participant had atrial fibrillation (during the LTE period of LUNA 2 Part B).
L559: ## Conclusion
L560: Cardiac arrhythmias are reported in Warnings and Precautions for all approved BTKIs with a frequency of Grade 3 or 4 events ranging from 1.5% to 3.7%. No Grade 3 or 4 events were reported in LUNA 2 or LUNA 3. The low-grade events of arrhythmias reported in the DB part of LUNA 3 occurred more frequently in the placebo arm (2.8%) compared to the rilzabrutinib arm (1.5%). Cardiac arrhythmias associated with approved BTKIs are an off-target effect and may be related to the approved populations (older patients with B-cell malignancies) or the non-selectivity of those BTKIs. Given the differences in patient populations and the higher degree of selectivity of rilzabrutinib, the review team does not recommend inclusion of increased risk of cardiac arrhythmias in Warnings and Precautions for rilzabrutinib in ITP. Given the lower frequency of events in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to placebo, inclusion in Section 6 is also not recommended.
L561: ## 7.6.3.4. Cytopenias
L562: ## Experience With Other BTKIs
L563: Cytopenias are included in Warnings and Precautions for all four of the approved BTKIs. Grade 3 or 4 neutropenia and thrombocytopenia is noted in all 4 of the BTKIs. Anemia is noted in 3 of them. The rate of Grade 3 or 4 neutropenia ranged from 21% to 26% with frequency of Grade 4 neutropenia of 14% noted in one USPI. The rate of Grade 3 or 4 anemia (hemoglobin decreased) ranged from 8% to 12%. The mechanism by which the approved BTKIs can cause neutropenia is not fully understood and may include both on-target effect on BTK in myeloid-cells and off-target kinase inhibition (Fiedler et al. 2011). The mechanism by which the approved BTKIs can cause anemia is not clear.
L564: ## Clinical Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L565: Outlier analysis for key hematology analytes for DB period of LUNA 3 is shown in Table 127. For all analysis of all hematology and coagulation laboratory findings, see Table 127.
L566: Overall, the frequency of low white count (Level 1, &lt;3.5 \times 10^3 cells/\mul) is higher in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to the placebo arm, 7.6% compared to 4.4%, respectively. This is all
L567: due to higher frequency of Level 2, WBC &lt;3\times 10^3 cells/\muL rilzabrutinib (4.5%) in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to 0% in the placebo arm. The frequency of low lymphocytes is similar between the arms for levels 1 and 2, but a slightly higher percentage of participants in the rilzabrutinib arm with Level 3 (&lt;0.5\times 10^3 cells/\muL).
L568: The frequency of low neutrophils (&lt;2\times 10^3 cells/\muL) is greater in the rilzabrutinib arm (11%) compared to placebo (4.4%); however, there are no significant differences between the arms at more severe levels of neutropenia. These differences in frequency of neutropenia are small and may be due to chance given the small sample size.
L569: There are no significant differences in frequency of anemia between the two arms.

L578: ## Conclusion
L579: Based on laboratory data from the DB period of LUNA 3, there does not appear to be a safety signal for anemia associated with rilzabrutinib. There does appear to be a safety signal for mild neutropenia associated with rilzabrutinib; however, the 95% CI for the risk difference for Level 1 does cross 0, indicating the observed difference may be due to chance. Although cytopenias, specifically anemia and neutropenia, are noted in Warnings and Precautions of other BTKIs, the risk varies with more favorable profiles in the more selective BTKIs. The risk of cytopenias with these approved BTKIs may also be related in part to the approved populations.
L580: Because the risk of anemia was not observed in clinical trials in ITP with rilzabrutinib and frequency observed with the approved BTKIs appears related to selectivity of the BTKI, the review team recommends against including anemia as a class Warnings and Precautions.
L581: Similarly, the review team concluded that neutropenia should not be included as a class warning in Section 5 of the USPI; however, because there was a signal of mild neutropenia with a higher rate observed in the rilzabrutinib arm, the review team recommends inclusion of the rate of neutropenia in Section 6 of the USPI.
L582: ## 7.6.3.5. New Malignancy
L583: ## Experience With Other BTKIs
L584: Malignancies (second primary) are reported in Warnings and Precautions for all 4 approved BTKIs. Frequency ranges from 9% to 18%, including nonskin and skin cancers. The mechanism by which BTKIs increases risk of new malignancy includes on and off-target effects specifically and include on-target immunomodulatory effects and off-target inhibition of other kinases (Wang et al. 2022).
L585: ## Clinical Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L586: In the ITP rilzabrutinib pool (N=284), which includes LUNA 2 (all doses) and LUNA 3 (all treatment periods), six participants had a report of a primary neoplasm specified as malignant or not specified (2%). They are summarized in Table 31. Half were of these were skin neoplasms. Several were either unspecified as malignancy and/or could be associated with benign conditions.
L587: **Table 31. New Primary Malignancies in Participants Receiving Rilzabrutinib1**

L592: The Exposure-Adjusted Incidence Rate (EAIR) per 100 participant-years for treatment-emergent malignancy was 1.64 overall12 in the ITP rilzabrutinib pool.
L593: ## Conclusion
L594: New malignancies were rare in participants receiving rilzabrutinib on clinical trials.
L595: In the 6-month study in mice and 2-year carcinogenicity study, rilzabrutinib was not carcinogenic. The frequency of new (primary) malignancy associated with approved BTKIs is reported in Warnings and Precautions of the USPIs is much higher than observed with
L596: 12 Applicant Submission, NDA 219685, SDN 1, Summary of Clinical Safety, Section 3.1.5.4.4.4
L597: rilzabrutinib in participants with ITP. This likely reflects the treated population, including age and prior therapies, as well as the effect of the earlier BTKI's on T-cell and NK-function, impairing tumor surveillance. Because rilzabrutinib is more selective and does not impact T-cell and NK-function and the EAIR for new malignancy in the rilzabrutinib pool is consistent with that reported in the literature in the ITP population of 1.8 per 100 patient-years (Ekstrand et al. 2020), the review team does not recommend inclusion of an increased risk of malignancy in the USPI for rilzabrutinib as a Warnings and Precautions or in Section 6.
L598: ## 7.6.3.6. Hemorrhage
L599: ## Experience With Other BTKIs
L600: The risk of hemorrhage is included Warnings and Precautions of all 4 approved BTKIs and includes fatal and serious or Grade \ge 3 events. The rate of major (serious or Grade 3 or higher and any central nervous system (CNS) bleeding) ranged from 3% to 4.4%. The rate of fatal hemorrhage was 0.2 to 0.4%. Bleeding of any grade excluding bruising and petechiae ranged from 17% to 40%. The underlying mechanism of action of the hemorrhage observed with BTKIs is impaired platelet function, including platelet aggregation and thrombus formation.
L601: ## Clinical Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L602: Based on OCMQ (Narrow) analysis of TEAE reporting during the DB period of LUNA 3, the frequency of hemorrhage was 5% in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to 9% in the placebo arm. The frequency of SAE of hemorrhage was 3% in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to 6% in the placebo arm.
L603: ## Conclusion
L604: In LUNA 3, the rate of hemorrhage TEAEs and SAEs was higher in the placebo arm compared to the rilzabrutinib arm. Analysis of hemorrhage related to a drug treating ITP is confounded by the underlying disease, which would predispose to bleeding related to low platelets, and to treatment effect of the drug under investigation, which would potentially decrease bleeding in participants who have a response. The review team recommends not including hemorrhage as a risk associated with rilzabrutinib.
L605: ## 7.6.3.7. Hypertension
L606: ## Experience With Other BTKIs
L607: Hypertension is included in the Warnings and Precautions for only one of the approved BTKIs (nonreversible). The rate of Grade \ge 3 HTN was 8% with mean onset of 5.9 months. The mechanism for the increased risk of hypertension observed with the approved BTKIs is not clear but is likely due to off-target effects.
L608: ## Clinical Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L609: Based on TEAE reporting and OCMQ (narrow) analysis, there were no cases of systemic hypertension in participants in the rilzabrutinib arm, compared to 3 participants (4%) in the placebo arm during the DB period of LUNA 3.
L610: In the ITP rilzabrutinib pool, 6 participants (2%) had a TEAE with the preferred term of hypertension. Three of these participants (1%) had Grade 3 hypertension.
L611: ## Conclusion
L612: Only one of the approved BTKIs includes hypertension in Warnings and Precautions, indicating it is not a class effect. Hypertension was rarely reported in participants receiving rilzabrutinib on clinical trials, and in the DB of LUNA 3, the frequency was not greater in the rilzabrutinib arm. The review team recommends not including hypertension in Warnings and Precautions or Section 6 (Adverse Reactions) of the USPI for rilzabrutinib.
L613: ## 7.6.3.8. Thromboembolic Events
L614: ## Experience With Other BTKIs
L615: Thrombosis/thromboembolic events are not included in Warnings and Precautions of other BTKIs
L616: ## Clinical Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L617: Thromboembolic events are identified by the Applicant as a significant adverse event of interest. Thromboembolic events can be seen in patients with ITP. During the DB period in LUNA 3, one participant in the rilzabrutinib arm had a thromboembolic event of Grade 3 peripheral embolism. The event was serious and was assessed based on narrative (see Section 7.6.1.3) as not related to rilzabrutinib. No participant in the placebo arm had thromboembolic TEAE.
L618: During the OL period of LUNA 3, one participant had SAEs of pulmonary embolism and deep vein thrombosis. Narrative for this participant (b) (6) is presented in Section 7.6.1.3. These events were also considered unlikely to be related to rilzabrutinib.
L619: One participant in the LTE of LUNA 2, Part A, experienced a thromboembolic event of pulmonary embolism. Based on narrative (see below), the event was considered at least possibly related to rilzabrutinib by the review team. No participant experienced thrombosis during PRN 1008-010 Part B.
L620: - Participant (b) (6) is a 46-year-old female with chronic ITP, depression, gastroesophageal reflux disease, migraine and thyroiditis randomized to receive rilzabrutinib. On Day 434 of rilzabrutinib, the participant developed a severe migraine. On Day 435 thrombocytosis (1099 x109/L) was reported. On Day 441, SAEs of **pulmonary embolism (Grade 3) and pneumonia** (Grade 3) were reported and required hospitalization. The participant was treated with apixaban and broad-spectrum antimicrobials. Rilzabrutinib was permanently discontinued and last received on Day 434. Thrombocytosis had improved by Day 449 (567 x109/L). Platelet count had further decreased to 27 x109/L by Day 463.
L621: Prior to Day 435, platelet evaluations (n=50) after initiation of rilzabrutinib on day 1 ranged from 2 X 109/L to 282 X 109/L. The maximum platelet count recorded in the 100 days prior to day 419 was 59 X 109/L. The last platelet evaluation before thrombocytosis was observed on day 434 was a platelet count of 6 X 109/L recorded on Day 419.
L622: Review of concomitant medications indicates the participant received rescue medication with prednisone from day 406 to day 442 and avatrombopag from day 422 to day 434.
L623: The Investigator's and Sponsor's assessments of attribution for the events of migraine, thrombocytosis, pulmonary embolism, and pneumonia were not related to rilzabrutinib. The Division assessed the SAE of pneumonia as at least possibly related to rilzabrutinib given the known increased risk of infection associated with BTKIs, including rilzabrutinib; however, assessed the pulmonary embolism as not related. Although rilzabrutinib does increase platelet count, the mechanism is through decreased destruction rather than increased production, and therefore it is unlikely rilzabrutinib would lead to thrombocytosis. In contrast, thrombocytosis is a known risk of oral thrombopoietin receptor agonists, including avatrombopag. Steroids can also increase production of platelets. Based on rilzabrutinib's mechanism of action, the participant's historic response to rilzabrutinib, and concomitant use of avatrombopag and steroid just prior to the thrombocytosis and pulmonary embolism, the review team assessed the SAE of pulmonary embolism as not related to rilzabrutinib.
L624: ## Conclusion
L625: Thromboembolic events were rare in participants receiving rilzabrutinib, and those observed were considered unlikely or not related to rilzabrutinib. The review team does not recommend inclusion of thromboembolic events in the USPI.
L626: ## 7.6.3.9. Gastrointestinal Toxicity
L627: ## Experience With Other BTKIs
L628: Gastrointestinal toxicities are not included in Warnings and Precautions of any of the approved BTKIs, however, are included in Section 6 of the USPI for all approved BTKIs. The gastrointestinal disorders of diarrhea, vomiting, nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, and constipation occurred at a rate of \ge 10\% in at least one clinical trial reported in the USPI for each of the approved BTKIs. The mechanism by which BTKIs cause gastrointestinal disorders is due to on-target and off-target effects.
L629: ## Clinical Experience With Rilzabrutinib
L630: Gastrointestinal disorders were the most common TEAE by SOC occurring in 53% of participants in the rilzabrutinib arm compared to 23% in the placebo arm during the DB period of LUNA 3. TEAS for all of the most frequent (\ge 10\%) specific gastrointestinal disorders (Based on OCMQ (Narrow) during the DB period occurred more frequently in the rilzabrutinib arm: diarrhea (32% rilzabrutinib, 9% placebo), nausea (20% rilzabrutinib, 6% placebo), abdominal pain (14% rilzabrutinib, 3.5% placebo), and dyspepsia (11% rilzabrutinib, 0.9% placebo). All events were Grade 1 or 2. Of those who experienced GI adverse reactions, 2 participants discontinued due to GI adverse reactions. Recovery or resolution with supportive treatment allowed continuation of treatment with rilzabrutinib occurred in 91% of patients with diarrhea, 85% with nausea and 79% with abdominal pain.
L631: ## Conclusion
L632: Gastrointestinal disorders were common and consistent with those observed with other BTKIs. The specific gastrointestinal disorders are included in the table of common adverse reactions in Section 6 of the USPI. While frequent, they rarely resulted in permanent discontinuation and
L633: could be managed with supportive care. Thus, inclusion in Warnings and Precautions is not warranted.
L634: ## 7.6.4. Integrated Safety Population Assessment
L635: To identify rare but potentially serious AEs that may be detectable only in a larger dataset, an analysis of the ISP was performed, (see Section 17.1). The analysis did not identify any new or more severe safety signal not identified in the analysis of the DB period of LUNA 2. There were no reported cases of anaphylaxis, Stevens Johnson Syndrome, Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS), toxic epidermal necrolysis, allergic edema, toxic skin eruption, or rhabdomyolysis. The frequency analysis based on PT and using OND Custom Medical Query (Narrow) did not identify any new, more frequent, or more serious safety signals not identified with the analysis of LUNA 3 DB period.
L636: ## 7.6.5. Adverse Reactions
L637: TEAEs were considered adverse reactions if they occurred in \ge 5 of participants and occurred in at least 3% more in the rilzabrutinib arm than the placebo arm. Most common adverse reactions are shown in Table 32.
L638: An additional rare adverse reaction identified through analysis of AESIs and adverse events of clinical interest (see Section 7.6.3) is neutropenia.

L644: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues

L646: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L647: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L648: ## 8.1.1. Hepatic Impairment
L649: A dedicated hepatic impairment study was conducted where participants with normal hepatic function, mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class A) and moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class B) were given a single 400-mg dose of rilzabrutinib (Study PRN1008-020). Results (Table 33) show that moderate hepatic impairment (HI) significantly increased systemic exposure of rilzabrutinib. The mean C_{max} and AUC_{last} values of total rilzabrutinib in the moderate hepatic impairment group were approximately 4.2- to 4.8-fold higher than those of the normal group. Rilzabrutinib was highly bound to plasma proteins across all cohorts. The mean C_{max} and AUC of unbound rilzabrutinib were similar in subjects with mild HI compared to healthy-matched control subjects, and approximately 4.8- to 5.5-fold higher in subjects with moderate HI compared to healthy-matched control subjects, consistent with the observations for total rilzabrutinib. The effect of severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class C) on the pharmacokinetics of rilzabrutinib was not evaluated.
L650: **Table 33. Summary of Statistical Comparisons of Plasma Total Rilzabrutinib PK Parameters Following Administration of 400 mg Rilzabrutinib in Participants With HI vs. Healthy-Matched Control Participants (PK Analysis Population)**

L654: Based on the results observed, the impact of moderate hepatic impairment on the PK of rilzabrutinib is significant. The review team agrees with the Applicant's proposal that rilzabrutinib should not be administered in patients with moderate and severe hepatic impairment.
L655: ## 8.1.2. Renal Impairment
L656: A dedicated renal impairment (RI) study was not conducted. Urinary excretion of rilzabrutinib as unchanged drug is low (fractional excretion =0.03%). Therefore, population PK (PopPK) and physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling were used to assess the effect of renal impairment. Mild to moderate renal function was assessed as a covariate in PopPK analysis, while the impact of moderate to severe renal impairment was assessed via PBPK. Based on Agency's review, the renal impairment model used in the submitted PBPK analyses is not adequately validated to predict the effects of renal impairment on rilzabrutinib exposure. In the model validation, for drugs whose exposure increased more than 50%, the effects of renal impairment on their exposure tended to be underpredicted (see detail in Section 14.5.3.4.4).
L657: PopPK analysis confirmed that mild to moderate renal function had no effect on the pharmacokinetics of rilzabrutinib (Study POH01157). Normal renal function (creatinine clearance (CLCr) \ge 90 mL/min) represented 76.7% of subjects in the dataset, with mild (CLCr between 60 to &lt;90 mL/min) and moderate (CLCr 30 to &lt;60 mL/min) renal impairment representing 19.7% and 3.5% of subjects, respectively, in the dataset. The lowest CLCr was 46 mL/min in the moderate renal impairment group. Based on pharmacometrics review, CLCr is not a significant covariate that would affect rilzabrutinib exposure (Figure 9).
L658: Figure 9. Box Plot of Rilzabrutinib PK Exposures Grouped by Renal Function
L659: Source: Reviewer analysis
L660: Abbreviations: AUCss, area under the concentration-time curve from 0 to 24 hours on the last day of exposure; CLCRG, creatinine clearance group; CMAXSS, maximum plasma concentration at steady state, n, number of subjects within renal function group; PK, pharmacokinetic
L661: The lack of exposure changes in mild and moderate renal impairment groups is also corroborated by the mass balance study (PRN1008-015) results which show only 5% of the administered dose excreted in urine, with unchanged rilzabrutinib only in trace amounts. However, no definitive conclusions about the impact of severe renal impairment can be drawn. It is known that inhibition of hepatic function by uremic toxins is a plausible mechanism to increase exposure in severe renal impairment. For rilzabrutinib, hepatic metabolism plays a significant role, as exposures in moderate hepatic impairment increases by 4-to-5 fold.
L662: The review team agrees with the Applicant's proposal that a dose adjustment is not needed for patients with mild or moderate RI. Patients with severe renal impairment have not been studied. As the potential for increased exposures cannot be ruled out in subjects with severe renal impairment; the review team recommends avoiding the use of rilzabrutinib in such patients. Further, a PMR will be issued for a study to evaluate the effect of severe renal impairment on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics.
L663: ## 8.1.3. Other Intrinsic Factors
L664: The covariate effect analyses in the PopPK model indicated that weight, age, race, and sex did not have a significant effect on the pharmacokinetics of rilzabrutinib. Hence, dose adjustment is not needed for these intrinsic factors.
L665: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L666: ## 8.2.1. Drug Interactions
L667: ## 8.2.1.1. Metabolic Pathway
L668: Rilzabrutinib is predominantly metabolized by cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4, with minor contributions from CYP2D6, in vitro. Drugs that are inhibitors or inducers of CYP3A have the potential to affect rilzabrutinib exposures. The Applicant conducted clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies to evaluate the impact of a strong CYP3A inhibitor and a strong CYP3A inducer on the pharmacokinetics of rilzabrutinib. The effects of strong, moderate, and weak CYP3A inhibitors as well as strong, moderate, and weak CYP3A inducers were evaluated by PBPK modeling and simulations.
L669: ## 8.2.1.2. Effects of Other Drugs on Rilzabrutinib
L670: ## 8.2.1.2.1. Effects of CYP3A4 Inhibitors on Rilzabrutinib
L671: Ritonavir, a strong CYP3A inhibitor, was dosed (100 mg, 2 doses) and its effect was evaluated on single doses of 100 mg and 400 mg rilzabrutinib. Ritonavir increased the AUC and C_{max} of 100 mg rilzabrutinib by approximately 18- and 8-fold, whereas increased the AUC and C_{max} of 400 mg rilzabrutinib by approximately 8- and 5-fold. The effects of ritonavir, three moderate CYP3A inhibitors, fluconazole, verapamil, and diltiazem, and one weak CYP3A inhibitor, cimetidine, were also evaluated using PBPK modeling and simulations (see Section 14.5.3.4.3 for details).
L672: PBPK simulations showed that ritonavir increased rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} by 7.3- and 6.3-fold, respectively, following a single rilzabrutinib dose of 400 mg. Fluconazole increased rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} by 3.1- and 3.3-fold, respectively, following a single rilzabrutinib dose of 400 mg. Verapamil increased rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} by 2.8- and 3-fold, respectively, following a single rilzabrutinib dose of 400 mg. Diltiazem increased both rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} by 1.5-fold, following a single rilzabrutinib dose of 400 mg. Cimetidine increased rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} by 1.4- and 1.6-fold, respectively, following a single rilzabrutinib dose of 400 mg.
L673: The review team agrees with the Applicant that the use of rilzabrutinib with strong and moderate CYP3A inhibitors should be avoided due to the large increase in rilzabrutinib exposure. For use with weak CYP3A inhibitors, the team does not propose any dose adjustment.
L674: ## 8.2.1.2.2. Effects of CYP3A4 Inducers on Rilzabrutinib
L675: Rifampin, a strong CYP3A inducer, was dosed (600 mg QD for 11 days) and its effect was evaluated on single dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib. Rifampin decreased the AUC and C_{max} of rilzabrutinib by 77% and 80%, respectively, similar to the simulated results from using PBPK modeling. The effects of a strong CYP3A inducer, carbamazepine, two moderate CYP3A inducers, efavirenz and rifabutin, and one weak CYP3A inducer, dexamethasone, were also evaluated using PBPK modeling and simulations (see Section 14.5.3.4.3 for details).
L676: Based on the PBPK simulations, carbamazepine decreased rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} by approximately 75% and 81%, respectively, following a single rilzabrutinib dosage of 400 mg. Efavirenz, a moderate CYP3A inducer, decreased both rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} by approximately 70%, following a single rilzabrutinib dosage of 400 mg. Rifabutin decreased rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} by approximately 60% and 66%, respectively, following a single rilzabrutinib dosage of 400 mg. Dexamethasone, a weak CYP3A inducer, decreased both rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} by approximately 30%, following a single rilzabrutinib dosage of 400 mg.
L677: Based on E-R analysis, 30% rilzabrutinib exposure decrease would lead to a reduction in platelet count response by approximately 15%, compared to no change in rilzabrutinib exposure. This degree of change is not likely clinically significant.
L678: The review team agrees with the Applicant that the use of rilzabrutinib with strong and moderate CYP3A inducers should be avoided due to the large decrease in rilzabrutinib exposure. The team agrees with the Applicant that the use of rilzabrutinib with weak CYP3A inducers are allowed without additional instructions or dose-adjustment (see Section 14.2.11 for details).
L679: ## 8.2.1.2.3. Effect of Acid Reducing Agents on Rilzabrutinib
L680: Rilzabrutinib has pH-dependent solubility in the relevant physiological pH range. The solubility of rilzabrutinib is &gt;120 mg/mL at pH values less than 4, however, decreases significantly to 0.35 mg/mL at pH 4.5. Thus, rilzabrutinib is likely to have interactions with acid reducing agents (ARAs).
L681: Esomeprazole, a proton pump inhibitor (PPI), was dosed (40 mg QD, 5 doses) and its effect was evaluated on single dose of 300 mg rilzabrutinib. Administering esomeprazole two hours before rilzabrutinib decreased both its AUC and C_{max} by approximately 50%. Famotidine, an H2 receptor antagonist (H2RA), was dosed (20 mg QD, 3 evening doses) and its effect was evaluated on the administration of 400 mg rilzabrutinib on Day 3 (10 hours after the famotidine dose on Day 2), as well as on the administration of evening dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib on Day 3 (2 hours before the famotidine dose on Day 3). Dosing rilzabrutinib in the morning following administration of 20 mg famotidine the previous evening, resulted in a reduction in the rilzabrutinib AUC_{inf} and C_{max} by 35.3% and 27.7%, respectively. However, when rilzabrutinib was administered in the evening, two hours prior to the evening famotidine dose, AUC and C_{max} were reduced by only 2.4% and 9.5%, respectively.
L682: The review team agrees with the Applicant that the use of rilzabrutinib with PPIs should be avoided due to the large decrease in rilzabrutinib exposure. The team agrees with the Applicant
L683: that the use of rilzabrutinib with H2RAs are allowed, however rilzabrutinib should be administrated 2 hours prior to the use of H2RAs.
L684: ## 8.2.1.2.4. Effect of P-Gp Inhibitor on Rilzabrutinib
L685: In vitro studies indicated that rilzabrutinib is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate. Quinidine, a P-gp inhibitor, was dosed (300 mg Q8h, 5 days) and its effect was evaluated on single dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib. Quinidine increased the AUC of rilzabrutinib by 13% and decreased C_{max} of rilzabrutinib by 14%. These changes in rilzabrutinib exposures are not clinically significant.
L686: The review team agrees with the Applicant that the use of rilzabrutinib with P-gp inhibitors are allowed.
L687: ## 8.2.1.3. Effect of Rilzabrutinib on Other Drugs
L688: In vitro studies indicated that rilzabrutinib is an inhibitor and a weak inducer of CYP3A4. Rilzabrutinib is also an inhibitor of transporters P-gp, breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP)1B3, and bile salt export pump (BSEP).
L689: Refer to Section 14.1 for details of the in vitro studies of rilzabrutinib.
L690: Effect of rilzabrutinib towards substrates of CYP3A was evaluated using midazolam in a dedicated study, and also evaluated using PBPK modeling and simulations.
L691: PBPK modeling and simulations were conducted for rilzabrutinib to test its inhibition potential on P-gp, BCRP, and OATP1B3 substrates.
L692: ## 8.2.1.3.1. Effect of Rilzabrutinib on CYP3A Substrates
L693: Rilzabrutinib is an inhibitor and a weak inducer of CYP3A. Administration of a single dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib increased midazolam exposure by approximately 1.7- to 2.2-fold. Based on PBPK simulation, 5-day administration of 400 mg rilzabrutinib (QD, BID, TID) increased midazolam exposure by approximately 2-fold. Further, testing only the induction potential of rilzabrutinib, 14-day administration of 400 mg rilzabrutinib (QD, BID, TID) decreased midazolam exposure by 20%. Analysis of the net effect of CYP3A inhibition and induction predicted an increase in midazolam exposure by approximately 2-fold indicating that CYP3A inhibition was the major driver of the interaction of rilzabrutinib with midazolam.
L694: The review team conducted PBPK simulations and midazolam AUC is predicted to increase up to 3.5-fold in patients with immune thrombocytopenia following coadministration with 400 mg rilzabrutinib twice daily (see Section 14.5.3.4.2 for details). (b) (4)
L695: ## 8.2.1.3.2. Effect of Rilzabrutinib on P-Gp, BCRP, and OATP1B3 Substrates
L696: In vitro studies showed that rilzabrutinib is an inhibitor of P-gp (IC_{50}&lt;10\mu M), BCRP (IC_{50}&lt;10\mu M), and OATP1B3 (IC_{50}=0.18\mu M). Based on the static model, the intestinal luminal
L697: NDA 219685
WAYRILZ (rilzabrutinib)
L698: concentration of the interacting drug (I_{\text{gut}})/IC_{50} for both P-gp and BCRP is greater than 10, and the R value for OATP1B3 is close to the threshold of 1.1.
L699: PBPK modeling and simulation were conducted to evaluate the potential interactions between rilzabrutinib and P-gp, BCRP, and OATP1B substrates. The inhibition was assessed using digoxin, pravastatin, and rosuvastatin as model substrates for P-gp, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3, respectively. Rilzabrutinib was predicted to not result in clinically significant changes in transporter substrates exposure, according to the Applicant.
L700: Based on the risk assessment using the PBPK approach, the potential interaction of rilzabrutinib with substrates of these transporters cannot be ruled out. The potential inhibitory effects of rilzabrutinib may increase plasma concentrations of substrate drugs that are used in this patient population, which may increase the incidence and severity of adverse reactions due to drug toxicity of the substrate drugs. A dedicated DDI study to evaluate the interaction potential between rilzabrutinib and P-gp, BCRP and OATP1B substrates will be issued as a PMR.
L701: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L702: Rilzabrutinib received orphan drug designation for the treatment of ITP (Designation request # DRU-2017-6057, dated 15 October 2018); therefore, rilzabrutinib is exempt from the Pediatric Research Equity Act requirements for the indication.
L703: Rilzabrutinib is currently being evaluated in [REDACTED] (b)(4) patients age \ge 12 and &lt;18 years with primary ITP &gt;6 months duration who were unresponsive to available therapies in a separate cohort as part of LUNA 3. The study design for the pediatric cohort is the same as for adults but with differences in randomization algorithms and stratification.
L704: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L705: ## Animal Data
L706: The following nonclinical data in Table 34 is used to support the label. Additional detailed information is available in Section 13.
L707: **Table 34. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy, and Lactation**
L708: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy	
Risk Summary
Based on animal data, rilzabrutinib may cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant woman. In animal reproduction studies, oral administration of rilzabrutinib to pregnant rats and rabbits during organogenesis at exposures 4 to 10 times the human exposure (based on AUC) at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 400 mg twice daily did not cause adverse developmental effects. However, adverse visceral and skeletal findings occurred in rat fetuses at a maternally toxic dose at exposures 22 times the human exposure (based on AUC) at the MRHD (see Animal Data).
Animal Data
Rilzabrutinib given to pregnant rats by oral gavage at 50, 150, or 300 mg/kg/day during the period of organogenesis (gestation day 7 to 17) did not cause adverse effects on embryo-fetal development at exposures approximately 10 times the clinical exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC. Increased incidence of postimplantation loss (25%), delayed ossification associated with a markedly lower (24%) mean fetal weight and increased skeletal (scoliosis) and visceral malformations (abnormalities of major vessels, urogenital tract, and kidney) occurred in a preliminary study in rats at a maternally toxic dose of 500 mg/kg/day that resulted in exposures 22 times the clinical exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC.
Rilzabrutinib given to pregnant rabbits by oral gavage at 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg/day during the period of organogenesis (gestation day 7 to 19) did not cause adverse effects on embryo-fetal development at exposures approximately 4 times the clinical exposures at the MRHD, based on AUC. Renal visceral malformations occurred in a single fetus in a preliminary study in rabbits at 150 mg/kg/day that resulted in exposures 5.6 times the clinical exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC.
In a pre- and postnatal developmental toxicity study, pregnant rats were given rilzabrutinib by oral gavage at doses of 50, 150, or 300 mg/kg/day during the periods of gestation, parturition, and lactation (gestation day 7 to lactation day 21). Decreased body weight was observed in pups in the presence of maternal toxicity at 300 mg/kg/day, at exposures approximately 18 times the MRHD based on AUC. There were no effects of rilzabrutinib on the ability of F1 offspring to reproduce or on subsequent F2 development at any dose.

8.2 Lactation	
There are no data on the presence of rilzabrutinib or its metabolites in either human or animal milk, the effects on the breastfed child, or the effects on milk production. Due to the potential for adverse reactions in a breastfed child from rilzabrutinib, advise lactating women not to breastfeed while taking rilzabrutinib and for at least 1 week after the final dose.
L709: Source: PI labeling and FDA reviewer
Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; MRHD, maximum recommended human dose; PI, prescribing information; F1, first filial

As such, Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.

L715: ## 10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site and Other Good Clinical Practice Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
L716: LUNA 3 and LUNA 2 clinical studies were conducted under International Council on Harmonisation Good Clinical Practice guidelines. A signed written Informed Consent Form was required to enroll in any of the trials. The studies were reviewed and monitored by an Institutional Review Board, Research Ethics Board, or any Independent Ethics Committee depending on the country in which the trial site was located.
L717: The results of the clinical site inspections conducted by the Office of Scientific Investigations is summarized below in Section 22 and Applicant inspection support the conclusion that the trials were conducted adequately, and the data generated support the proposed indication. Review of the financial disclosures did not raise any concerns about the validity or reliability of the data. Please see Section 22 for a summary of inspection findings and Section 25 for financial disclosures for LUNA 3.
L718: ## 11. Advisory Committee Summary
L719: The application did not raise efficacy or safety issues requiring external expert advice. Therefore, an Advisory Committee meeting was not convened to discuss this application.
L720: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L721: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L722: On October 17, 2016, the Sponsor submitted a Type B pre-investigational new drug application (PIND) 132668 meeting request to discuss the development of PRN1008 (also known as rilzabrutinib), the design of the proposed study in patients with ITP, and the overall clinical development plan. On December 14, 2016, FDA provided written responses to Sponsor questions in lieu of a Type B PIND meeting under PIND 132668. The Agency agreed the proposed tests for drug substance specifications were appropriate for the stage development. The Agency recommended the Sponsor request an end of Phase 1 (EOP1) meeting at the completion of Phase 2 to discuss dose selection for future clinical trials based on available pharmacokinetic, pharmacodynamic, safety, and activity data and to discuss their proposed clinical pharmacology development plan.
L723: On April 12, 2017, the Sponsor submitted an investigational new drug (IND 132668) application under Section 505(i) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act (IND 132668) from Principia Biopharma titled “an Adaptive, Open-Label, Dose-Finding, Phase 1/2 Study Investing the Safety, Pharmacokinetics, Pharmacodynamics and Clinical Activity of PRN1008, an Oral BTK Inhibitor, in Patients with Relapsed Immune Thrombocytopenic Purpura.”
L724: On May 10, 2017, the IND was placed on full clinical hold due to unreasonable and significant risk of illness or injury to human subjects [21 CFR 312.42(b)(1)(i)] and due to insufficient information to assess risks to human subjects [21 CFR 312.42(b)(1)(iv)]. Deficiencies included findings of neurotoxicity in animal models based on influx of neutrophils into meninges and neuropil and the unknown long-term clinical effects when considering the potential for chronic use in ITP.
L725: On July 5, 2017, a Type B meeting was held to discuss the clinical hold deficiencies [21 CFR 312.42(b)(1)(i) and 21 CFR 312.42(b)(1)(iv)] and responses to the nonclinical hold comments identified in the clinical hold letter dated May 10, 2017. The Sponsor submitted a complete response to clinical hold on July 7, 2017. The full clinical hold was removed on August 1, 2017.
L726: On June 1, 2018, a request for Special Protocol Assessment (SPA) was submitted for a 2-year animal carcinogenicity protocol. On June 26, 2018, the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC) held a meeting to discuss conducting a carcinogenicity study to support the registration of rilzabrutinib for the treatment of various autoimmune and inflammatory diseases. An ECAC report with recommendations and conclusions on the 2-year animal carcinogenicity protocol was provided to the Sponsor, on June 28, 2018.
L727: Rilzabrutinib was granted ODD on October 15, 2018, for the treatment of ITP.
L728: On June 10, 2019, a request for SPA was submitted for a 26-week animal carcinogenicity protocol. The ECAC held a meeting on July 9, 2019, to discuss the proposed 6-month carcinogenicity study for rilzabrutinib for the treatment of various autoimmune and inflammatory diseases. An ECAC report with recommendations and conclusions was provided to the Sponsor on July 19, 2019.
L729: NDA 219685
WAYRILZ (rilzabrutinib)
L730: On March 19, 2020, a request for SPA was submitted for 26-week animal carcinogenicity study. The ECAC held a meeting on April 21, 2020, to discuss major metabolite concentrations in the transgenic mice in the proposed 26-week carcinogenicity study. An ECAC report with recommendations and conclusions was provided to the Sponsor on April 29, 2020.
L731: On May 27, 2020, an end of Phase 2 (EOP2) Type B guidance meeting was held to discuss the clinical development plan for rilzabrutinib, including LUNA 2 Part B with historical cross study comparison and a proposed randomized placebo-controlled trial in support of a marketing application. In the meeting, the Agency identified concerns regarding early crossover of participants at 12 weeks, which would complicate the analysis and interpretation of study results. Specifically, participants who cross over will be 'missing' from the analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint because they will not have completed the 24-week treatment period, even though they will be counted early as failures. These participants will reflect a selected subpopulation of the enrolled participants, and there likely will be imbalances in the numbers of these less effected participants between the two treatment arms. The Agency also recommended to include age, severity of platelet count, and prior splenectomy as stratification factors and to include an increase in doses of concomitant ITP medication e.g., corticosteroids, as use of rescue medication. Participants who take rescue therapy or who are withdrawn due to an adverse event should be censored as having a loss of platelet response at the time of the event occurring.
L732: During the meeting, the Sponsor's proposed clinical outcome assessments were discussed. The topics of whether the instruments intended for measurement were fit for purpose (b) (4) were addressed as "premature to comment on". FDA recommendations for any COA (b) (4) and the importance of use of patient input were provided to the Sponsor.
L733: On July 17, 2020, the Sponsor submitted the Phase 3 clinical study protocol for PRN1008-018 (LUNA 3). FDA comments were received on August 19, 2020. On September 18, 2020, the Sponsor submitted their responses to these FDA comments, including the following (b) (4)
L734: (b) (4)
L735: Fast Track designation for rilzabrutinib for the treatment of ITP was granted on October 15, 2020.
L736: On March 16, 2021, the Sponsor submitted General Correspondence – Pediatric Study Plan, stating that since rilzabrutinib for ITP has ODD, it is exempt from Pediatric Research Equity Act requirement, and thus, they do not intend to submit an agreed iPSP.
L737: On December 12, 2021, the Sponsor submitted Protocol PRN1008-018 Amendment 03, which included moving the Idiopathic Thrombocytopenic Purpura Bleeding Scale (IBLS) assessment
L738: from a secondary to an exploratory endpoint and to introduce fatigue assessment using the Fatigue component of the ITP-PAQ, as a key secondary endpoint.
L739: On February 10, 2022, clinical responses/information requests were provided to the Sponsor via Advice/Information Request email communication and included the following comments from the Division of Clinical Outcome Assessments.
L740: - The ITP-PAQ uses only one item (item 10) to assess physical fatigue over the past 4 weeks. A single item cannot sufficiently measure fatigue, which is multidimensional (physical fatigue, mental fatigue). Item 10 measures both disease and treatment-related symptoms that may overlap. FDA stated concerns regarding recall error due to the 4-week recall period. (b) (4) FDA recommended using a multi-item fatigue assessment measuring the most important and relevant fatigue dimensions of the target population.
- PGIS and PGIC anchor scales to assess the threshold for meaningful within-patient score change in the ITP-PAQ Fatigue domain score may limit the interpretability of the anchor-based analyses. The PGIS and PGIS-Fatigue assess “past week” while ITP-PAQ item 10 assesses “past 4 weeks” and ITP-PAQ item 10 measures symptom frequency, while PGIS scales measure symptom severity. Due to the limitations of the anchor scales, FDA recommended the Sponsor consider exploring other methods to assess the threshold for meaningful within-patient change.
L741: On June 30, 2022, the Sponsor responded to the February 10, 2022, FDA feedback regarding PRN1008-018 draft protocol Amendment 03 PRO strategy. In the Sponsor response, they stated they recognized that “the Agency has questions about whether item 10 from the ITP-PAQ instrument is a well-defined and reliable measure of fatigue” and acknowledged “the limitations of the anchor scales in PRN1008-018 highlighted by the Agency.”
L742: On September 30, 2022 FDA provided an email communication reiterating that, “We continue to have concern regarding the use of a single item to measure a multidimensional concept like fatigue, particularly over a lengthy time frame (i.e., 4 weeks), but will review the completed qualitative and quantitative data to evaluate content validity and other measurement properties for item 10 of the Immune Thrombocytopenia Patient Assessment Questionnaire (ITP-PAQ) at the time of NDA submission.”
L743: On October 13, 2022, comments via email were provided to the Sponsor in reference to PRN1008-018 draft protocol Amendment 03, specifically highlighting continued concerns regarding the use of a single item to measure a multidimensional concept like fatigue over a lengthy timeframe (4 weeks). Additional questions regarding the “meaningful change assessment of ITP-PAQ item 10” were the subject of other information requests. FDA reiterated it will review the data and other measurement properties for item 10 of the ITP-PAQ at the time of NDA submission.
L744: On January 10, 2024, the Agency provided Written Responses for a Type C meeting request. The Applicant was strongly encouraged to request a meeting to discuss topline efficacy and safety results from PRN1008-018 and plans for providing confirmatory evidence prior to the NDA submission. In addition, FDA requested justification for the Sponsor’s proposed data cutoff for financial disclosure (approximately 6 months prior to the planned NDA submission).
