L747: (b) (4)
L748: A Type B pre-NDA meeting was held on July 15, 2024. The purpose of the meeting was to seek the Agency's concurrence regarding the Sponsor's plan to submit an NDA for rilzabrutinib for the treatment of ITP and to discuss the adequacy of the efficacy and safety data package to support the filing and review of the NDA for the proposed indication, Drug Abuse Liability Assessment strategy, proposed plan for the 120-day safety update, proposed NDA table of contents, proposed study data standardization plan, regulatory milestones including application orientation meeting and advisory committee meeting. The FDA disagreed with the Applicant's proposal to submit the Day 120-Safety Update one month late (end of February 2025) to allow for all participants to complete the open-label period of LUNA 3, requesting instead that it be submitted Day 120 (end of January 2025). In addition, the FDA stated concerns regarding the high number of participants in both treatment arms who did not complete the 24-week double-blind treatment period and noted this may impact interpretability of the safety and efficacy results. FDA reiterated its continued concerns regarding the use of a single item to measure a multidimensional concept like fatigue, particularly over a lengthy time frame (i.e., 4 weeks) for the PRO endpoint, but noted, it would review the completed qualitative and quantitative data to evaluate content validity and other measurement properties for item 10 of ITP-PAQ at the time of NDA submission. Additional nonclinical studies were requested to be included with the dossier.

L752: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L753: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L754: ## 13.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
L755: Data from a series of in vitro and in vivo nonclinical pharmacology studies (Table 35) were completed with rilzabrutinib, a new molecular entity BTK inhibitor, to support the mechanism of action and potential benefits of rilzabrutinib for the treatment in patients with ITP.
L756: Primary pharmacology studies established:
L757: - Rilzabrutinib is a reversible, covalent BTK inhibitor with an IC50 of 1.3nM for BTK enzymatic activity as demonstrated in a biochemical assay using glutathione S-transferase-tagged full-length recombinant BTK and fluorescent peptide substrate.In vitro, rilzabrutinib demonstrates extended target occupancy with 85% BTK occupancy maintained at 24 hours after initial binding.
- Rilzabrutinib exhibits inhibition of key BTK-dependent immune pathways with IC50 values in the low nM to ~100nM range for B cell proliferation, IgG-mediated monocyte activation, and B cell receptor-mediated B cell activation in human whole blood.Rilzabrutinib does not affect platelet aggregation in normal healthy volunteers or participants with ITP despite BTK expression in platelets.
- In a proof-of-concept ITP mouse model, rilzabrutinib demonstrated dose-dependent prevention of platelet loss compared to vehicle-treated controls.
L758: **Table 35. Summary of Primary Pharmacology Studies**
L759: Study No./ Study Title	Key Study Findings
Study DVR0102/ Biochemical characterization of PRN1008*
GLP compliance: No	In Vitro
Rilzabrutinib inhibited BTK with an IC50 of 1.3nM in a biochemical kinase assay using glutathione S-transferase (GST)-tagged, full-length recombinant BTK enzyme and a fluorescent peptide substrate (FAM-GEEPLYWSFPAKKK-NH2) with detection via electrophoretic separation on the LabChip 3000 platform.
Study DVR0070/ The in vitro cell pharmacology of PRN1008*
GLP compliance: No	Rilzabrutinib inhibition in human whole blood:Decreased B cell activation, mean IC50 of 123±38nM. (Inhibition of CD69 expression on stimulated CD20 plus B cells by flow cytometry).
BTK target occupancy in PBMCs, IC50 of 233±75nM. (BTK target occupancy IC50 is the concentration of rilzabrutinib needed to block 50% of BTK binding sites, measured by adding fluorescent probes that can only bind to unoccupied BTK sites and then detecting how much probe binding is prevented.)
Decreased basophil activation IC50 of 490±130nM. (Inhibition of CD63 expression by rilzabrutinib measured using flow cytometry).
Inhibition of rilzabrutinib in human Ramos B cells:BTK target occupancy, IC50 of 8±2nM (BTK target occupancy in Ramos B cells was measured by treating cells with a biotinylated probe (PRN299-001) that binds to unoccupied BTK sites and detecting the probe binding using Alphascreen technology.)
Durable BTK binding, 76±8% occupancy 4 hours after washout and 60±13% at 18 hours after washout (occupancy in Ramos B cells over time).
Activity of rilzabrutinib in off-target cellular cross screens:Minimal activity (IC50>5 µM) in non-BTK pathways, as determined measuring IL-4-induced STAT6 (signal transducer and activator of transcription 6) activation in Ramos B cells, nuclear factor of activated T cells (NFAT) activation in Jurkat T cells (stimulated with anti-CD3/anti-CD28 or thapsigargin), AP-1 (activator protein 1) activation in ME-180 (human cervical carcinoma) cells (stimulated with epidermal growth factor), and cytotoxicity testing in HCT-116 (human colon carcinoma) cells.
Overall, the potency of rilzabrutinib in the B cell activation assay mirrored BTK target occupancy (Figure 10).

L765: Study DVR0167/ Inhibitory Effect of 4 compounds on anti-IgM induced human B cell proliferation	Rilzabrutinib showed inhibitory effects on anti-IgM-induced human B cell proliferation with an IC_{50} value of 5.03\pm 2.44nM. This inhibitory effect at nM levels supported rilzabrutinib-related inhibition of B cell proliferation induced by anti-IgM, which mimics B cell receptor signaling, which is considered a key mechanism in B cell-mediated autoimmune diseases like ITP.

This inhibitory effect at nM levels demonstrated that rilzabrutinib can potentially inhibit IgG-mediated TNF\alpha production in human monocytes. This is important because it shows a potent effect of rilzabrutinib on Fc receptor signaling, considered another key pathway in autoimmune diseases like ITP, in addition to its effects on B cells.
GLP compliance: No
L768: Study No./ Study Title	Key Study Findings
Study DVR0307/ Assessing in vitro effects of PRN1008* and ibrutinib on agonist-induced platelet aggregation
GLP compliance: No	
The study tested rilzabrutinib's effects on platelet aggregation using platelet-rich plasma isolated from whole blood collected from healthy volunteers and participants with ITP induced by multiple agonists: 5 µM (7.14 µg/ml) thrombin receptor activating peptide, 10 µM (4.27 µg/ml) adenosine diphosphate (ADP), 1,500 µg/ml ristocetin, 5 µg/ml collagen, 2.5 µg/ml collagen, and 5 µM (1.76 µg/ml) U46619 (a thromboxane receptor agonist). Rilzabrutinib at concentrations of 0.3 µM (0.197 µg/ml) and 1 µM (0.666 µg/ml) had no significant effect on platelet aggregation induced by any of the agonists tested in either healthy volunteers or participants with ITP.
Rilzabrutinib did not induce spontaneous platelet aggregation when tested with saline. However, the positive control roxifiban (an integrin \alphaIIb\beta3 antagonist) substantially inhibited platelet aggregation, as expected, confirming the validity of the assay.

In Vivo
Study DVR0106/ Murine in vivo BTK PK/PD occupancy durability PO study
Female Lewis and Sprague Dawley rats, female
C57BL/6 mice; oral (ROA in rats: oral gavage; in mice: in diet)
GLP compliance: no	
At 14 hours postdoses of 10, 20, and 40 mg/kg rilzabrutinib to Lewis rats, BTK occupancy was 60.9%, 63.6%, and 74.6%, respectively. In addition, at 24 hours, BTK occupancy remained 49.9-65.3%, despite rilzabrutinib plasma levels being below the quantification limit.
BTK occupancy at 14 hours where rilzabrutinib was present at 1.3 ng/mL in plasma was 61.5% at 20 mg/kg in Sprague Dawley rats.
BTK occupancy was 84.6% and 91.4% when rilzabrutinib was given at 1.67 and 3.33 g/kg/day for 14 days in medicated feed, respectively, to C57BL/6 mice.

L774: ## 13.1.2. Secondary Pharmacology
L775: **Table 36. Summary of Secondary Pharmacology Studies**
L776: Study No./ Study Title	Key Study Findings
Study DVR0102/ Biochemical Characterization of PRN1008*
GLP compliance: no	
In Vitro
In a screen of 251 kinases, only 6 kinases (including BTK) showed >90% inhibition at 1 µM rilzabrutinib. BTK is the primary target for treating ITP, as it plays a crucial role in B cell receptor and Fc receptor signaling, which are important in the pathogenesis of ITP.
The most potently inhibited kinases besides BTK were tyrosine kinase expressed in hepatocellular carcinoma (TEC), bone marrow tyrosine kinase in chromosome X (BMX), T cell X chromosome kinase (TXK), and B-cell lymphocyte kinase (BLK), with IC50 values <10nM. The IC50 for BTK was 1.3nM. Rilzabrutinib shows similar potency against TEC, BMX, TXK, and BTK, while BLK is about 5-fold less potent (Table 37) than BTK inhibition and the relevance of inhibition of these additional targets is unclear. However, toxicology studies in two species (rats and dogs) did not reveal any adverse effects associated with this additional kinase inhibition.

L796: ## **In Vivo**
L797: ## Study DVR0159/ Excretion Mass Balance and Tissue Distribution by Quantitative Whole-Body Autoradiography in Rats Following a Single Oral Dose (40 mg/kg) of [14C]PRN1008*
L798: Quantitative whole-body autoradiography (QWBA) evaluated the tissue distribution of [14C]rilzabrutinib following a single 40 mg/kg oral dose in male rats. The analysis detected radioactivity concentrations below the limit of
L799: ## NDA 219685
## WAYRILZ (rilzabrutinib)
L800: GLP compliance: no
L801: quantification (BQL) in all examined central nervous system tissues, including the cerebrum, cerebellum, medulla, and spinal cord.
L802: These findings demonstrate that rilzabrutinib exhibits minimal CNS penetration and does not readily cross the blood-brain barrier at the tested dose level.
L803: ## Study DVR0212/ Cerebral Spinal Fluid Concentrations of PRN1008* in Female Wistar Han Rats Following an Oral Dose
L804: The blood-brain barrier penetration study demonstrated that rilzabrutinib does not cross into the central nervous system when administered orally at 50 mg/kg in female Wistar Han rats. All rilzabrutinib concentrations in cerebrospinal fluid remained below the lower limit of quantitation (LLOQ = 1.25 ng/mL) at all timepoints, indicating no detectable CNS penetration. Plasma concentrations validated adequate systemic exposure, with rilzabrutinib levels at 1 hour (894 ng/mL), 4 hours (320 ng/mL), and 24 hours (0.6 ng/mL) consistent with those observed in the 13-week repeat dose toxicology study (1010 ng/mL, 142 ng/mL, and 1.5 ng/mL, respectively). Despite achieving equivalent plasma exposures, rilzabrutinib was undetectable in cerebrospinal fluid, establishing limited central nervous system penetration.
L805: GLP compliance: no
L806: ## Study DVR0223/ Pharmacokinetic/Pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) Study With Daily PO Dosing of PRN1008*, PRN35, PRN2296 and PRN1669 for 3 Days and Daily IV Dosing of PRN1008 for 3 Days
L807: A 3-day rat study assessed CNS effects of rilzabrutinib and other three orally administered BTK inhibitors and intravenous rilzabrutinib, examining neuroinflammation and microglial activation in brain tissue.
L808: Rilzabrutinib:
L809: GLP compliance: no
L810: - At 500 mg/kg oral dose: Caused minimal to moderate neutrophilic inflammation in meninges and neuropil (4/5 animals), and increased IBA-1 positivity in periventricular areas and neuropil.
- At 10 mg/kg intravenous dose: No significant microscopic changes or increased IBA-1 positivity observed.
L811: Other BTK inhibitors (oral dosing only):
L812: - Ibrutinib (PRN35) at 175 mg/kg: Caused minimal inflammation (1/5 animals) and increased IBA-1 positivity in periventricular areas and neuropil.
- PRN2296 (Structural analog/chemical structure variant of rilzabrutinib within the same company's pipeline) at 300 mg/kg: No inflammation, but increased IBA-1 positivity in periventricular areas and neuropil.
- PRN1669 (Alternative BTK inhibitor, a backup compound or alternative development candidate) at 300 mg/kg: No significant microscopic changes or increased IBA-1 positivity observed.
L813: The study revealed that oral administration of rilzabrutinib at high doses (AUC at 500 mg/kg is 32,600 ng*hr/mL) can result minimal to moderate neuroinflammation and microglial activation in rats, while intravenous
L814: Study DVR0219/ Study DVR0224/ Pharmacokinetic-Pharmacodynamic Study With Daily Intraejunal Dosing of PRN1008* up to 100 mg/kg for 3 Days in Female Wistar Hans Rats
GLP compliance: no	administration of rilzabrutinib at a lower dose (AUC at 10 mg/kg, 7,280 ng*hr/mL) resulting in a 5-fold lower plasma exposure did not produce CNS effects. These results suggested the study may have used insufficient intravenous dose(s) of rilzabrutinib to assess any difference in impact of the intravenous route on the CNS.
A previous study (DVR0223) with oral administration caused minimal to moderate neutrophilic inflammation in meninges and neuropil (4/5 animals), and increased IBA-1 positivity in periventricular areas and neuropil. These findings raised questions about potential CNS toxicity.
In the current study, administration of rilzabrutinib at 100 mg/kg for 3 days by the intraejunal route (bypassing the stomach), resulted in a minimal increase in IBA-1 positivity in the periventricular region surrounding the lateral ventricles in one of four rats.
IBA-1 is a relatively specific immunohistochemical marker for microglial cells. Increased IBA-1 positivity suggests an increase in the number and/or size of microglial cells in the brain. While this could suggest potential neuroinflammation, H&E staining revealed no evidence of neurodegeneration or inflammation.
This study suggested that avoiding upper GI exposure decreased the CNS effect compared to oral gavage administration at similar plasma exposures. The implications of this effect are unclear.
Study DVR0225/ Pharmacokinetic-Pharmacodynamic Study With Daily PO Dosing of PRN1008* at 500 mg/kg and PRN35 (Ibrutinib) at 175 mg/kg for 3 Days in Female Wistar Hans Rats
GLP compliance: no	Rilzabrutinib administered orally at 500 mg/kg caused minimal to mild neutrophilic inflammation in the meninges and neuropil and increased IBA-1 positivity (indicating microglial activation) in all treated animals (5 out of 5).
Despite these observations, no detectable evidence of neurodegeneration was found in brain sections by H&E staining, and the significance of the increased IBA-1 positivity remains unclear.
Study DVR0226/ Crossover Canine Gut Hormones' Study of PRN1008* Through PO Administration to Male Beagle Dogs
GLP compliance: no	A single oral dose of 30 mg/kg rilzabrutinib increased plasma levels of both peptide YY and leptin in male beagle dogs. In this acute, single dose study with a limited 6-hour food intake observation period, no reduction in food consumption was observed.
These findings demonstrate that rilzabrutinib can influence gut hormone pathways, though acute hormonal changes did not translate to immediate effects on appetite regulation in this short-term study.
Study DVR0235/ Effect of PRN1008* and Cinnamaldehyde on Gastric Emptying Through Oral or Intraejunal Administration to Wistar Hans Female Rats	Delayed gastric emptying was observed at 1, 3, 5, and 7-hours postdose following repeated oral dosing of rilzabrutinib at 500 mg/kg for 4 days, while no
L815: effect on gastric emptying was observed up to 7 hours postdose following repeated intrajejunal dosing of rilzabrutinib at 100 mg/kg for 3 days.
L816: GLP compliance: no
L817: These results suggest local action in the stomach may influence the gastric effects rather than rilzabrutinib direct systemic effects after absorption.
L818: Oral administration of rilzabrutinib showed that the stomach weights and sizes were increased compared to vehicle control, whereas intrajejunal administration did not affect stomach weight or size.
L819: Study DVR0301/ Twelve-Week Oral Capsule Bridging Study of PRN1008* in Beagle Dogs (n=16; 4/sex/Group; Doses 30 or 50 mg/kg/day)
L820: The study demonstrated that the capsule formulation was well-tolerated with no mortality and all animals surviving to scheduled termination.
L821: Dose-related emesis (males at 50 mg/kg; females at both doses), abnormal fecal quality at both dose levels, and body weight loss in females at the high dose (two animals lost 15%-18% body weight, requiring supplemental feeding for one animal).
L822: GLP compliance: no
L823: Systemic exposure was confirmed in all animals with T_{max} &lt;2 hours and less than 2-fold accumulation over the dosing period.
L824: Overall, this capsule formulation demonstrated improved tolerability compared to the 13-week gavage study (study # 1281-14256 discussed under general toxicology section) using liquid citric acid formulation, showing significant reductions in stool changes and emesis, absence of deaths from severe weight loss, while maintaining similar drug exposure levels. The 30 mg/kg dose represents a well-tolerated dose level with minimal adverse findings, while 50 mg/kg represents a maximum tolerated dose with reversible findings.
L825: Source: Applicant's submission
L826: *PRN1008 denotes rilzabrutinib
L827: Rilzabrutinib (and its major metabolites) showed minimal to no brain penetration in rats, indicating it does not readily cross the blood-brain-barrier. Studies comparing intrajejunal (IJ) to PO dosing in rats showed that brain findings were less frequent and severe with IJ administration despite achieving similar plasma drug concentrations by PO dosing. While these results did not determine the mechanism of action causing brain findings with rilzabrutinib in rats, they do suggest involvement of the gut-brain axis and indicate that the lesions were unlikely to represent a direct effect of rilzabrutinib in the brain.
L828: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; BTK, Bruton's tyrosine kinase; CNS, central nervous system; GI, gastrointestinal; GLP, good laboratory practice; H&amp;E, hematoxylin and eosin; IBA-1, ionized calcium binding adapter protein 1; ITP, immune thrombocytopenia; IV, intravenous; PO, by mouth; T_{max}, time to maximum concentration
L829: ## 13.1.3. Safety Pharmacology
L830: A core battery of safety pharmacology studies evaluated the potential effects of rilzabrutinib on cardiovascular, respiratory, and neurologic endpoints. These studies included an in vitro human ether-à-go-go related gene (hERG) assay, an in vivo assessment of the cardiovascular system in dogs, and in vivo assessments of the CNS and respiratory system in Sprague Dawley rats. The hERG assay showed significant inhibition only at irrelevantly high plasma concentrations (based on free drug). The in vivo studies were conducted at doses up to 500 mg/kg, which did not show any adverse rilzabrutinib-related effects in the CNS, respiratory or cardiovascular systems (including QTc).

L839: An IC50 was determined at 3.5 µM (2330 ng/mL). The risk of QT prolongation in human is considered low due to rilzabrutinib's high protein binding (97.5%) in plasma. This free drug concentration is approximately 621-fold below the hERG IC50, providing an adequate safety margin.

L845: ## 13.1.4. ADME/Pharmacokinetics
L846: ## 13.1.4.1. Nonclinical Pharmacokinetic Parameters of Rilzabrutinib
L847: Rilzabrutinib is a small molecule combination of E (up to 90%) and Z isomers and R/S enantiomers (predominantly R). The Z isomer is mostly converted to E isomer following oral dosing in rats and humans. Both E and Z isomers have comparable pharmacological activity. The R enantiomer has greater durability of pharmacological potency, but there is negligible conversion of R to S in vivo. In addition, physiochemical properties of rilzabrutinib include high hydrophobicity and pH-dependent solubility, which appear to dictate its absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) profile in both animals and humans, resulting in minimal renal clearance.
L848: The pharmacokinetic and toxicokinetic properties of rilzabrutinib were addressed primarily in rats, dogs, mice, rabbits, and monkeys. Single oral/IV dose PK studies found rilzabrutinib to have a rapid rate of absorption, metabolism, and clearance rate and variable bioavailability in all test species. The half-life of rilzabrutinib in single dose studies ranged from 0.6 to 2.6 hrs. (Table 41). The shortest t1/2 in all test species occurred following IV and low oral doses and t1/2 appeared to be longer after larger oral doses. In single dose studies, a 5-fold increase in dose was associated with a 10-fold increase in exposure (20 mg/kg versus 100 mg/kg) in rats. In contrast, the increase in exposure was dose proportional in repeat dose studies. Rilzabrutinib bioavailability in rats increased from 12.5% at 20 mg/kg to 52.7% at 100 mg/kg in single dose
L849: PK studies. Similar findings were also seen in dogs where the bioavailability increased from 7.5% at 20 mg/kg to 28.2% at 100 mg/kg (Table 41).
L850: **Table 41. Pharmacokinetic Parameters of Rilzabrutinib After Single Oral or Intravenous Administration of Rilzabrutinib in Rats, Dogs, and Monkeys**

L854: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the plasma concentration time curve; CL, clearance; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; F, female; IV, intravenous; M, male; NA, not applicable; PK, pharmacokinetic; PO, by mouth; t1/2z, terminal half-life; Tmax, time to maximum concentration; Vz, apparent volume of distribution during the terminal phase
L855: The variability observed in the t1/2, plasma exposure, and bioavailability in single dose studies from small to large doses of rilzabrutinib (20 versus 100 mg/kg) is likely reflective of limited capacity of liver CYP enzymes to metabolize rilzabrutinib. Large doses of rilzabrutinib were likely exceed the metabolizing capacity, which is not seen with low doses. Repeated administration of rilzabrutinib resulted in adaptive hepatic increase in microsomal enzymes (liver hypertrophy) leading to increase in liver CYP capacity, preventing rilzabrutinib accumulation with large doses in repeat dose studies (Gilbert and Goldberg 1965). Whether greater absorption occurred at higher doses is not clear. When rilzabrutinib was delivered intrajejunal route bypassing the stomach, a significant increase in rilzabrutinib was observed at 50 mg/kg (2 to 3-fold) and 100 mg/kg (6 to 7-fold). Rilzabrutinib is an acidic compound, and it is possible that larger doses allowed faster transit from the stomach than small doses.
L856: Rilzabrutinib was highly protein bound in all species tested. Rilzabrutinib plasma protein binding in rat, dog and human was ≥99%, 96% and 97.5%, respectively. As a small hydrophobic molecule with minimal solubility in water or basic environment, rilzabrutinib is not expected to readily enter red blood cells (RBCs). In vitro partitioning assays of rilzabrutinib to blood cells versus plasma (Table 42) found rilzabrutinib had limited ability to bind RBCs and the plasma concentration of rilzabrutinib was more than 2-fold greater than the concentration in RBCs. In vivo partitioning of [14C] rilzabrutinib found blood to plasma ratio nearly equal (up to 4 hrs. postdose). The blood to plasma ratio increased to 1.55 at 168 hrs. suggesting that slow release from blood cells was one of the last reservoirs for rilzabrutinib.
L857: **Table 42. Rilzabrutinib Partitioning to RBC Across Different Species**
L858: Compound	Red Blood Cells to Plasma Ratio (K_{RBC/PI})
Human	Dog	Rat
Rilzabrutinib	0.471	0.40	0.27
Chloroquine (control)	5.07	8.19	7.01
L859: Source: Applicant study DVR0083
L860: 1 Red blood cell to plasma distribution ratio of 0.47, indicating limited RBC binding in human
Chloroquine was used as a comparator
L861: Distribution of rilzabrutinib to different tissues and biological fluids in rats (Long Evans and Sprague Dawley rats) were evaluated using radiolabeled [14C] rilzabrutinib. Radiolabeled rilzabrutinib was distributed to most tissues in both strains of rats following oral administration with most of radioactivity detected in the gastrointestinal (GI) tract, from esophagus to large intestine. Rilzabrutinib metabolized in the liver was excreted into bile (67% of total radioactivity) and to feces (90%) in rats. Limited radioactivity was detected in the urine in rats (1-3%) highlighting that kidney played a minor role in clearance of rilzabrutinib, which is consistent with limited renal clearance in humans. Tissues with the lowest rilzabrutinib distribution were, fatty tissues such as mammary gland, adipose, bone marrow, spinal cord, and (unexpectedly) brain. Transfer of rilzabrutinib and metabolites across the blood brain barrier to cerebral spinal fluid, spinal cord and brain were below detection limits in Wistar rats. Rilzabrutinib concentrations in the pigmented tissues (eye uveal, skin) were higher than nonpigmented tissues suggesting rilzabrutinib may interact with melanin.
L862: Rilzabrutinib underwent oxidation, dealkylation, hydrogenation, hydrolysis, and sulfation. Metabolic pathway among test species and humans were qualitatively similar. Hepatic CYP3A4 was identified as the primary CYP enzyme responsible for rapid metabolism of rilzabrutinib to the prominent thiocyanate metabolite, PRN4400 (M1), which comprised ~94% of total drug-related plasma radioactivity in rats. In humans, M1 was a major circulating metabolite (\ge 10\% of total drug-related AUC). PRN834 (also known as M16 or hydrolyzed metabolite), representing ~6% of plasma radioactivity in rats and &lt;10% in humans, was formed in all species by extrahepatic pathway and is not expected to compete with coadministered drugs that are metabolized by CYP3A4.
L863: The M1 thiocyanate metabolite was identified in later stages of rilzabrutinib development during an in vivo human mass balance study. In vitro metabolism studies and in vivo animal metabolism studies failed to identify thiocyanate as a metabolite. It appears that earlier studies had failed due to coelution of thiocyanate with cyano acetic acid in high performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) analysis and the fact that thiocyanate is also formed endogenously from dietary sources in humans and animals, may have contributed to uncertainty. Thiocyanate concentrations in human serum have been reported to range from 3 to 15 mg/L (3 to 15 \mug/mL) (Exela®Pharma®Sciences 1988). The mass balance study in humans conducted by the Applicant determined the C_{max} for thiocyanate in plasma between 2.4 and 6.6 \mug/mL at maximum clinical dose of 400 mg BID. The identity of thiocyanate as rilzabrutinib's primary metabolite was confirmed in a 14-day bridging study in rats at 150 and 500 mg/kg/day. The T_{max} for M1 thiocyanate metabolite ranged from 0.5 to 24 hours on Day 1. There was no notable evidence of M1 accumulation at 150 mg/kg (&lt;2-fold). However, larger 500 mg/kg dose resulted in greater than 2-fold in M1 thiocyanate metabolite in rats which is consistent with adaptive hepatic CYP enzyme to repeat large doses breaking down more rilzabrutinib discussed earlier (Table 43). The C_{max} for thiocyanate in rats at 150 mg/kg and 500 mg/kg rilzabrutinib approximated the clinical
L864: thiocyanate exposure at maximum clinical dose of 400 mg BID rilzabrutinib. Thiocyanate has the potential to interfere with iodine uptake by inhibiting thyroid sodium-iodide symporter (EPA 2006; Darrouzet et al. 2014), and be mildly neurotoxicant at very high concentration (60 µg/mL). However, since thiocyanate levels formed from rilzabrutinib were within the normal range reported in humans and similar thiocyanate levels were tested in rats, the overall safety risk is considered to be negligible.

L871: ## 13.1.4.2. Toxicokinetic Parameters of Rilzabrutinib
L872: The toxicokinetics of rilzabrutinib were evaluated following once daily oral administration in chronic toxicity studies conducted in Sprague Dawley rats (Table 44) and beagle dogs (Table 45). Plasma concentrations of rilzabrutinib and its metabolites were measured by validated HPLC tandem mass spectrometry. Oral administration of rilzabrutinib was associated with dose-proportional increases in AUC exposure with no evidence of accumulation (&lt;2-fold) with repeated exposures in rats and dogs. The increases with repeated dosing were lower than normally anticipated at steady state. Low accumulation at steady-state was likely due to adaptive increases in hepatic microsomal CYP enzyme (seen as hepatic hypertrophy) in rats. Rilzabrutinib exposures in female rats was ~2-fold greater than in males. There was no notable sex-related difference (&lt;2-fold) in rilzabrutinib exposures in dogs.

L883: ## 13.1.5. General Toxicology
L884: ## Study 1281-16101: Six-Month Oral GLP Toxicity Study of Rilzabrutinib in Rats
L885: ## Key Study Findings
L886: - Target organs of toxicity included brain and gastrointestinal tract.
- Rilzabrutinib-related mortality was noted in five females and 1 male at high dose (HD) but cause of death was not determined except for a HD female that was linked to brain inflammation at exposures 12x higher than the MRHD exposure.
- Microscopic findings included:Brain: Minimal to moderate mixed cell inflammation in meninges, neutrophilic inflammation in neuropil, increased periventricular IBA-1 positivity (microgliosis) and GFAP positivity (astrocyte increase) indicative of neurotoxicity that were partially resolved at the end of recovery. Similar findings were observed in the 13-week toxicology study in rats.
- Gastrointestinal system: Hemorrhage/inflammation in stomach, duodenum, and esophagus (inflammation only at HD in two females) occurred at exposures that were approximately 9 to 12 times higher than the MRHD exposure.
- Overall, administration of rilzabrutinib shows dose-dependent effects across multiple organ systems. Brain findings suggest neurotoxicity with partial recovery, consistent with previous 13-week study in rats. Inflammatory changes (neutrophil infiltration, microglial activation) in brains occurred without evidence of neuronal damage or degeneration. The occurrence of microgliosis in the absence a direct effect on neurons is consistent with the reported BTK expression pattern in the brain (higher microglial expression, lower neuronal expression).
- Thyroid changes indicate potential endocrine disruption, with some sex-specific effects. Liver findings suggest adaptive responses even at lower doses. Gastrointestinal effects, observed at high exposures, imply potential for mucosal irritation and/or damage.
L887: - The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was defined at 50 mg/kg/day based on mortality, inflammation of meninges/neuropil and hemorrhage/inflammation of stomach at higher doses with corresponding AUC0-24 2060 and 5760 ng*h/mL in males and females. Exposures at the NOAEL approximated the clinical exposures at the MRHD of 400 mg BID, based on AUC.

L889: Yes.

L895: Parameter	Major Findings
Mortality	Eight toxicology animals and five toxicokinetic animals did not survive to scheduled sacrifice. Six of eight early deaths in the toxicology groups (3 females at 150 mg/kg/day, 1 male at 300 mg/kg/day, and 2 females at 300 mg/kg/day) were considered rilzabrutinib-related but cause of death was not determined except for a HD female that was linked to brain inflammation.
Clinical signs	No rilzabrutinib-associated clinical signs except dyspnea, red staining around mouth, pallor, and decreased activity at 150 and 300 mg/kg/day. No clinical signs were noted during the recovery period.
Body weights	Group mean body weight was consistently lower in males and females at 300 mg/kg/day (up to -6%). No changes were noted during the recovery period.
Food consumptions
ophthalmology,
hematology, coagulation,
clinical chemistry,
urinalysis, or peripheral
blood immunophenotyping.	There were no rilzabrutinib-related findings.
Gross pathology	Red and black foci in glandular stomach corresponding to hemorrhage findings in four terminal sacrifice male and females were noted at ≥150 mg/kg/day.
L896: Parameter	Major Findings
Histopathology
Adequate battery: yes	Histopathology findings were noted in the thyroid, brain, stomach, duodenum, esophagus, and liver.
L897: Rilzabrutinib-related minimal diffuse, follicular cell hypertrophy in females (4/15) at 300 mg/kg were observed in the thyroid. Follicular cell hypertrophy was not observed in males at any dose. The hypertrophy is likely linked to hepatic microsomal induction, as hepatocellular hypertrophy was also observed microscopically in females at the same dose. The transcriptomic data from rat liver spheroids confirmed that rilzabrutinib induces uridine 5'-diphospho-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) enzymes involved in thyroid hormone metabolism, supporting the proposed mechanism (hepatic microsomal enzyme induction) regardless of the atypical sex distribution. In addition, a single male at 50 mg/kg (3M41) exhibited minimal, focal, follicular cell hyperplasia, while another male (5M96) at 300 mg/kg had minimal, focal, C-cell hyperplasia. The link between these findings and rilzabrutinib remains unclear.
L898: In the brain, mixed cell inflammation in the meninges was seen in 300 mg/kg/day males and 150 and 300 mg/kg/day females and neutrophilic inflammation in the neuropil was seen in 150 and 300 mg/kg/day females. These findings were not noted during the recovery period. In addition, increased periventricular ionized calcium binding adapter protein 1 (IBA-1) positivity, indicative of microgliosis, was observed in 150 and 300 mg/kg/day males and 50, 150, and 300 mg/kg/day females. Increased periventricular glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP) positivity, indicative of increase in astrocytes, was observed in 150 and 300 mg/kg/day males and females. At the recovery necropsy, partial recovery of IBA-1 and GFAP staining positivity was observed.
L899: Hemorrhage/inflammation in stomach was seen in two 300 mg/kg/day males, two 150 mg/kg/day females and three 300 mg/kg/day females. Hemorrhage/inflammation was also noted in duodenum (one 300 mg/kg/day female) and esophagus (inflammation only, one 1500 mg/kg/day female and one 300 mg/kg/day female).
L900: Minimal hepatocyte hypertrophy was mainly noted at 150 and 300 mg/kg/day in both sexes. The hypertrophy was observed in centrlobular location, which is characteristic of and consistent with microsomal enzyme induction, as this zone contains the highest concentration of cytochrome P450 enzymes and is the primary site where drug-metabolizing enzymes are induced. While the minimal severity may appear relatively mild compared to classical enzyme inducers, the centrlobular distribution pattern supports the enzyme induction mechanism.
L901: The histopathology findings in stomach, duodenum, esophagus, and liver were resolved during the recovery period.
L902: Parameter	Major Findings
Toxicokinetics	TK results are shown in Table 48 below. Exposure was generally greater in females than in males. Exposure increased with dose, in a roughly greater than dose proportional manner. Slight drug accumulation was seen in males over repeat dosing.

L909: ## Study 1281-14255: 13-Week Oral GLP Toxicity Study of Rilzabrutinib in Rats With One-Month Recovery Period
L910: ## Key Study Findings
L911: - The NOAEL was conservatively determined at 50 mg/kg/day in males and females (Cmax=715 and 2690 ng/mL; AUClast=2170 and 3120 ng*h/mL, respectively) based on rilzabrutinib-related increased IBA-1 and GFAP staining in brain at ≥150 mg/kg/day, which can indicate neuroinflammation, neurodegeneration, and injury, but adverse histopathological findings in the brain were absent.
- Exposures at the NOAEL dose approximated the clinical exposures at the MRHD of 400 mg BID, based on AUC.
- Nonadverse microscopic findings were observed across multiple organ systems in the 13-week rat toxicity study, including the brain, thyroid, liver, lung, and thymus, primarily characterized by their minimal severity and reversible nature.
L912: ## Good Laboratory Practice Compliance
L913: Yes.

L918: **Table 50. Observations and Results of the 13-Week Oral (Gavage) Toxicity Study in Rats**
L919: Parameter	Major Findings
Mortality	No rilzabrutinib-related mortality except one toxicokinetic male (7M144) at 150 mg/kg/day found dead on Study Day 41.
Clinical signs	Dose-related clinical signs were wetness/staining around mouth and urogenital area noted mainly at 150, and 500 mg/kg/day. No clinical signs were noted during the recovery period.
Body weights	The administration of 500 mg/kg/day rilzabrutinib produced reduced body weight (14%) in males with transient reduction (23%) of food consumption (first two weeks). Lower dosages of rilzabrutinib did not significantly affect body weight or body weight change in either male or female rats throughout the study.
Hematology	There were no rilzabrutinib-related findings.
Coagulation	Rilzabrutinib-related reduced APTT (up to 21%) was noted at 150 mg/kg/day in males and 500 mg/kg/day in both males and females compared to vehicle controls. No group differences in coagulation parameters were observed at the end of the recovery period.
Clinical chemistry	While female rats administered rilzabrutinib showed elevated aspartate aminotransferase (AST) levels (up to 3.2X) across the dose groups, lacked corresponding increases in other liver enzymes, and had no histological correlates concluded that these AST elevations were not considered adverse. In addition, total bilirubin (TBIL) was decreased (1.75X) in females given 500 mg/kg/day rilzabrutinib at the end of dosing and this finding was resolved at the end of the recovery period.
Urinalysis, or flow cytometry parameters.	There were no rilzabrutinib-related findings.
Gross pathology	There were no rilzabrutinib-related findings.
L920: Parameter	Major Findings
Organ weights	
Rilzabrutinib-related increased absolute and relative weights (up to 33%) of liver were noted at 150 and 500 mg/kg/day in both males and females. Minimal microscopic centrilobular hypertrophy (1/10) observed at 500 mg/kg/day in males corresponds to the increased liver weight.
Decreased absolute and relative weights (up to 22%) at 500 mg/kg/day were observed in the thymus both males and females. In females, this finding corresponds with minimal decreased lymphocytes in the thymic cortex and medulla at 500 mg/kg/day. The thymic weight decrease, and lymphocyte depletion are most likely secondary effects related to stress rather than a direct pharmacological effect of rilzabrutinib on thymic tissue. This is a common finding in toxicology studies when animals experience systemic stress from high drug doses.

Histopathology
Adequate battery: yes	
Histopathology findings were noted in the thyroid, liver, thymus, brain, and lung.
Rilzabrutinib-related minimal diffuse follicular cell hypertrophy in the thyroid was observed in 7 out of 10 females in the 500 mg/kg/day group, characterized by reduced colloid in follicles and high-cuboidal to columnar follicular cells with vacuolated cytoplasm. This finding, likely an adaptive response to hepatic enzyme induction, was supported by increased liver weights in both sexes at 150 and 500 mg/kg/day doses. The female predominance likely reflects enhanced sensitivity in females to enzyme induction effects. In addition, females consistently showed ~1.5x higher systemic AUC exposures than males. The combination of higher exposure and enhanced sensitivity in females likely reached the threshold for detectable thyroid effects at 500 mg/kg/day, while males might require higher doses to achieve similar tissue-level enzyme induction effects.
Minimal hepatocellular hypertrophy was noted in one high dose (500 mg/kg/day) male.
One high dose (500 mg/kg/day) female showed decreased lymphocytes in cortex and medulla in thymus.
Increased IBA-1 immunopositivity of microglial cells were noted in females \ge50 mg/kg/day and males \ge150 mg/kg/day. Increased GFAP staining (astrocytes) was seen in all treated rats. In addition, one high dose (500 mg/kg/day) female showed minimal numbers of neutrophils seen in the meninges and perivascularly in the neuropil.
Increased alveolar macrophages with accumulations of mucus and mucosal papillary bronchiolar hyperplasia in 4/10 males and 9/10 females at 500 mg/kg/day were observed.
Most findings were resolved or tend to be resolved at the end of the recovery periods except minimal IBA-1 staining in one high dose (500 mg/kg/day) male and one mid dose female (150 mg/kg/day).
While these microscopic changes in the brain, thyroid, liver, lung, and thymus were rilzabrutinib-related, they were not considered adverse due to their minimal severity, adaptive nature, and evidence of reversibility during the recovery period.
L921: Parameter	Major Findings
Toxicokinetics	TK results are shown in Table 51 below. Exposure was generally greater in females than in males. Exposure increased with dose proportional manner. Slight drug accumulation was seen in males over repeat dosing.

L926: ## **Study 1281-16100: Nine-Month Oral GLP Toxicity Study of Rilzabrutinib in Beagle Dogs**
L927: ## **Key Study Findings**
L928: - Target organ of toxicity included liver and stomach.
- Microscopic findings included:Liver: minimal to mild Kupffer cell hypertrophy and increased Kupffer cell pigment at HD in both males and females suggestive of an adaptive immune response to injury and stress rather than acute injury (necrosis) observed in 13-week study.
- Stomach: mild increased intraepithelial lymphocytes with mucosal atrophy at HD in males indicating injury to the stomach's lining or inflammation and an immune response potentially associated with gastritis.
- The NOAEL was determined at 30 mg/kg/day in both males and females (C_{max}=257 and 339 ng/mL; AUC_{last}=747 and 812 ng*h/mL, respectively) based on rilzabrutinib-related adverse findings of body weight loss with decreased food consumption and subsequent mortality at 50 mg/kg/day. Exposures at the NOAEL dose approximated the clinical exposures at the MRHD of 400 mg BID in both males and females.

L946: ## **Study 1281-14256: 13-Week Oral GLP Toxicity Study of Rilzabrutinib in Beagle Dogs**
L947: ## **Key Study Findings**
L948: - Target organs of toxicity included liver and lymphoid tissues.
- Microscopic findings included:Liver: Minimal increased single cell necrosis, centrilobular hepatocyte vacuolation, and brown pigment in Kupffer cells (both sexes, most pronounced in females at HD; correlated with elevated ALT (↑7-14X) and total bilirubin (↑2-3X) at mid dose (MD) and HD in females. These findings suggest potential hepatocellular injury, with a more pronounced effect in females at higher doses.
- Lymphoid tissues: Minimal to moderate decreased lymphocyte numbers in mesenteric lymph nodes (reduced lymphocytes in germinal centers at MD and HD in both males and females, cervical lymph nodes (less consistently affected), and Peyer's patches (decreased lymphocytes across all dose levels, both sexes, affecting B and T lymphocyte compartments; small, poorly formed, or absent germinal centers; reduced cell numbers in T lymphocyte zones).
- These findings indicate a significant impact of rilzabrutinib on the immune system, particularly on lymphocyte populations. The widespread effect across different lymphoid tissues suggests a systemic immunomodulatory action.
- The NOAEL was determined at 30 mg/kg/day in both males and females (Cmax=592 and 484 ng/mL; AUClast=1070 and 1040 ng*h/mL, respectively) based on rilzabrutinib-related
L949: adverse findings of body weight loss with decreased food consumption and subsequent mortality at \ge 100 mg/kg/day. Exposures at the NOAEL dose approximated the clinical exposures at the MRHD of 400 mg BID, based on AUC.

L956: **Table 56. Observations and Results of the 13-Week Oral (Gavage) Toxicity Study in Dogs**
L957: Parameter	Major Findings
Mortality	Early terminations were noted at 100 mg/kg/day (2/10, 1 male and 1 female) and at 300 mg/kg/day (7/10, 4 males, 3 females) due to reduced food consumption (also anorexia and/or inappetence) and body weight reduction (\sim 20\% of Predose).
Mortality was first observed on Day 27 of the study period.
Clinical signs	Frequent observations of abnormal fecal quality (e.g., diarrhea) and emesis at \ge 100 mg/kg/day.
Body weights	Rilzabrutinib-related significant weight loss was observed in male dogs at \ge 100 mg/kg/day, leading to early euthanasia and necessitating food supplementation. Statistically significant decreases in food consumption observed in males administered 300 mg/kg.
Female dogs showed statistically significant weight reductions at Week 4 (\ge 300 mg/kg) and Week 6 (\ge 100 mg/kg), with a 10% decrease from control at \ge 100 mg/kg/day on Day 91. Statistically significant decreases in food consumption were observed at \ge 100 mg/kg.
Ophthalmology, ECG, Hematology, Coagulation, Urinalysis, Gross pathology, and Immunophenotyping (flow cytometry)	There were no rilzabrutinib-related findings.
Clinical chemistry	Rilzabrutinib-related increased ALT (\uparrow 7-14\times) and TBIL (\uparrow 2-3\times) were noted in female dogs at \ge 100 mg/kg/day. There was no supportive histopathological evidence for changes of ALT and total bilirubin levels. These findings were resolved at the end of the recovery periods.
L958: Parameter	Major Findings
Organ weights	
Rilzabrutinib-related decreased liver weight was noted in female dogs at \ge 100 mg/kg/day, which corresponded with the increasing ALT and TBIL and histopathological findings of single cell necrosis, centrilobular hepatocyte vacuolation, and brown pigment accumulation in Kupffer cells in liver.
Decreased spleen, thyroid, ovary, uterus, and thymus weights (absolute and relative) were noted at \ge 100 mg/kg/day.

Histopathology
Adequate battery: yes	
Histopathology findings were noted in the liver and lymphoid tissues.
Rilzabrutinib-related minimal increased single cell necrosis, centrilobular hepatocyte vacuolation, and brown pigment in Kupffer cells were noted in both males and females. However, these findings were most pronounced in females at 300 mg/kg/day, which correlated with elevated ALT and total bilirubin levels at \ge 100 mg/kg/day in females. These findings were resolved or tended to resolve by the end of the recovery period, except for minimal hepatocyte vacuolation in one female at 100 mg/kg/day.

Toxicokinetics	
Rilzabrutinib treatment resulted in decreased lymphocyte numbers in various lymphoid tissues. The mesenteric lymph nodes were more consistently affected than cervical lymph nodes, with reduced lymphocytes in germinal centers in both males and females at \ge 100 mg/kg/day. Peyer's patches showed decreased lymphocytes across all rilzabrutinib dose levels in both males and females, affecting both B and T lymphocyte compartments. Germinal centers were small, poorly formed, or absent, and T lymphocyte zones showed reduced cell numbers. These findings were resolved at the end of the recovery period.
TK results are shown in the Table 57 below. Exposure was generally greater in females than in males. Exposure increased with dose proportional manner. No drug accumulation was seen in both males and females over repeat dosing.

Human peripheral blood lymphocytes were treated with rilzabrutinib up to 45 \mug/mL with and without S-9 metabolic activation. L978: ## 13.1.8. Carcinogenicity Studies
L979: ## Study 1281-20014: Twenty-Six Week Oral GLP Carcinogenicity Study of Rilzabrutinib in Tg-RasH2 Mice
L980: ## Key Study Findings
L981: - Tg-RasH2 mice (25/sex/group) were administered rilzabrutinib at 0, 30, 100, or 300 mg/kg/day by oral gavage in 0.5% aqueous methylcellulose (400 cps) in deionized water for up to 6 months.
- There was no rilzabrutinib-related statistically significant dose response in mortality or increased incidence of neoplastic lesions in male and female Tg-RasH2 mice at doses up to 300 mg/kg/day.
- FDA statistical analysis did not identify imbalances in tumor incidence that were statistically significant by both trend and pairwise comparison.
- The positive control of urethane produced expected increases in tumors.
L982: ## Good Laboratory Practice Compliance
L983: Yes.
L984: ## Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee Conclusions
L985: - The Committee concluded that the carcinogenicity study was adequate.
- The Committee concluded that there was no evidence of drug-related neoplasms in male and female mice in the 6-month study in Tg-rasH2 mice.

L1000: ## 13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted With the New Drug Application
L1001: ## 13.2.1. Reproductive Toxicology
L1002: ## Study 20131082: Study of Fertility and Early Embryonic Development to Implantation of Rilzabrutinib Administered by Oral Gavage in Rats
L1003: ## Key Study Findings
L1004: - Mean body weights were significantly reduced in both male (\downarrow4-7%) and female (\downarrow2-4%) at 300 mg/kg/day with no effects on reproductive competence. In addition, a reduction in body weight gain was noted in both males (\downarrow27%) and females (\downarrow33%) at 300 mg/kg/day with corresponding reduction in food consumption in both males (\downarrow7%) and females (\downarrow8%) during the premating period, compared to control. In addition, the reduction in body weight gain was \downarrow28% in males during the entire dosing period.
- There were no rilzabrutinib-related effects on any reproductive parameters, including estrous cycling, mating, fertility, sperm parameters, or any ovarian and uterine parameters.
- The NOAEL for general toxicity was identified as 150 mg/kg/day, for both males and females, based on adverse effects on body weight in males and body weight gain in females noted at 300 mg/kg/day.
- The NOAEL for fertility and reproductive performance in males and the early embryonic development in females was identified as the high dose, 300 mg/kg/day, based on the absence of any adverse effects at the highest dose with exposures approximately 12x and 18x the human AUC exposure at the MRHD of 400 mg BID in male and females, respectively.
- Exposure multiples were based on AUC from 6-month rat study (#1281-16101) since PK data were not assessed in this study.

L1006: Yes.

L1012: **Table 63. Observations and Results of Fertility and Early Embryonic Developmental Study in Rats**
L1013: Parameter	Major Findings
Mortality	No rilzabrutinib-related death except one male at 50 mg/kg/day group euthanized on Day 6 due to adverse clinical signs and one female (9484) at 300 mg/kg/day on Day 11, whose deaths were not dose-dependent and were attributed to a dosing error.
Clinical signs	No rilzabrutinib-associated clinical signs except an increased incidence of rales in the 300 mg/kg/day dose group in males (5 animals on 1 or 2 days of the dose period beginning on Day 6).
Body weights	Mean body weights were significantly reduced in both male (\downarrow4%-7%) and female (\downarrow2%-4%) at 300 mg/kg/day (Figure 12). Mean body weight gains at the 300 mg/kg/day dose group were significantly reduced on Day 1-28 and Day 1-57 (up to 28%) in males (Figure 12 and Table 64). Rilzabrutinib-related reduced premating body weight gain on Day 1-15 (33%) was noted with statistical significance at 300 mg/kg/day (Figure 12 and Table 64). No rilzabrutinib-related effects on maternal body weights or maternal mean body weight gains at any dose level during the gestation period. Corresponding decrease in food consumption (9-28%) were observed during premating period (Day 1-4 and Day 4-8) and GD 0-7 in female rats.

L1020: Parameter	Major Findings
Spermatogenic evaluation	No rilzabrutinib-related effect on spermatogenesis endpoints, includes mean cauda epididymal sperm numbers, sperm motility, or epididymal weight. However, the caudal epididymal sperm density was significantly increased (20%-25%) in the 50 and 300 mg/kg/day dose groups, which was not considered adverse as this finding has no impact on sperm numbers and motility.
Estrous cycling, mating, and fertility	There were no rilzabrutinib-related effects on the number of days cohabitated prior to mating (2.1 to 2.4 days), the number of rats that mated (100%), or the fertility index (90.5% to 100%). In addition, there were no rilzabrutinib-related effects on any reproductive parameters, including estrous cycling, mating, fertility, or any ovarian and uterine parameters.

L1022: ## **Study AB20877-D: Rilzabrutinib Free Base, Dose Range-Finding Study by the Oral Route (Gavage) in the Pregnant Rat**
L1023: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1024: - Rilzabrutinib-related maternal toxicity in rats was associated with dose-related reductions in mean body weight gain (68%) corresponding to reduced food consumption (36%) during the dosing period (gestation day (GD) 6-18), with clinical changes (hypersalivation, abnormal foraging, decreased activity, and piloerection) observed at 500 mg/kg/day.
- There were no rilzabrutinib-related macroscopic findings noted at maternal necropsy examinations.
- The maternal NOAEL for rilzabrutinib is defined at 150 mg/kg/day (mean AUCinf of 15,300 ng*h/mL on GD 17) based on decreased body weight gain, food consumption, with clinical changes (hypersalivation, abnormal foraging, decreased activity, and piloerection) observed at 500 mg/kg/day, with exposures approximately 10-times the clinical AUC exposure at 400 mg BID.
- The NOAEL for fetal developmental and fetal external, visceral, or skeletal malformation or variations was 150 mg/kg/day (mean AUCinf of 15,300 ng*h/mL on GD 17), based on the increased incidence of postimplantation loss (25%), delayed ossification associated with a markedly lower (24%) mean fetal weight, and increased skeletal (scoliosis) and visceral malformations (abnormalities of major vessels, urogenital tract, and kidney) at 500 mg/kg/day, with exposures approximately 10-times the clinical AUC exposure at 400 mg BID.
L1025: ## **Good Laboratory Practice Compliance**
L1026: No.

L1031: **Table 66. Observations and Results of Oral DRF Embryo-Fetal Developmental Study in Rats**
L1032: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality	All survived
Clinical signs	Rilzabrutinib-related clinical signs included moderate to marked hypersalivation, abnormal foraging, decreased activity, and piloerection in all animals at 500 mg/kg/day. In addition, hypersalivation with or without abnormal foraging were noted in all females at 150 mg/kg/day.
Body weight and food consumption	
Administration of rilzabrutinib resulted in dose-related marked reduction in body weight (up to 15%) (Figure 13) and body weight gain (68%) during dosing period (GD 6-18) at 500 mg/kg/day. In addition, reduction in body weight gain (16%) during dosing period was noted at 150 mg/kg/day.
Rilzabrutinib-related marked food consumption reduction (36%) throughout dosing period, most pronounced during first 3 days (GD 6-9) (47%) were observed at 500 mg/kg/day.
There were no rilzabrutinib-related effects on lower mean maternal body weights, mean maternal body weight gains in the 50 mg/kg/day dose groups, and on lower mean maternal food consumption in the 50 and 150 mg/kg/day dose groups.
Figure 13. Administration of Rilzabrutinib at the High Dose (500 mg/Kg/Day) Resulted in Maternal Toxicity of Lower Mean Body Weights
Body weight (g)
Source: Applicant's submission

Necropsy findings/cesarean section data	No rilzabrutinib-related macroscopic findings at maternal necropsy. Gravid uterus weight was reduced (35%) in the 500 mg/kg/day group compared to control due to decreased mean live litter size and fetal weight (24%), while the 50 and 150 mg/kg/day groups showed no rilzabrutinib-related effects on gravid uterus weight. All groups achieved 100% pregnancy rates with viable fetuses at cesarean section, and pre-implantation data were comparable across groups. However, the 500 mg/kg/day group demonstrated higher postimplantation loss (25%) compared to concurrent control, resulting in reduced mean live litter size.
L1033: Parameters	Major Findings
Necropsy findings/offspring	Delayed ossification associated with a markedly lower (24%) mean fetal weight and increased skeletal (scoliosis) and visceral malformations (abnormalities of major vessels, urogenital tract, and kidney) occurred at a maternally toxic dose of 500 mg/kg/day.
L1034: Source: Applicant's study (Study #AB20877-D)
L1035: Abbreviations: DRF, developmental reproductive toxicity; GD, gestation day
L1036: ## Toxicokinetics
L1037: - AUC exposure increased with increasing dose in a more than dose-proportional manner between 50 and 150 mg/kg/day and in a less than dose-proportional manner between 150 and 500 mg/kg/day on both GD 6 and GD 17. Specifically, AUCinf increased approximately 4.3-fold when dose increased 3-fold (from 50 to 150 mg/kg/day) and only 1.5-fold when dose increased 3.3-fold (from 150 to 500 mg/kg/day) on GD 6. There were no signs of rilzabrutinib accumulation after repeated dosing.
- Tmax was consistently rapid, with maximum plasma concentrations observed between 0.25 and 0.5 hours after dosing across most treatment groups, except for females treated at 500 mg/kg/day on GD 6 (Tmax=1 hour).

L1042: ## **Study 20131080: An Embryo-Fetal Development Study of Rilzabrutinib by Oral Gavage in Rats**
L1043: ## Key Study Findings
L1044: - Rilzabrutinib-related maternal toxicity in rats was associated with lower mean maternal body weights (4%) and lower mean maternal food consumption (16%) at 300 mg/kg/day from the GD 7 to GD 21 but did not induce fetal developmental or toxic effects.
- There were no rilzabrutinib-related macroscopic findings noted at maternal necropsy examinations, and there were no rilzabrutinib-related effects on any ovarian and uterine parameters. In addition, there were no rilzabrutinib-related external, visceral, or skeletal fetal malformations or variations.
- The maternal NOAEL for rilzabrutinib is defined at 150 mg/kg/day (Cmax of 3470 and 2970 ng/mL and AUC(0-t) of 10600 and 18200 ng*h/mL on GD 7 and 17, respectively) based
L1045: on the lower mean maternal body weights, lower mean maternal body weight gains, and lower mean maternal food consumption at 300 mg/kg/day. Exposures at the maternal NOAEL were approximately 12-times the clinical AUC exposure at 400 mg BID.

- The NOAEL for fetal developmental and fetal external, visceral, or skeletal malformation or variations was 300 mg/kg/day (C_{max} of 3390 and 3260 ng/mL and AUC_{(0-t)} of 31200 and 15800 ng*h/mL on GD 7 and 17, respectively), based on the absence of any adverse effects at the highest dose with exposures approximately 10-times the clinical AUC exposure at 400 mg BID.

L1056: Source: Applicant's submission
L1057: Necropsy findings/cesarean section data	There were no rilzabrutinib-related effects on any ovarian or uterine parameter in dams at any dose level and falls within the historical control ranges.
Necropsy findings/offspring	There were no rilzabrutinib-related fetal body weight changes or external, visceral, or skeletal fetal malformations or variations observed. There was a statistically significant reduction (p<0.05) in the number of litters that had fetuses with an incompletely ossified squamosal bone. This finding was unrelated to rilzabrutinib because it reflected an overall increase in this observation in the control group.
In addition, there was a statistically significant increase (p<0.01) in the incidence of supernumerary thoracic rib pairs with associated statistically significant increases and decreases (p<0.01) in the mean numbers of thoracic and lumbar vertebrae at 300 mg/kg/day. This finding was considered unrelated to rilzabrutinib because there was no effect on the total number of presacral vertebrae, indicating a homeotic transformation rather than a true malformation.

L1059: ## Toxicokinetics
L1060: - AUC exposure increased with increasing dose in an approximately dose proportional manner on GD 7 and in a less than dose proportional manner (4-fold across a 6-fold dose increase from 50 to 300 mg/kg/day) on GD 17. C_{max} exposure increased in a less than dose proportional manner (approximately 1-fold increase across a 6-fold dose increase from 50 to 300 mg/kg/day) on both GD 7 and GD 17 days (Table 70). There were no signs of rilzabrutinib accumulation after repeated dosing.

L1066: ## **Study AB20878: Rilzabrutinib Free Base, Dose Range-Finding Study by the Oral Route (Gavage) in the Pregnant Rabbit**
L1067: A non-GLP dose range-finding study was conducted in pregnant New Zealand White rabbits to evaluate the embryo-fetal development effects of rilzabrutinib administered orally at 50, 100, and 150 mg/kg/day during organogenesis (Days 6 to 19 of gestation).
L1068: Malformations observed in the rabbit dose range-finding study were considered equivocal and not rilzabrutinib-related due to the lack of dose-response relationship and occurrence as isolated findings in single fetuses from individual litters. Given the absence of clear rilzabrutinib-related developmental effects, no further detailed discussion of malformation findings from this dose range-finding study is included in this developmental toxicity assessment.
L1069: ## **Study 20131081: An Embryo-Fetal Development Study of Rilzabrutinib by Oral Gavage in Rabbits**
L1070: ## Key Study Findings
L1071: - Rilzabrutinib-related maternal toxicity in rabbits was associated with lower mean maternal body weight gains (29%) and lower mean maternal food consumption (11%) compared to concurrent control at 100 mg/kg/day.
L1072: - There were no macroscopic findings noted at maternal necropsy examinations, and there were no rilzabrutinib-related effects on any ovarian and uterine parameters. In addition, there were no rilzabrutinib-related effects on embryo-fetal viability, fetal body weights, or fetal external, visceral, or skeletal morphology at any dose level.
- The maternal NOAEL for rilzabrutinib is defined at 30 mg/kg/day (C_{max} of 232 and 340 ng/mL and AUC_{(0-t)} of 175 and 313 ng\cdoth/mL on GD 7 and 19, respectively) based on lower body weight gain and food consumption at 100 mg/kg/day, which is approximately 0.2-times the clinical AUC exposure at 400 mg BID.
- The NOAEL for fetal developmental and fetal external, visceral, or skeletal malformation or variations in rabbit was defined at 100 mg/kg/day (C_{max} of 2240 and 5110 ng/mL and AUC_{(0-t)} of 3980 and 6920 ng\cdoth/mL on GD 7 and 19, respectively), based on the absence of any adverse effects at the highest dose, which is approximately 4.5-times the clinical AUC exposure at 400 mg BID.

L1086: ## **Study 00958013: An Oral (Gavage) Study of the Effects of Rilzabrutinib on Pre- and Postnatal Development, Including Maternal Function in Rats**
L1087: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1088: - Rilzabrutinib-related maternal toxicity in rats was associated with lower mean body weights, body weight gains, and food consumption during gestation compared to concurrent control at 150 and 300 mg/kg/day.
- Decreased mean body weight gain corresponds to the clinical observations includes increased occurrence of small stature in 22 pups from 12 litters in first filial (F1) male and female pups at 300 mg/kg/day during the preweaning period.
- There were no rilzabrutinib-related effects on F1 males and females at any dose.
- The NOAEL for the maternal (F0) toxicity was 50 mg/kg/day based on lower mean body weight gain, and food consumption during gestation compared to concurrent control at 150 and 300 mg/kg/day, which provides approximately 3.7-times the clinical AUC exposure at 400 mg BID.
- There were no direct rilzabrutinib-related F1 neonatal/developmental effects observed. The increased occurrence of small stature at 300 mg/kg/day was considered secondary to maternal toxicity (decreased body weight gains and food consumption) rather than direct developmental toxicity.
- The NOAELs for F1 parental toxicity, F1 reproductive toxicity, and F2 embryonic toxicity were 300 mg/kg/day, based on the absence of any adverse effects at the highest dose, which is approximately 18-times the clinical AUC exposure at 400 mg BID.
- Exposure multiples were based on AUC from 6-month rat study (#1281-16101) since PK data were not assessed in this study.
L1089: ## **Good Laboratory Practice Compliance**
L1090: Yes.

Body weight	Rilzabrutinib-related effects were observed in both male and female F1 pups (Figure 17 and Figure 18) at 300 mg/kg/day, provided evidence of significantly lower (9-9.3%) body weights in F1 pups compared to concurrent control. This trend persisted through postnatal day 1-21, with reductions of 14.9-23.5% in males and 12.8%-20.6% in females.

L1120: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology
L1121: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L1122: ## 14.1.1. Plasma Protein Binding
L1123: The plasma protein binding of rilzabrutinib at 1\mu\text{M}, determined in human plasma by using equilibrium dialysis method, averaged 97.5%. Similar high plasma protein binding, with unbound fractions ranging from 3.67% to 3.81%, was observed using rilzabrutinib at concentrations of 0.1, 1, 5 and 10\mu\text{M}.
L1124: ## 14.1.2. Distribution in Red Blood Cells
L1125: Rilzabrutinib was not preferentially partitioned into red blood cells (RBCs), with blood-to-plasma ratio of 0.47 at 5\mu\text{M}.
L1126: ## 14.1.3. Metabolism Studies
L1127: ## 14.1.3.1. Rilzabrutinib as Substrate
L1128: The metabolism of rilzabrutinib was studied in human liver fractions and recombinant microsomes.
L1129: Biotransformation of rilzabrutinib via oxidation, dealkylation, and/or hydrogenation was noted using human liver S9 fractions.
L1130: Incubation of rilzabrutinib with recombinant CYP enzymes suggested that rilzabrutinib is metabolized primarily by CYP3A4 and to a lesser extent by CYP2D6.
L1131: ## 14.1.3.2. Potential for CYP Enzyme Inhibition
L1132: Rilzabrutinib was evaluated as a direct inhibitor of CYP3A4 over the concentration range of 0.1 to 50\mu\text{M} in human liver microsomes (HLM).
L1133: Rilzabrutinib exhibited direct inhibition of CYP3A4/5 (\text{IC}_{50} 1.09\mu\text{M}; midazolam). The \text{IC}_{50}, k_{\text{inact}}, and K_I values were normalized with protein binding of rilzabrutinib to HLM. Estimated R_I and R_{I_{\text{gut}}} values suggest the potential for rilzabrutinib to cause DDIs resulting from inhibition of CYP3A4/5, but not other CYP enzymes.
L1134: The ability of rilzabrutinib to inhibit human liver uridine 5'-diphospho-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) 1A1 and sulfotransferase 1E1 enzymes was evaluated over the concentration range of 3 to 200\mu\text{M} in HLM. Rilzabrutinib exhibited inhibition of UGT1A1 and sulfotransferase 1E1, with an \text{IC}_{50} value of 6.46\mu\text{M} (alamethicin) and 28.9\mu\text{M} (estradiol), respectively. These results show the poor inhibitory potential of rilzabrutinib in comparison to measured clinical exposure, with a margin of more than 800-fold above the clinical C_{\text{max}} of unbound rilzabrutinib (\sim 5\text{ ng/mL}).
L1135: ## 14.1.3.3. Potential for CYP Enzyme Induction
L1136: Rilzabrutinib was evaluated as an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 expression in human hepatocytes. Treatment of cultured human hepatocytes with up to 30\muM rilzabrutinib caused increase in mRNA expression of CYP3A in one of the three donors. Calculated maximum drug-induced effect (E_{max}) was 4.4-fold, and concentration of drug that achieved half-maximal effect values for induction of CYP3A enzyme mRNA expression was 1.49\muM. Based on these *in vitro* data, rilzabrutinib has a calculated R3 value of 1.12, thus is a potential inducer of CYP3A.
L1137: ## 14.1.4. Transporter Characterization
L1138: ## 14.1.4.1. Rilzabrutinib as Substrate
L1139: To determine if rilzabrutinib is a substrate of P-gp, the bidirectional permeability of rilzabrutinib at 1\muM across Caco-2 cells was measured (Study DVR0501). Rilzabrutinib (\muM) had moderate permeability (apparent permeability [Papp], A\rightarrow B=1.713\times 10^{-6} cm/sec and Papp, B\rightarrow A=5.299\times 10^{-6} cm/sec) in Caco-2 cells and a concomitant moderate net flux ratio of 3.1 in the absence of a P-gp inhibitor. Overall, rilzabrutinib exhibited a flux ratio greater than 2 in this experiment, suggesting that it may be a potential substrate of P-gp efflux transporter expressed in Caco-2 cells.
L1140: Madin-Darby canine kidney (MDCK)- multidrug resistance mutation (MDR1) cells were also used to determine whether rilzabrutinib is a P-gp substrate at the concentration of 1\muM (Study DVR0091). Rilzabrutinib (1\muM) had moderate permeability in MDCK-MDR1 cells and a concomitant moderate net flux ratio of 4.3 in the absence of the P-gp inhibitor. The addition of the P-gp inhibitor reduced the net flux ratio by more than 50% to 0.8. These results indicate that rilzabrutinib may be a potential P-gp substrate.
L1141: To determine if rilzabrutinib is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP, the bidirectional transport of rilzabrutinib (1 to 100\muM) across Caco-2/TC7 cells was measured in the presence and absence of P-gp and BCRP inhibitors, PSC833 and fumitremorgin C (FTC), respectively (Study TRS0024). The efflux ratio decreased from 12.3 in the absence of inhibitor to 0.387 in the presence of PSC833 and to 3.51 in the presence of FTC. The suppression of transport asymmetry in the presence of PSC833 and the reduction of transport symmetry in the presence of FTC indicated that efflux transporters (P-gp and BCRP) are involved in rilzabrutinib transport.
L1142: Rilzabrutinib was investigated for its potential to be a substrate for organic anion transporter (OAT)1, OAT3, organic cation transporter (OCT)1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3 (in MDCK-II cells), BCRP (in Caco-2 cells), P-gp (in MDCK-MDR1), or BSEP (in Sf9 cell vesicles) (Study DVR0158). Results show that rilzabrutinib was not a substrate for OAT1, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, BSEP, or BCRP. At 10\muM rilzabrutinib, uptake was 13.8% higher in OAT3-transfected cells than in control cells, however, this difference was less than 2-fold, therefore rilzabrutinib was not considered to be a substrate of OAT3. The efflux ratios for P-gp were approximately 20 and 15 for rilzabrutinib at 1 and 10\muM, respectively, and the efflux ratio dropped to 1 upon the addition of the P-gp inhibitor verapamil. Therefore, these findings indicate that rilzabrutinib is a P-gp substrate.
L1143: Based on the above, rilzabrutinib is a substrate of P-gp.
L1144: ## 14.1.4.2. Potential Transporter Inhibition
L1145: The ability of rilzabrutinib to inhibit human ABC efflux transporters P-gp (ABCB1/MDR1), BCRP (ABCG2/MXR), and BSEP (ABCB11/SPGP) was evaluated (Study DVR0157). The ability of rilzabrutinib (3\muM) to inhibit the transport of reference substrates was assessed using MDCK-MDR1 cells expressing P-gp (digoxin), Caco-2 cells expressing BCRP (genistein), or Sf9 membrane vesicles containing human BSEP (taurocholate). The results indicate that rilzabrutinib did not inhibit P-gp or BCRP at 3\muM.
L1146: The ability of rilzabrutinib to inhibit human uptake transporters OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3 was evaluated indirectly by assessing accumulation of probe substrates in MDCK-II cells overexpressing individual uptake transporters (Study DVR0157). The results indicate that rilzabrutinib did not inhibit OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, or OATP1B3 at 3\muM.
L1147: Rilzabrutinib (0.008 to 26\muM) was evaluated as an inhibitor of the solute carrier transporters OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, multidrug and toxin extrusion protein (MATE)1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3 by measuring the inhibition of substrate uptake after incubation with HEK293 cells (Study TRI0053). The results suggest that rilzabrutinib is an inhibitor of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, OCT1, OCT2, OAT1, OAT3, and OCT2 with IC50 values of 0.705\muM, 0.180\muM, 2.9\muM, 6.71\muM, 2.93\muM, 5.36\muM, 22.2\muM, and 36.4\muM, respectively. Inhibitory potential of rilzabrutinib of efflux transporters (BCRP and BSEP) was also assessed in this study. Results show that rilzabrutinib has the ability to inhibit BCRP and BSEP with IC50 values of 7.61\muM and 1.42\muM, respectively.
L1148: P-gp was found to be inhibited by rilzabrutinib with IC50&lt;10\muM in study report 0055.
L1149: Based on the comparison of calculated Igut/IC50 values, R values with the current FDA recommended thresholds, there is potential for DDIs with P-gp, BCRP, and OATP1B3 substrates. Rilzabrutinib is also an inhibitor of BSEP.
L1150: The clinically relevant effect of rilzabrutinib on the systemic exposures of transporter substrates was investigated via PBPK modeling and simulations (Study PBS0017).
L1151: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1152: ## 14.2.1. Study PRN1008-001: a Phase 1, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Ascending-Dose Study of the Safety, Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics of Orally Administered Rilzabrutinib
L1153: ## Study Design
L1154: This was a two-part study. Part A contained a total of 40 healthy participants, 8 participants per cohort (6 active/2 placebo) were treated with single dose of rilzabrutinib oral liquid formulation (50, 150, 300, 600, and 1200 mg) or placebo. Blood samples were collected at predose and up to
L1155: ## NDA 219685
## WAYRILZ (rilzabrutinib)
L1156: 24 hours postdose for the measurement of plasma concentrations of rilzabrutinib. Blood samples were also drawn at 4, 12 and 24 hours postdose to measure BTK occupancy in peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs). Part B contained a total of 40 healthy participants, 10 participants per cohort (8 active/2 placebo) were treated with repeat doses of rilzabrutinib oral liquid formulation (300 mg QD, 300 mg BID, 600 mg QD and 450 mg BID) or matching placebo QD for 10 days. Blood samples were collected at predose and up to 24 hours postdose on dosing Day -1, 1 and 10 for the measurement of plasma concentrations of rilzabrutinib. Blood samples were also drawn at 4, 12, 24 and 36 hours postdose on Day 1 and Day 10 to measure BTK occupancy in PBMCs. All doses were administered orally after a 10-hour fast, and participants continued to fast until 4 hours postdose.
L1157: ## **Results**
L1158: In Part A, following single oral doses of a liquid formulation of rilzabrutinib, the mean plasma C_{\text{max}} and \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} increased close to or more than dose-proportionally from 50 mg to 600 mg. Increasing the dose from 600 mg to 1200 mg did not result in further increases in C_{\text{max}} or \text{AUC}_{0-\infty}, suggesting a plateau in absorption with doses greater than 600 mg with the liquid formulation administered in this study. The median T_{\text{max}} of rilzabrutinib increased from 0.5 to 2.5 hours as doses increased from 50 mg to 1200 mg. The mean apparent terminal elimination half-life of rilzabrutinib was approximately 3 to 4 hours. Measurement of BTK occupancy showed a dose-dependent increase of BTK occupancy. The maximum observed response was at 4 hours postdose and a plateau of response was reached with the 600 mg dose (mean of 92%).
L1159: In Part B, following multiple oral doses of rilzabrutinib on Day 10, the mean plasma \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} of rilzabrutinib in plasma increased in a dose-proportional fashion over a dose range of 300 mg to 600 mg. The mean apparent terminal elimination half-life ranged from 3.8 to 4.5 hours for all cohorts on Day 10 and did not appear to change with the different dosing regimen. Minimal accumulation (&lt;2-fold) was observed after multiple oral dose administration. Following multiple oral doses of rilzabrutinib, mean BTK occupancy in PBMCs of 91% was observed 4 hours after the final dose for all but the 300 mg QD regimen, and approximately 55-60% was observed 24 hours later.
