L1170: ## **Summary**
L1171: Using the liquid formulation, rilzabrutinib exposure increased in a near dose proportional manner (50 to 600 mg). No additional increase in exposure was observed when doses were increased beyond 600 mg. Rilzabrutinib mean plasma half-life ranged from approximately 3 to 4.5 hrs. and showed modest accumulation between 50 to 80% when dosed twice daily over 10 days.
L1172: Maximum BTK occupancy (approximately 90%) was achieved with daily doses of 600 mg and above.
L1173: ## 14.2.2. Study PRN1008-015: a Two Part-Phase 1, Open-Label Study of the Absorption, Metabolism, Excretion and Absolute Bioavailability of [14C]-PRN1008 in Healthy Male Participants
L1174: ## **Study Design**
L1175: This was a two-part study. In Part A, 8 participants received a single oral dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib immediate release tablet followed 2 hours later by an intravenous (IV) administration of 100 µg [14C]-rilzabrutinib (containing about 1 µCi), as an IV push over 2 minutes. Blood samples were collected predose and up to 72 hours postdose for determination of plasma concentrations of rilzabrutinib and [14C]-rilzabrutinib. In Part B, 10 participants received a single oral dose of 300 mg [14C]-rilzabrutinib as a liquid formulation (containing about 1000 µCi). All participants had blood, urine, and feces collection. Two of the 10 participants also had bile sample collected. All administrations were given after a 10-hour fast, and participants continued to fast until 4 hours postdose.
L1176: ## **Results**
L1177: In Part A, after a 400 mg oral dose followed by an IV injection of 100 µg [14C]-rilzabrutinib, rilzabrutinib clearance was estimated to be 58.1 L/h. Volume of distribution at terminal phase after IV administration was estimated to be 149 L. Oral bioavailability of rilzabrutinib was estimated to be 4.73%. The median t_{max} was approximately 2.0 hours and geometric mean t_{1/2} was 3.2 h.
L1178: In Part B, following oral administration of [14C]-rilzabrutinib, rapid absorption of rilzabrutinib was observed with a median t_{max} value of 1.0 hour in plasma, while maximal levels of total radioactivity in plasma and blood were reached with a median t_{max} of 3.5 hours postdose. After reaching C_{max}, plasma concentrations of rilzabrutinib declined in a biphasic manner, with a geometric mean t_{1/2} of 2.5 hours (range: 1.2 to 9.0 hours). In contrast, for total radioactivity, the terminal t_{1/2} in plasma and blood was 182 hours and 176 hours, respectively. The mean AUC_{last} of rilzabrutinib in plasma was approximately 0.3% of that of the total radioactivity in plasma. Data of this study demonstrated that rilzabrutinib undergoes extensive metabolism, mediated primarily by dealkylation, oxidative N-dealkylation, and oxidation. The metabolism produced a total of 46 radiolabeled components, of which, 25 metabolites were identified or characterized. Rilzabrutinib was a trace radiolabeled component in plasma and accounted for 0.760% of total radioactivity. PRN4400 (thiocyanate, M1) was identified as the primary circulating metabolite representing 94.2% of total radioactivity exposure in plasma. PRN834 (M45), a previously characterized metabolite, accounted for 1.09% of total plasma radioactivity exposure. The other metabolites were identified as trace amount (&lt;1%) metabolites in plasma. The overall mean recoveries of radioactivity in urine, bile (as applicable), feces, and vomitus samples were 92.9% (non-bile collection participants) and 87.6% (bile collection participants). [14C]-Rilzabrutinib-derived radioactivity was predominantly excreted in feces (approximately 86% of dose) and to a lesser extent in urine (approximately 5% of dose, total radioactivity) and bile (approximately 6%
L1179: of dose). Rilzabrutinib was the most abundant radiolabeled component in feces, accounting for a mean of 9.02% of the dose in non-bile collection participants. Approximately 0.03% of dose was recovered as unchanged drug in urine. Rilzabrutinib metabolites accounted for approximately 3.8% of the dose in urine, approximately 4.2% of the dose in bile, and approximately 63.0% of the dose in feces. The prevalence of metabolites in feces indicates that metabolism is the major mechanism of elimination of rilzabrutinib in humans.
L1180: ## Summary
L1181: The mass balance data indicate that rilzabrutinib is rapidly absorbed and undergoes extensive metabolism. Renal excretion is not the major elimination pathway for rilzabrutinib.
L1182: ## **14.2.3. Study PRN1008-020: an Open-Label, Phase 1 Study To Evaluate the Effect of Mild to Moderate Hepatic Impairment on the Single-Dose Pharmacokinetics of Rilzabrutinib**
L1183: ## Study Design
L1184: The primary objective of this study was to evaluate the PK profile of rilzabrutinib following a single oral tablet dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib in participants with mild and moderate HI relative to control participants with normal hepatic function. The study population consisted of male and female participants who were \ge 18 to \le 75 years, with mild HI (Group 1; 8 participants, Child-Pugh Class A, score of 5 to 6), moderate HI (Group 2; 8 participants; Child-Pugh Class B, score of 7 to 9) and normal hepatic function (Group 3; 13 participants). Participants with normal hepatic function were to be matched by age (\pm 10 years), sex and body mass index (BMI; \pm 15\%) to participants with HI. Rilzabrutinib tablet of 400 mg under fasted condition was administered after a 10-hour fast, and participants continued to fast until 4 hours postdose. Blood samples for analysis of rilzabrutinib in plasma were collected predose and up to 30 hours postdose. Blood samples to determine unbound fraction of rilzabrutinib were collected predose and up to 24 hours postdose.
L1185: ## Results
L1186: Following treatment with rilzabrutinib, the geometric least squares means (LSM) and the ratios of HI/control group for the primary PK parameters, plasma AUC and C_{max}, of total rilzabrutinib are presented in Table 81. The geometric mean ratios (90% CI) expressed as percentages in mild and moderate HI groups compared to control for C_{max} were 153% (76%, 309%) and 423% (224%, 798%); for AUC_{(0-t)} were 153% (76%, 308%) and 484% (265%, 883%), and for AUC_{(0-inf)} were 135% (66%, 275%) and 448% (219%, 916%), respectively. Following a single oral dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib, C_{max}, AUC_{(0-t)}, and AUC_{(0-inf)} were modestly higher in the mild HI group compared to the normal hepatic function group and significantly higher (&gt;4-fold) in the moderate HI group compared to the normal hepatic function group.
L1187: Rilzabrutinib was highly bound to plasma proteins across all cohorts, with mean percent unbound (%fu) of &lt;3% across the cohorts and did not increase significantly in participants with HI (Table 82). Changes in rilzabrutinib exposure based on unbound rilzabrutinib is similar to what was observed with total rilzabrutinib.

L1198: ## 14.2.4. Study PRN1008-025: a Randomized, Open-Label Phase I Study, To Assess the Effects of Food and Formulation on the Pharmacokinetics of a Single Dose of Rilzabrutinib in Healthy Male and Female Participants
L1199: ## Study Design
L1200: This was a two-part study. Only Part A, the food effect study, is under review. A total of 24 participants participated in a 2-period crossover study in which participants received a single 400 mg dose of the immediate release (IR) tablet formulation of rilzabrutinib in the fed and fasted states. Treatment A: fasted treatment, participants received rilzabrutinib with 240 mL of water following an overnight fast of 10 hours, food was also restricted until 4 hours after dosing. Treatment B: fed treatment, participants fasted overnight for approximately 10 hours, then ate a high-fat breakfast 30 minutes prior to study drug administration. Administration of each treatment were separated by a washout period of 4 days. Blood samples for analysis of rilzabrutinib in plasma were collected predose and up to 24 hours postdose on Day 1 and Day 6.
L1201: ## Results
L1202: Following coadministration of 400 mg IR tablet formulation with a high-fat meal or rilzabrutinib, the ratio of geometric LSM (Fed/Fasted [B/A]) and 90% CI were 81.75% (69.40% to 96.29%), 78.70% (67.27% to 92.06%) and 69.04% (55.36% to 86.11%) for AUC0-last, AUC0-inf, and Cmax, respectively. The intraparticipant variability (coefficient of variation (CV))3 were 34.0%, 31.6%, and 46.9% for AUC0-last, AUC0-inf, and Cmax, respectively.

L1207: ## Summary
L1208: The design of this food effect study is acceptable with a standard high-fat meal and sufficiently long washout period. The tablet formulation used in this food effect study is the same as used in the pivotal Phase 3 study and to-be-marketed product. Rilzabrutinib total exposure was lower by about 20%, and Cmax was lower by approximately 30%, compared to the fasting conditions. The food effect was minimal and considered not clinically significant. This study guided instructions
L1209: for administering rilzabrutinib in relation to meals for study participants (taken with or without food).
L1210: ## 14.2.5. Study PRN1008-022: a Phase 1, Single-Center, Open-Label Study, To Evaluate the Pharmacokinetics, Safety and Tolerability of Rilzabrutinib in Chinese Healthy Male and Female Participants
L1211: ## Study Design
L1212: Ten healthy Chinese male and female participants, aged between 18 to 45 years, enrolled in this open-label study to investigate the PK, safety, and tolerability of a single dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib in the IR tablet formulation. Rilzabrutinib was given after an overnight fast. Blood samples were collected at predose and 24 hours postdose for plasma quantitation of rilzabrutinib.
L1213: ## Results
L1214: Following a single oral administration of 400 mg rilzabrutinib, rilzabrutinib was absorbed with a median t_{max} of 1.5 hours and eliminated with a mean t_{1/2} of 3.1 hours. The mean systemic exposure parameters for rilzabrutinib, C_{max} and AUC_{last}, were 221 ng/mL and 614 h*ng/mL, respectively. Note that the half-life in Chinese participants is shorter compared with participants of other races.

L1221: ## **14.2.6. Study PRN1008-023: a Phase 1, Single-Center, Open-Label Study To Evaluate the Pharmacokinetics and Tolerability of Rilzabrutinib in Japanese and Caucasian Healthy Male and Female Participants**
L1222: ## Study Design
L1223: Eleven healthy Japanese male and female participants and 12 healthy Caucasian male and female participants, aged between 18 to 45 years, enrolled in this open-label study to evaluate the PK of rilzabrutinib following single and multiple doses. Japanese participants had three sequential treatment periods separated by 4-day washout periods: 100 mg single dose on Day 1, 200 mg single dose on Day 5 and 400 mg BID from Day 9 to 13, and Caucasian participants had one treatment period (400 mg BID from Day 1 to 5). All doses were administered as IR tablets after an overnight fast. Blood samples were collected at predose and for up to 24 hours postdose (for 100 mg and 200 mg) or up to 48 hours postdose (for 400 mg BID, on Day 13 in Japanese group and Day 5 in Caucasian group) for plasma quantitation of rilzabrutinib. Additional blood samples were collected predose and postdose after single dose and multiple doses, to assess the BTK occupancy in PBMCs.
L1224: ## Results
L1225: Following administration of the first dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib to Japanese and Caucasian participants, slightly higher (16%) C_{max} of rilzabrutinib was observed for Japanese participants, while AUC_{last} and AUC_{tau} was slightly lower (6.5%) in Japanese participants versus Caucasian participants (Table 85). Following multiple dose administration of 400 mg BID, Japanese participants had lower C_{max} and AUC values (16% and 25%) of rilzabrutinib compared to Caucasian participants.

L1230: At 400 mg BID, the mean BTK occupancies at steady state were comparable between the Japanese and Caucasian participants.
L1231: ## Summary
L1232: Total variability for PK measurements was relatively high in both race groups, with CV% generally &gt;70%. Variability for PD measurements was also high for Japanese participants.
L1233: ## **14.2.7. Study PRN1008-002: a Phase 1, Single-Center, Open-Label, Fixed-Sequence Study To Assess the Effects of Rilzabrutinib on the Pharmacokinetics of Midazolam, a CYP3A4 Substrate, in Healthy Adults**
L1234: ## Study Design
L1235: This is a Phase 1, open-label study to evaluate DDI of rilzabrutinib with midazolam (a CYP3A4 substrate) in 12 healthy adult participants aged 18 to 55.
L1236: The primary objective was to assess the effect of a single oral dose of rilzabrutinib (600 mg, liquid formulation) on the pharmacokinetics of a single oral dose of midazolam (2 mg). A single 2-mg oral dose of midazolam solution was administered alone on Day 1 under fasting condition, and again following a three-day washout period, on Day 4, one hour after a single oral dose of 600 mg rilzabrutinib as a liquid formulation. Blood samples were obtained over a period of 24 hours post-midazolam dosing to characterize the PK profile of midazolam and \alpha-hydroxymidazolam and rilzabrutinib.
L1237: ## Results
L1238: Systemic exposure of midazolam was increased when coadministered with rilzabrutinib. Statistical analysis comparing the effect of rilzabrutinib 600 mg on C_{max} and AUC values of midazolam are shown in Table 86. The geometric mean ratios for midazolam AUC(0-inf), AUC(0-last), and C_{max} when coadministered with rilzabrutinib compared to midazolam administered alone were 314%, 360%, and 241%, respectively.

L1242: ## Summary
L1243: Rilzabrutinib was not dosed to reach steady state in this study. However, the oral liquid formulation of rilzabrutinib has higher bioavailability compared with the tablet formulation, and
L1244: rilzabrutinib is dosed at 600 mg in this study. It is also noted that accumulation of rilzabrutinib when dose twice daily at 400 mg is only about 50%. Taken together, the results from this study indicate that rilzabrutinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor following clinically relevant doses.
L1245: ## 14.2.8. Study PRN1008-006: Relative Bioavailability and Effect of Food Esomeprazole on the Pharmacokinetics of Rilzabrutinib in Healthy Volunteers
L1246: ## Study Design
L1247: This is a Phase 1, open-label, four-period, crossover study to evaluate the relative bioavailability of a single oral dose of rilzabrutinib when administered as a liquid formulation compared to a tablet formulation in the fasted state, to compare the impact of food on the pharmacokinetics of a tablet formulation of rilzabrutinib, and to evaluate the effect of a PPI (esomeprazole) on the pharmacokinetics of rilzabrutinib tablet formulation. The food effect part of this study is not under review since the tablet formulation used is not the commercial formulation.
L1248: Twelve healthy adult participants received 300 mg doses of two formulations under fasted (liquid and IR tablet) or fed (IR tablet only) conditions as the first 3 treatments in a randomized manner. The final treatment involved the administration of esomeprazole (40 mg QD) for 4 days and coadministration with a single dose of rilzabrutinib (300 mg IR tablet) on the fifth day after an overnight fast. Esomeprazole was administered 2 hours before the administration of rilzabrutinib. Blood samples were collected at predose and over 24 hours following rilzabrutinib dose to determine plasma concentrations of rilzabrutinib.
L1249: ## Results
L1250: Bioavailability of rilzabrutinib from the tablet formulation was lower relative to the liquid formulation. The geometric mean ratios for AUC_{0-\infty}, AUC_{0-\text{last}} and C_{\text{max}} were 70.8%, 70.5%, and 69.6% for the tablet formulation relative to the liquid formulation, respectively, under fasted conditions.
L1251: The geometric mean ratios for rilzabrutinib AUC_{\text{inf}}, AUC_{\text{last}}, and C_{\text{max}} were 49.1%, 47.9%, and 45.4%, respectively, for rilzabrutinib administered with esomeprazole compared to rilzabrutinib administered alone.

L1257: Rilzabrutinib exposure decreased by about 50% in the presence of a PPI (esomeprazole).
L1258: ## **14.2.9. Study PRN1008-011: a Phase 1 Study of the Relative Bioavailability of Two Rilzabrutinib Tablet Formulations, Effect of Rilzabrutinib on Midazolam Pharmacokinetics, and Impact of Famotidine on Rilzabrutinib Pharmacokinetics in Healthy Participants**
L1259: ## Study Design
L1260: This is a single-center, 4-period, open-label, randomized, crossover study to evaluate the potential DDI of rilzabrutinib with a H2-receptor antagonist (famotidine) as a precipitant drug, and with a CYP3A substrate (midazolam) as a substrate drug. The relative bioavailability part of this study is not under review.
L1261: 14 healthy adult male and female participants received a single 2 mg oral dose of midazolam (1 mg/mL solution) on Day -1. Rilzabrutinib treatment administration occurred as per the assigned sequence on Days 1, 3, 5 and 7 involving administration of one of the following treatments:
L1262: - Treatment 1: Single 400 mg oral dose of rilzabrutinib (reference IR tablet) with simultaneous administration of a single 2 mg oral dose of midazolam.
- Treatment 2: Single 400 mg oral dose of rilzabrutinib (Test Formulation #1) with simultaneous administration of a single 2 mg oral dose of midazolam.
- Treatment 3: Single 400 mg oral dose of rilzabrutinib (Test Formulation #2) followed by a single 2 mg oral dose of midazolam 2 hours later.
- Treatment 4: Participants received single 20 mg oral doses of famotidine in the evening of Days 5, 6, and 7. On Day 7, participants received two 400 mg oral doses of rilzabrutinib (IR tablet), one in the morning (after an overnight fast) and a second in the evening (approximately 12 hours after the morning dose). Participants then received a final famotidine dose 2 hours after the evening rilzabrutinib dose.
L1263: Blood samples were collected over 12 hours postdosing for determination of rilzabrutinib and midazolam PK.
L1264: Note that Test Formulation # 2 is most relevant to the commercial formulation.
L1265: ## Results
L1266: When administered simultaneously with single dose 400 mg rilzabrutinib IR tablet formulation (treatment 1), the midazolam geometric mean ratio for AUC_{0-inf} was 168% (90% CI: 144 to 195%) and for C_{max} was 141% (90% CI: 118 to 168%). Similar results were observed when midazolam was administered simultaneously with Test Formulation #1, with the geometric mean ratio for AUC_{0-inf} 172% (90% CI: 148 to 200%) and C_{max} 140% (90% CI: 117 to 168%). When
L1267: midazolam was administered 2 hours post-rilzabrutinib dosing, the geometric mean ratios of midazolam exposure for AUC_{0-inf} and C_{max} were 217% (90% CI: 187 to 252%) and 223% (90% CI: 187 to 267%), respectively, compared to midazolam alone.
L1268: Dosing rilzabrutinib in the morning following 20 mg famotidine administered the previous evening resulted in a reduction in the rilzabrutinib AUC_{inf} and C_{max} by 33.2% and 25.8%, respectively. When rilzabrutinib was administered in the evening, two hours prior to the evening famotidine dose, AUC_{inf} and C_{max} were reduced by only 2.5% and 8.1%, respectively (Table 88).

L1273: ## Summary
L1274: Rilzabrutinib was not dosed to steady state in this study. Even then, based on the result from this study and study PRN1008-002, rilzabrutinib increases the exposure of midazolam. Administration of famotidine two hours after the administration of evening dose rilzabrutinib did not significantly impact the exposure of the next evening dose, however, could decrease rilzabrutinib morning dose exposure by roughly 35%.
L1275: ## 14.2.10. Study PRN1008-014: a Two-Part Study To Assess the Effect of Ritonavir on Rilzabrutinib Pharmacokinetics, and the Effects of Rilzabrutinib on QTc Interval Compared to Placebo and Moxifloxacin in Healthy Participants
L1276: ## Study Design
L1277: This is a single center, 2-part study in healthy participants. Part A was an open-label, 3-period crossover study in 12 adult participants to assess the safety and pharmacokinetics of single oral doses of rilzabrutinib when given alone (100 mg and 1200 mg, as 4x300 mg tablets) and when coadministered with ritonavir (100 mg rilzabrutinib with concomitant 100 mg ritonavir, with second dose of 100 mg ritonavir 12 hours later). Part B was a double-blind, placebo-controlled, 4-way crossover study with a 7-day washout between treatments. Thirty-nine participants received the first 3 treatments in a blinded manner (placebo, rilzabrutinib 400 mg single dose, and rilzabrutinib 400 mg single dose with two doses of 100 mg concomitant ritonavir 12 hours apart), followed by the 4th treatment in an open-label manner (moxifloxacin 400 mg single dose). Blood samples were collected for pharmacokinetics at predose and over 24 hours postdose for the determination of rilzabrutinib and moxifloxacin (open-label treatment only) concentrations.
L1278: The QT portion of this study is under review by the integrate review team, and report will be uploaded accordingly.
L1279: ## Results
L1280: Part A: Coadministration with ritonavir with 100 mg rilzabrutinib increased rilzabrutinib plasma AUC by approximately 18-fold, and C_{max} by approximately 8-fold (Table 89). When rilzabrutinib was administered alone at 1200 mg, three of the first four participants demonstrated significant gastrointestinal adverse events, and further dosing was stopped. Therefore, the 1200 mg dose of rilzabrutinib was considered insufficient to obtain adequate supratherapeutic exposures and coadministration of a lower dose of rilzabrutinib (400 mg) with ritonavir was chosen for ECG assessments in Part B.

L1285: Part B: Coadministration of the therapeutic 400 mg rilzabrutinib dose with ritonavir increased rilzabrutinib AUC by approximately 8-fold and Cmax by approximately 5-fold (Table 90). Results showed that the mean rilzabrutinib exposure following 1200 mg dose was lower than the exposure obtained following interaction with ritonavir (400 mg with 100 mg BID ritonavir), further supporting the dosing strategy for the QT prolongation analysis.

L1290: ## Summary
L1291: Study results confirmed rilzabrutinib is a substrate for CYP3A4 and/or P-gp. The study results also demonstrated that the effect of ritonavir on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics was dependent on the dose of the substrate, as the magnitude of the drug interaction decreased with the increase in rilzabrutinib dose from 100 mg in Part A to 400 mg in Part B.
L1292: ## **14.2.11. Study PRN1008-024: an Open-Label, Phase 1, Two-Part Drug-Drug Interaction Study To Evaluate the Effects of Quinidine and Rifampin on the Single-Dose Pharmacokinetics and Safety or Rilzabrutinib in Healthy Male and Female Volunteers**
L1293: ## Study Design
L1294: This is an open-label, two-period, fixed-sequence study to evaluate the effect of a P-gp inhibitor, quinidine (Part A), and a CYP3A inducer, rifampin (Part B), on the pharmacokinetics of rilzabrutinib. Thirty-two healthy adult participants participated in two parts (16 participants per part).
L1295: Part A evaluated the effect of quinidine on single dose rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics. On Period 1 Day 1 morning, participants received a single oral dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib following an overnight fast. In Period 2, 300 mg quinidine once every 8 hours (Q8h) was administered for 5 days from Day 7 through Day 11. On Day 10 morning (following an overnight fast), a single oral dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib was administered approximately 1 hour after the first 300 mg quinidine dose.
L1296: Part B evaluated the effect rifampin on single dose rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics. On Period 1 Day 1 morning, participants received a single oral dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib following an overnight fast. In Period 2, 600 mg rifampin QD was administered for 11 days from Day 7 through Day 17. On Day 16 morning (following an overnight fast), a single oral dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib was coadministered with 600 mg rifampin.
L1297: For both parts, blood samples were collected at predose and over 48 hours in each period for the determination of plasma concentrations of rilzabrutinib and PRN834 (M45).
L1298: ## Results
L1299: Part A: After coadministration of rilzabrutinib with quinidine, a strong P-gp inhibitor, a modest increase in exposure of rilzabrutinib was observed by 12.7% and 6.9% for AUC_{inf} and AUC_{last}, respectively, relative to rilzabrutinib alone. The C_{max} of rilzabrutinib was decreased by 13.6% after coadministration of rilzabrutinib and quinidine, compared to rilzabrutinib only.

L1309: ## Summary
L1310: The effect of P-gp inhibitor, quinidine, on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics was modest and not clinically significant. Rifampin, a strong CYP3A inducer, significantly decreased the exposure of rilzabrutinib, confirming the involvement of CYP3A in the metabolism of rilzabrutinib. The
L1311: substantial decreases seen in PRN834 (M45) exposure with rifampin treatment, suggests that its formation from rilzabrutinib is not mainly mediated by CYP3A. Unfortunately, the measurement of PRN4400 (M1), the major metabolite, was not available for all participants.

L1326: ## 14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of Pharmacokinetics/Pharmacodynamics, Efficacy, and Safety
L1327: Not applicable.
L1328: ## 14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
L1329: ## 14.5.1. Summary of Applicant's Population Pharmacokinetics and Pharmacokinetic Modeling Analyses
L1330: Population pharmacokinetic analysis was conducted to support the dose selection of rilzabrutinib for adult participants in ITP. The objectives of Applicant's modeling analyses were:
L1331: - To characterize rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics using pooled data from healthy and ITP participants.
- To assess the influence of selected intrinsic and extrinsic factors on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics, in healthy volunteer and ITP population.
- Generate individual rilzabrutinib post hoc PK exposures in ITP participants that can be used to quantify exposure-response relationships with efficacy and safety endpoints.
L1332: ## Method
L1333: Pooled data from Phase 1-3 studies in adult participants with ITP after subcutaneous administration of rilzabrutinib were included in the analysis. NONMEM version 7.5.1 was used for all model estimations. The covariate analysis was performed using a stepwise selection procedure (stepwise covariate model building within the PsN tool (Lindbom et al. 2025))
L1334: including forward selection and backwards exclusion procedure. The sensitivity analysis was performed to assess the robustness of selected covariates through a set of model evaluations, such as visual predictive checks and bootstrap. After model validation, the final PopPK model was used to generate individual PK parameters and post hoc exposures in participants with ITP from 2 clinical studies (LUNA 2 and EFC17093). Simulations were conducted to assess the impact of statistically significant covariates on rilzabrutinib exposure in participants with ITP.
L1335: ## **Results**
L1336: A total of 7076 rilzabrutinib concentration records from 545 adult participants (255 healthy participants and 290 participants ITP) were included in the dataset from 10 clinical studies in healthy participants and two clinical studies with participants with ITP. Table 96 summarizes the clinical studies included for this analysis. A two-compartment model with linear elimination and first order absorption with identical interindividual variability between clearance and central volume of distribution and including an interindividual variability on the residual error was confirmed as the final PopPK model. Among the tested covariates, disease, body weight, gender, formulation, and dose were identified as statistically significant covariates on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics in healthy participants and participants with ITP. The PK parameter estimates and associated standard errors for the final PopPK model in participants with ITP are provided in Table 97. The goodness-of-fit plots for the final PopPK base model is presented in Figure 19. No important systematic deviations or major bias in any of the goodness of fit plots were observed, which demonstrated a reasonable model performance. The predictive performance of the model was acceptable based on bootstrap evaluation and visual predictive checks (Figure 20), indicating stability and predictive performance of the final PopPK model in participants with ITP. The final PopPK model was used to conduct the simulations for the PK exposures (e.g., AUC and Cmax) and to evaluate the impact of selected statistically significant covariates on these PK parameters. The impact of significant covariates was acceptable for the overall PK variability observed in participants with ITP, which indicated no clinically relevant effect of these covariates on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics in participants with ITP (Figure 21).

L1356: ## Summary
L1357: - Applicant's PopPK model appears to describe the rilzabrutinib serum concentration-time profiles in the observed range following the administration of rilzabrutinib in participants with ITP from all clinical studies adequately. Therefore, the PopPK model is acceptable for simulating post hoc exposure metrics, e.g., C_{max} and AUC of rilzabrutinib for E-R analyses of efficacy or safety measurements.
- Covariate analysis identified disease, body weight, gender, formulation and dose as statistically significant covariates on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics, which did not indicate clinically relevant effect of these covariates on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics in participants with ITP.
L1358: ## **14.5.2. Exposure-Response Analysis of Efficacy and Safety**
L1359: The Applicant conducted the E-R analysis of efficacy to characterize the relationship between rilzabrutinib exposures and efficacy and safety endpoints in participants with immune thrombocytopenia. The objective of Applicant's E-R analysis was to confirm the adequacy of the 400 mg BID regimen of rilzabrutinib for the treatment of ITP.
L1360: ## Method
L1361: Participants from studies LUNA 2 and LUNA 3 who had at least one platelet count observation and evaluable PK data, if on active treatment were included in the analyses dataset for efficacy and safety. Data manipulation, visualization, and simulations was conducted using R (version 4.1.3 or later). The longitudinal platelet counts were selected as the primary efficacy endpoint which was based on key aspects of the ITP disease etiology and the presumed mechanisms of action of rilzabrutinib. E-R analyses of efficacy was conducted based on the repeated-measures endpoints (longitudinal platelet counts) and the exposure metrics. In addition, the exploratory analyses were performed to characterize the E-R for three adverse events (AEs) (nausea, diarrhea, and anemia). Three exposure metrics, maximum concentration in the dosing interval at steady state (C_{max,ss}), minimum concentration in the dosing interval at steady state (C_{min,ss}), and area under the concentration-time curve at steady state (AUC_{ss}) were selected as the exposure metrics. Logistic regression model fitting was carried out in a Bayesian full-model framework to build the final model. Participants in Study PRN1008-018 were included in the dataset of time for first rescue therapy and dropout analysis.
L1362: ## Results
L1363: The analysis population consisted of 86 participants from Studies LUNA 2 and 219 participants from PRN1008-018, which included 10,652 weekly platelet counts with no missing data or data that were below limit of quantification. An indirect exposure-response model adequately characterized platelet counts, including the magnitude of persistent and sometimes large fluctuations in platelet counts. The model indicated that response to prior corticosteroid therapy and concomitant use of corticosteroids were associated with higher response rates. In addition, baseline platelet counts below the median were associated with lower response rates while
L1364: baseline counts above the median were associated with higher response rates. The reviewer explored the E-R relationships for both efficacy and safety between the PK exposures (e.g., AUC_{ss} or C_{min,ss}) and the primary endpoints using the logistics regression. Figure 3 showed that participants with higher rilzabrutinib exposure (AUC_{ss}) were predicted to have more platelet counts increase from the baseline. However, response rate as defined by the primary endpoint of LUNA 3, did not appear to depend on exposure over the range at 400 mg BID (i.e., flat exposure-response) (see Table 10 in Section 6.1.1.1). In terms of the safety analyses, participants with higher rilzabrutinib exposures (AUC_{ss} and C_{min,ss}) generally had higher rates of diarrhea and nausea (Figure 4 and Figure 5). The Applicant's analyses results were basically similar as reviewer's findings (Figure 22 and Figure 23). Anemia was not considered for further model development because events were rare overall, with less than 5% of participants experiencing an anemia event. The analysis of time to rescue therapy and dropout showed that rilzabrutinib exposure was not found to be predictive of time to first rescue therapy or time to dropout (Figure 24).

L1375: ## Summary
L1376: The results of the E-R analyses show a trend for greater increase in platelet count from baseline with higher rilzabrutinib exposure (AUC_{ss}). However, response rate as defined by the primary endpoint of LUNA 3, does not appear to depend on exposure over the range at 400 mg BID (i.e., flat exposure-response). In addition, participants with higher rilzabrutinib exposures (AUC_{ss} and C_{min,ss}) had relatively higher rates of diarrhea and nausea events.
L1377: ## 14.5.3. Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic Modeling
L1378: ## 14.5.3.1. Executive Summary
L1379: The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant's PBPK analyses to:
L1380: - evaluate the DDI potential of rilzabrutinib as a substrate of moderate and weak CYP3A inhibitors at the therapeutic dose (400 mg)
- evaluate the DDI potential of rilzabrutinib as a substrate of moderate and weak CYP3A inducers
- evaluate the DDI potential of rilzabrutinib as an inhibitor of CYP3A, P-gp, OATP1Bs and BCRP, and an inducer of CYP3A at the therapeutic dose (400 mg)
L1381: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK reports (PBS0017, PMB0130 and PBS020) and related model summary reports, responses to FDA Clinical Pharmacology PBPK information requests dated October 22, 2024 (seq0015) and February 10, 2025 (seq0023 and seq0025), and the modeling supporting files, and concluded that:
L1382: - The rilzabrutinib PBPK model could be used to predict the effects of inhibitors or inducers on rilzabrutinib exposure.The moderate CYP3A inhibitors fluconazole, erythromycin and verapamil were predicted to increase rilzabrutinib exposure approximately 3-fold following twice daily administration of 400 mg rilzabrutinib.
- The weak CYP3A inhibitor cimetidine was predicted to increase rilzabrutinib exposure approximately 2-fold following twice daily administration of 400 mg rilzabrutinib.
- The strong CYP3A inducers were predicted to reduce rilzabrutinib exposure by approximately 80%.
- The moderate CYP3A inducer efavirenz was predicted to reduce rilzabrutinib exposure by approximately 70%.
- The weak CYP3A inducer modafinil was predicted to reduce rilzabrutinib exposure by approximately 20%.
- Rilzabrutinib was predicted to increase midazolam C_{max} and AUC up to 2.6- and 3.5-fold in ITP participants following oral administration of rilzabrutinib 400 mg twice daily.
- The predicted effects of rilzabrutinib on the exposure of transporter substrates are considered inadequate because there are uncertainties about the in vitro to in vivo extrapolation of transporter inhibition parameters and potential effects of the major metabolite PRN834 on the transporter substrates were not considered. The potential interaction of rilzabrutinib with substrates of P-gp, OATP1B and/or BCRP cannot be ruled out.
- The renal impairment model was insufficiently verified for predicting the effects of renal impairment on rilzabrutinib exposure, especially in participants with severe renal impairment.
L1383: ## 14.5.3.2. Background
L1384: Rilzabrutinib (PRN1008/SAR444671) is a reversible, covalent, potent BTKI in development for the treatment of autoimmune and inflammatory diseases. The recommended dosing regimen of rilzabrutinib is 400 mg taken orally twice daily with or without food. The 400 mg tablet is the only dose strength available.
L1385: Following oral administration of single doses of rilzabrutinib (liquid formulation) in healthy participants, AUC_{inf} increased greater than dose proportionally up to 600 mg, but C_{max} appeared to increase proportionally to the dose up to 300 mg (PRN1008-001). Similar exposures were observed from 600 to 1200 mg, indicating absorption with this formulation was dose limited. Following oral administration of multiple doses of rilzabrutinib (liquid formulation), the mean plasma AUC of rilzabrutinib increased approximately dose proportionality from 300 to 600 mg (PRN1008-001). Steady state was achieved every 3rd and 4th dosing day each week with approximately 2-fold accumulation. Compared to the fasted state, coadministration of rilzabrutinib as a liquid formulation with a moderate fat meal reduced C_{max} and AUC_{inf} by approximately 40% to 51%, respectively (PRN1008-001). Steady state rilzabrutinib exposures are comparable between a typical healthy subject and ITP participants (Study POH1156).
L1386: Rilzabrutinib is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 with minor contribution from CYP2D6 in vitro. Following coadministration of a single dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib with two doses of 100 mg ritonavir 12h apart in healthy participants, rilzabrutinib AUC and C_{max} increased by 8- and 5-fold, respectively (PRN1008-014). The results from the human ADME study in healthy male participants (PRN1008-015), conducted with a single oral dose of 300 mg rilzabrutinib (liquid formulation), showed that approximately 92.9% of the dose administered orally was recovered, in which approximately 5% and 86% of the dose were recovered in the urine and the feces, respectively, and unchanged drug was not detected in the urine and feces. Rilzabrutinib and its metabolite PRN834 (M45) accounted for 0.76% and 1.09% of the total radioactivity exposure in plasma, respectively, and the AUC of PRN834 was approximately 130% of its parent AUC (PRN1008-015). The absolute bioavailability study, conducted with a single oral dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib (immediate release tablet) with an intravenous (IV) microdose of rilzabrutinib (100 µg), showed that rilzabrutinib had an oral bioavailability of 4.73%, and total plasma clearance of 58.1 L/h. Following oral administration of 300 mg rilzabrutinib, it had a renal clearance of 0.243 L/h (PRN1008-015).
L1387: Based on in vitro drug interaction studies, rilzabrutinib was not an inhibitor of most CYP enzymes except CYP3A with IC_{50} of 1.09µM (ICH0140). The rilzabrutinib metabolite, PRN834, was not identified as a reversible inhibitor of human CYPs but may be a time-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A. Its K_I and k_{inact} values were not determined due to activation effect at low concentration (ICH0140). A midazolam DDI study was conducted in healthy participants following a single dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib (PRN1008-011). Midazolam exposure was increased by 1.7-fold when midazolam was given simultaneously with rilzabrutinib immediately released tablet, and 2.2-fold when midazolam was given 2 hours after rilzabrutinib administration (PRN1008-011). Midazolam exposure increased 3.14-fold when midazolam was given 1 hour after a single dose of 600 mg rilzabrutinib (PRN1008-002, liquid formulation). In vitro, rilzabrutinib is a CYP3A inducer and PRN834 is an inducer of CYP3A and CYP2B6 (IHH0096). However, at 50 times of unbound C_{max}, rilzabrutinib and PRN834 induced CYP3A mRNA less than 2-fold.
L1388: Rilzabrutinib is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP (DVR0091 and TRS0024), but not a substrate of OATP1B1/1B3, OAT1, OCT1/OCT2, (DVR0158). Rilzabrutinib and PRN834 are inhibitors of various efflux, and efflux and uptake transporters listed in Table 98. In addition, rilzabrutinib is a P-gp inhibitor with an IC50 value of 9.53 µM when assessed using the Caco-2/TC7 model, or 2.17 µM when assessed using the transfected vesicle model (TRI0055). Refer to Section 14.1 for information on rilzabrutinib ADME properties, in vitro and clinical studies used in PBPK modeling.

L1392: ## 14.5.3.3. Methods
L1393: All simulations were performed using the PK/PD Profiles mode in the Simcyp® Simulator (Versions 20, Certara, Sheffield, UK). Simulations were performed using healthy subject population (sim-Healthy Volunteers) model. Schemes of the PBPK modeling and simulation strategy are shown in Figure 25, which summarizes the studies used for rilzabrutinib model development and verification, and model applications in predicting DDI of rilzabrutinib as a substrate of CYP3A4 induction or inhibition and as a precipitant of various transports and enzymes (not shown). The final model input parameters were summarized in Table 99. The rilzabrutinib PBPK model consists of an Advanced Dissolution, Absorption and Metabolism (ADAM) model for describing drug absorption in each gut segment, a full PBPK model (method 3) for distribution, and an enzyme kinetics model and renal clearance for elimination. The Simcyp library files SV-ritonavir, erythromycin, verapamil, fluconazole, cimetidine, rifampin (SV-Rifampicin-MD), efavirenz, rifabutin, phenytoin, carbamazepine, midazolam, digoxin, pravastatin and rosuvastatin were used without any modification unless otherwise noted.

L1401: ## 14.5.3.4. Results
L1402: ## 14.5.3.4.1. Can the PBPK Model Adequately Describe the PK Profiles of Rilzabrutinib?
L1403: Yes. Clinical PK data that had not been used in model development was used to verify the rilzabrutinib PBPK model. The model could reasonably describe the plasma concentration-time profiles of rilzabrutinib following single and multiple oral doses of rilzabrutinib in healthy participants (Figure 26 and Figure 27). The model-estimated AUC were mostly within 0.8- and 1.25-fold of the observed values, but the Cmax values were mostly underpredicted (Table 100 and Table 101). However, the simulated steady-state Cmax and AUC were 2-fold lower than that in ITP participants estimated from the PopPK analysis (Study POH1156, FDA-response-22Oct2024, Table 6).

L1417: ## 14.5.3.4.2. Can PBPK Analyses Be Used To Estimate the Effects of Rilzabrutinib on Substrates of CYP3A, OATP1B, and BCRP?
L1418: ## **Effects of Rilzabrutinib on Midazolam**
L1419: Rilzabrutinib was predicted to increase midazolam AUC and C_{max} by 3.5- and 2.4-fold, respectively, following administration of 400 mg rilzabrutinib twice daily in ITP participants. Details are discussed as follows:
L1420: - In vitro, rilzabrutinib is an inhibitor and inducer of CYP3A. At 50 times of unbound C_{max} (0.3 to 0.6\muM for rilzabrutinib and 0.66\muM for PRN834), rilzabrutinib and its metabolite PRN834 induced CYP3A mRNA by less than 2-fold, indicating that these compounds are not considered as clinical inducers according to the ICH M12 guidelines.
L1421: - Clinical DDI studies of rilzabrutinib with midazolam in healthy participants showed that, following a single dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib as tablet formulation (PRN1008-011), midazolam exposure was increased by 2.2-fold when midazolam was given 2 hours after rilzabrutinib administration (PRN1008-011). Midazolam exposure increased 3.14-fold when midazolam was given 1 hour after a single dose of 600 mg rilzabrutinib as liquid formulation (PRN1008-002). Simulation studies comparing predicted versus observed effects of rilzabrutinib on midazolam are presented in Table 102. The effect of 400 mg rilzabrutinib tablet on midazolam was reasonably predicted, while the effect of the 600 mg liquid formulation was underpredicted. For the latter, the exposure of rilzabrutinib (Table 103) and midazolam in the absence of rilzabrutinib (Table 102) were well predicted, indicating that the underprediction may be caused by the CYP3A inhibition parameter K_i of rilzabrutinib. Reducing the K_i value from (b)(4)\mu\text{M}, used by the Applicant, to 0.003\mu\text{M}, improved prediction accuracy for both 400 mg and 600 mg doses. Notably, the simulated exposure of rilzabrutinib following a single dose of 400 mg rilzabrutinib was (b)(4) higher than that observed in the midazolam study (Table 103), potentially explaining the reasonably prediction despite the suboptimal CYP3A K_i value.
- The effects of steady state rilzabrutinib on midazolam exposure, following administration of 400 mg rilzabrutinib twice daily in ITP participants, were simulated by both the Applicant and the reviewer. The results are shown in Table 104. The effects predicted by the Applicant are likely underpredicted for two reasons: (1) the predicted steady-state rilzabrutinib following administration of 400 mg BID was approximately (b)(4) lower than that estimated in ITP participants by PopPK (Table 105), and (2) the suboptimal CYP3A K_i value were used in Applicant's simulations. To achieve the steady-state exposure of rilzabrutinib following administration of rilzabrutinib 400 mg BID in ITP participants, the simulations need to be performed at 700 mg BID. At the rilzabrutinib exposure achieved in ITP participants, rilzabrutinib was predicted to increase midazolam AUC and C_{\text{max}} by 3.5- and 2.4-fold, respectively. These results are similar to that observed in the midazolam study with a single 600 mg rilzabrutinib (Table 104), in which study rilzabrutinib exposure was similar to that in ITP participants and that simulated at rilzabrutinib 700 mg BID by the reviewer (Table 105).
- In Section 12.3 of the final product labeling, a 3-fold (b)(4) increase in midazolam AUC in patients with immune thrombocytopenia following coadministration with 400 mg rilzabrutinib twice daily was reported. This occurred because, during the labeling negotiation, the Applicant simulated this interaction using a CYP3A K_i value of 0.006\mu\text{M} and Simcyp version 20.

L1441: ## Effects of Rilzabrutinib on OATP1B Substrate Pravastatin
L1442: Potential interaction between rilzabrutinib and the OATP1B1 substrate pravastatin cannot be ruled out. Details are discussed as follows:
L1443: - Drug interactions of pravastatin with OATP1B1/3 inhibitors were often not predicted using the pravastatin model in the Simcyp library even when the lowest in vitro transporter inhibition parameters of the inhibitors in the Certara DDI Database were applied in the simulations. The observed interactions with pravastatin could only be reproduced, within 0.8-1.25 of clinical observations, when the lowest experimentally generated Ki or IC50 values of OATP1B1/3 inhibitors were reduced approximately 10- to 100-fold (ISSX 2024). Rilzabrutinib predicted to increase pravastatin AUC and CMax by 1.4 and 1.8, respectively, when Ki values for both OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 were reduced by 100-fold (Table 106), when the rilzabrutinib exposure observed in ITP participants following administration of rilzabrutinib 400 mg BID was applied in the simulation. In addition to the uncertainty about the Ki value, it should be noted that this simulation didn't account for the potential inhibitory effects of the major circulating metabolite PRN834 on OATP1B1 and OATP1B3.
- PRN834 is the major metabolite of rilzabrutinib. Collectively, PRN834 and related metabolites account for approximately 26% of the total radioactive material recovered in the human ADME study and approximately 43% of total oxidative metabolites identified in the urine and feces (PRN1008-015-POP17292). PRN834 is one of the major circulating metabolites in human plasma. Following single and twice daily doses of 400 mg rilzabrutinib, the PRN834 to rilzabrutinib AUC ratio ranged from 1.6- to 2.1-fold in Japanese and Caucasian participants (PRN1008-023). Compared to rilzabrutinib, the in vitro IC50 value of PRN834 for OATP1B1 was approximately 2.7-fold lower, Therefore, potential inhibitory effects of PRN834 on transporters cannot be ruled out.

L1447: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; BID, twice daily; BCRP, breast cancer resistance protein; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; CYP, cytochrome P450; IC50, half maximal inhibitory concentration; Ki, inhibitory constant; OATP, organic anion transporting polypeptide
L1448: ## **Effects of Rilzabrutinib on OATP1B and BCRP Substrate Rosuvastatin**
L1449: Potential interaction between rilzabrutinib and the OATP1B/BCRP substrate rosuvastatin cannot be ruled out. Details are discussed as follows:
L1450: - The predicted effect of rilzabrutinib on rosuvastatin was likely inaccurate for two reasons: (1) the rosuvastatin model (V20) used to evaluate the effect of rilzabrutinib on both OATP1B and BCRP cannot capture the Cmax of rosuvastatin and tends to underpredict its AUC in participants with OATP1B1 or BCRP genotypes (Table 107), and (2) the reviewer's analyses showed that drug interactions of rosuvastatin with OATP1B1/3 and BCRP inhibitors were either not predicted or significantly underpredicted even when the lowest in vitro transporter inhibition parameters of the inhibitors in the Certara DDI Database were applied in the simulations.
- For risk assessment, the reviewer performed simulations using the rosuvastatin model in Simcyp V22, which could reasonably predict its AUC in OATP1B1 and BCRP genotypes. However, the in vitro to in vivo extrapolation of BCRP inhibition parameter (Ki or IC50) has not been established. Through the reviewer's independent analysis of OATP1B or/and BCRP inhibitors with clinical interaction data with both OATP1B1 endogenous biomarker CP-I and rosuvastatin, (b) (4) When rilzabrutinib and rosuvastatin were administered simultaneously, (b) (4) rilzabrutinib was predicted to increase rosuvastatin AUC and Cmax by approximately (b) (4), respectively (Table 106). Additionally, the lowest IC50 values of BCRP inhibitors in the DDI Database were mostly generated using the membrane vesicle system not the Caco-2 monolayer system used for rilzabrutinib. The BCRP IC50 generated using the Caco-2 monolayer system was on average 11-fold less potent, ranging from 0.3 to 53-fold lower, compared to the HEK293 membrane vesicle system. Furthermore,
L1451: the major oxidative metabolite PRN834 is 50% more potent BCRP inhibitor compared to rilzabrutinib. Given that rilzabrutinib is extensively metabolized, potentially in part to PRN834, by CYP3A at the intestine, the potential effect of PRN834 on BCRP may not be ignored. Taken together, significant interaction between rilzabrutinib and rosuvastatin cannot be ruled out.

L1455: ## **Effects of Rilzabrutinib on P-gp**
L1456: Significant interaction between rilzabrutinib and the P-gp substrate dabigatran etexilate cannot be ruled out. Details are discussed as follows:
L1457: - The Applicant predicted the effect of rilzabrutinib on P-gp using the digoxin PBPK model, which is not fully verified. For risk assessment, the reviewer performed simulations using the PBPK models of dabigatran etexilate and dabigatran in Simcyp V22. Dabigatran etexilate is a P-gp substrate. Dabigatran is a metabolite of dabigatran etexilate and is not a P-gp substrate. The effects of P-gp inhibitors on dabigatran etexilate are assessed by changes in dabigatran plasma exposure. The ability of the PBPK models of dabigatran etexilate and dabigatran to predict P-gp interaction was assessed using clinical DDIs of dabigatran with verapamil, clarithromycin, quinidine, and ketoconazole (Dabigatran Simcyp summary report). In addition, the model could describe the dabigatran pharmacokinetics following administration of a microdose of dabigatran etexilate (0.375 mg, Simcyp V22 Dabigatran Etexilate and Dabigatran summary report). Of note, the ketoconazole DDI data were also used in the model development.
- Large range of IC50 values of these inhibitors are reported in the Certara DDI Database. The difference in IC50 between minimum and maximum values of each inhibitor ranges from 10- to 7450-fold if all the results were considered, and 1- to 42-fold if only experiments conducted in the condition similar to that of rilzabrutinib (e.g., P-gp expressing HEK membrane vesicles and N-methyl-quinidine as a probe substrate, study report TRI0055) were considered. Compared with the Ki values used in the models of the aforementioned inhibitors, the maximum IC50 values generated using membrane vesicles were approximately 4 to 200-fold higher, and the minimum IC50 values generated using membrane vesicles were approximately 2- to 15-fold higher. Given the large variability in P-gp IC50, the predictability
L1458: of clinical DDI from in vitro data is of concern. For risk assessment, the reviewer conducted sensitivity analyses of Ki. Significant increase in dabigatran exposure was predicted when the Ki value was reduced only 10-fold (Table 106). Therefore, significant interaction between rilzabrutinib and the P-gp substrate dabigatran etexilate cannot be ruled out.
L1459: ## 14.5.3.4.3. Can PBPK Analyses Predict the Effects of CYP3A Precipitants on the Pharmacokinetics of Rilzabrutinib?
L1460: Yes, the rilzabrutinib PBPK model could be used to predict the effects of CYP3A inhibitors or inducers. The moderate CYP3A inhibitors were predicted to increase rilzabrutinib exposure approximately 3-fold, and the weak CYP3A inhibitor cimetidine was predicted to increase rilzabrutinib exposure by approximately 2-fold (Table 108). The effect of rifampin on rilzabrutinib exposure was overpredicted, thus there may be some uncertainties about the predicted effects of other CYP3A inducers on rilzabrutinib exposure. See detailed discussion in the comments below.

L1466: ## Summary
L1467: - To predict the effects of CYP3A inhibitors and inducers on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics, the fraction metabolized by CYP3A (f_{m,CYP3A}) is one of key parameters that need to be verified in the rilzabrutinib PBPK model. Based on the results from the study conducted using recombinant human CYP enzymes (DVR0108) and chemical inhibition study conducted in primary human hepatocytes (MIH0984), approximately 90% of rilzabrutinib metabolism was mediated by CYP3A4 with minor contribution from CYP2D6, approximately 8% on average. In the human ADME study (PRN1008-015-POP17292), although not all the radioactive components were identified, approximately 73% of the quantitated dose were identified as oxidative metabolites, indicating oxidative metabolism is the major elimination pathway of rilzabrutinib. The f_{m,CYP3A} value in the rilzabrutinib model was optimized to be 80% based on its ability to reproduce the inhibitory effects of ritonavir on rilzabrutinib (Table 108).
- Ritonavir is an inhibitor of CYP3A and P-gp. Rilzabrutinib is a substrate of CYP3A and P-gp. rilzabrutinib had moderate permeability (DVR0091). Therefore, P-gp may play a role in rilzabrutinib absorption, and the observed effect of ritonavir on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics could be due to inhibition of both CYP3A and P-gp. Coadministration of rilzabrutinib with a P-gp inhibitor quinidine increased rilzabrutinib AUC only 12.7% (PRN1008-024). In addition, the human ADME study showed that approximately 90% of rilzabrutinib was absorbed. These data indicate that P-gp may not play a significant role in rilzabrutinib disposition. Therefore, the observed effect of ritonavir on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics is most likely due to CYP3A inhibition, and this rilzabrutinib model is suitable to predict the effects of CYP3A inhibitors that are also inhibitors of P-gp such as erythromycin and verapamil.
- Rifampin is known to decrease the exposure of P-gp substrates by P-gp induction. Even though P-gp may play an insignificant role in rilzabrutinib absorption, increase in P-gp expression by rifampin could potentially increase hepatic biliary clearance of rilzabrutinib. The PBPK simulation overpredicted the induction effects of rifampin on rilzabrutinib without incorporating P-gp induction in the rifampin model. The reason for this overprediction is unknown. Therefore, there may be some uncertainties in using the rilzabrutinib model to predict the effects of an inducer on its exposure.
- The Applicant also predicted the effect of a moderate CYP3A inducer using the rifabutin PBPK model. However, the results are not included in Table 108 due to several limitations:
L1468: (b) (4)
L1469: (b) (4) Therefore, further verification is needed to establish the reliability of rifabutin's CYP3A induction capability.
L1470: - For the weak CYP3A inhibitor cimetidine, its PBPK model to predict inhibitory effects of cimetidine on CYP3A substrates was inadequately verified. As shown in (Table 109), the observed inhibitory effects of cimetidine on the exposure of CYP3A substrates were not predicted in &gt;50-60% of the studies, even though predicted ratios of C_{max} and AUC were within 0.8- 1.25-fold of the observed values. For those that cimetidine was predicted to have inhibitory effects (&gt;1.25 fold), its effects were generally underpredicted. The Applicant was requested to improve the prediction results by optimizing the CYP3A K_i parameter in the cimetidine model (FDA information request_10Jan2025). The Applicant reduced CYP3A K_i value (b) (4) to 11 \muM, which improved the predictions except for two studies (Table 109). This improved cimetidine model was used to predict the interaction of cimetidine with rilzabrutinib (Table 108).

L1475: - For the weak CYP3A inducer dexamethasone, its PBPK model has not been adequately verified to be used to simulate the effect of a weak CYP3A inducer on rilzabrutinib. The Applicant selected modafinil as a weak CYP3A inducer. The modafinil PBPK model could adequately simulated its effects on CYP3A substrates (Table 110).

L1480: ## 14.5.3.4.4. Can PBPK Analyses Predict the Effects of Renal Impairment on the Pharmacokinetics of Rilzabrutinib?
L1481: The renal impairment model may not be sufficiently verified for predicting the effects of renal impairment on rilzabrutinib exposure, especially in participants with severe renal impairment.
L1482: PBPK analyses were used to evaluate the effects of renal impairment on rilzabrutinib pharmacokinetics. The exposure of rilzabrutinib in participants with renal impairment were predicted to be no more than 1.5-fold higher compared to that in healthy participants (report PBS020). To verify the performance of renal impairment population models, the Applicant was requested to provide model verification documents that include the key parameters relating to the population models, the source of the values used, and, if any parameter optimization was done, list drugs and clinical studies used and describe the optimization process. In addition, the performance of each subpopulation needs to be demonstrated by comparing the observed and simulated pharmacokinetics of a set of drugs that have not been used in the parameter optimization to demonstrate the model performance. The drugs used in the model performance evaluation should have similar characteristics to rilzabrutinib that is predominantly (&gt;80% preferred) metabolized by CYP3A with minimal renal elimination. Table 111 summarizes the performance of the Applicant selected CYP3A substrates. Among the investigated drugs, metformin is mainly eliminated via renal excretion and the contribution of CYP3A in the model has not been verified. Nifedipine and sildenafil data were used to optimize the CYP3A abundance in these populations. In addition, renal impairment had limited impact on the exposure of most of the selected drugs. For drugs whose exposure increased more than 50%, the effects of renal impairment on their exposure tended to be underpredicted (Table 111). Therefore, the renal impairment model may not be sufficiently verified for predicting the effects of renal impairment on rilzabrutinib exposure, especially in participants with severe renal impairment.
